ABOUT THIS VOLUME

This is the first of five volumes.

It carries the front matter and Part One, the general account of the neuron, which apply to all five volumes and are not repeated in the others.

It then gives Chapters 1 to 59 of the catalogue.


HOW TO READ THIS BOOK

This book is written for three readers at once, and it does not ask any of them to compromise.

The first reader is a scientist who needs the numbers, the markers, the projections and the citations.

The second reader is a person with no biology background at all who wants to understand the most complicated object in the known universe, and who is entitled to have it explained properly rather than simplified into mush.

The third reader is not a person.

It is an Artificial Intelligence (AI) coding agent that will be handed this document as a vision document and a product requirements document, and asked to build a working simulation of the human nervous system.

Writing for all three turned out to be easier than expected, because all three want the same thing.

They want the truth, stated exactly, in words that do not hide.

Every sentence in this book is written so that a beginner can understand it.

Every technical term is defined the first time it is used.

Every acronym is spelled out in full before it is abbreviated.

Nothing is dumbed down, because the science is more interesting than any simplification of it.


WHAT IS IN THIS BOOK

Part One describes the neuron in general: every part, every process, and every capability that all neurons share.

It ends by answering a question that turns out to be the most important question in the book, and one that most textbooks never ask directly.

Parts Two onwards work through every named type of neuron in the human nervous system, one at a time, three hundred and two of them.

For each type, twenty aspects are given, in the same order every time, so that the entries can be compared, searched, and machine-read.

The catalogue follows the human body from the surface of the brain down to the wall of the gut.

The final parts give the counts, the glossary, and the specification notes.


THE TAGS AND FLAGS

Two markings appear throughout, and both are explained here before they are used.


The Previously Unnamed Construct tag, written (PUC)

Some of the neuron types in this book are real, are functionally necessary, and are relied upon every day in the scientific literature, and yet have never been given an accepted proper name.

They are known instead by a label.

They are known by a gene symbol such as Sst Chodl, or by a cluster number from a database, or by an address such as layer 6b, or by a phrase that changes its wording from one paper to the next.

A label is a key that gets the right row out of a spreadsheet.

A name is a teaching instrument.

Eleven such cells appear in this book, each tagged (PUC), meaning Previously Unnamed Construct, and each given a plain English name for the first time.

Naming them is not a claim of discovery.

Every one of those cells was already there, already published, already proved by experiment.

What was missing was a proper name, and a science cannot reason efficiently about a thing it cannot name properly.


The cognition flags, written (COG) and (NON-COG)

Every type in this book carries one of two flags.

(COG) means the cell is involved in cognition.

(NON-COG) means it is not.

Cognition here means the acquisition, representation, transformation, storage and use of information: perception, attention, memory, language, reasoning, decision-making, and the regulation of those processes; which are especially interesting to Artificial Intelligence endeavors.

A cell is flagged (COG) if it represents, transforms, stores, selects or routes information used for perception, memory, decision or action selection, or if it sets the global brain state within which such processing happens.

A cell is flagged (NON-COG) if its documented role is pure sensory transduction at the body surface, pure motor execution at the muscle, autonomic or endocrine or homeostatic regulation of the body's internal state, or developmental scaffolding.

Of the three hundred and two types in this book, one hundred and seventy-five are flagged (COG) and one hundred and twenty-seven are flagged (NON-COG).


PART ONE: THE NEURON IN GENERAL

1.1 The Cell That Thinks

There is a cell in your head right now that is reading this sentence.

Not alone, and not in the way a person reads, but the claim is literally true.

Somewhere in the back of your skull, a cell is receiving a pattern of electrical signals that corresponds to the shapes on this page, and it is deciding whether that pattern is worth passing on.

There are roughly eighty-six billion such cells in an adult human brain.

They are called neurons, or nerve cells, and they are the reason you exist as a point of view rather than as an object.

A neuron is, at bottom, an ordinary animal cell.

It has a membrane, a nucleus, mitochondria to make energy, ribosomes to build proteins, and the same basic housekeeping machinery as a cell in your liver.

What makes it a neuron is that it has specialised, to an extreme degree, in one thing: handling information.

It has grown a branching antenna to collect signals.

It has grown a cable to send them.

It has covered its surface in molecular switches that open and close in response to voltage and to chemicals.

And it has arranged all of this so that it can perform a computation and pass on the answer.

A liver cell lives a settled life.

A neuron lives a life of continuous conversation.


1.2 The Universal Parts

Every neuron, of every type, in every animal that has them, is built from the same set of parts.

The types described in this book differ in the proportions, the numbers, the chemistry and the wiring of these parts, but never in the inventory.

• The soma, also called the cell body or perikaryon.

This is the bulbous central part that contains the nucleus.

The nucleus holds the deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA), the cell's complete instruction set.

The soma is the factory floor: it makes most of the cell's proteins, and it keeps the whole structure alive.

Soma diameters in the human nervous system run from about four micrometres, in a cerebellar granule cell, to about one hundred micrometres, in a large sensory neuron of the dorsal root ganglion.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so even the largest of these is far too small to see without a microscope.

• The dendrites.

These are the branching, tree-like extensions that receive incoming signals.

The word comes from the Greek for tree, and the resemblance is exact.

A single human cortical pyramidal neuron may carry a dendritic tree with a total length of several centimetres, folded into a space smaller than a grain of salt.

The surface of that tree is where nearly all incoming information arrives.

• The dendritic spines.

On many but not all neurons, the dendrites are studded with tiny knobs, each about one micrometre long, called spines.

Each spine is the receiving half of one synapse.

Spines are not fixed furniture.

They grow, shrink, appear and vanish over minutes to hours, and that motion is one of the physical forms that learning takes.

A single human cortical pyramidal neuron may carry between ten thousand and thirty thousand of them.

• The axon hillock and the axon initial segment.

The hillock is the small cone where the axon leaves the soma, and the initial segment is the short stretch of axon just beyond it.

This is the decision point.

It carries the highest density of voltage-gated sodium channels anywhere on the cell, which makes it the place where the electrical trigger is pulled.

Everything the dendrites collect is summed here, and here the cell commits.

• The axon.

This is the single output fibre that carries the outgoing signal away.

Axon lengths span six orders of magnitude in one body.

The axon of a cerebellar granule cell is a fraction of a millimetre long.

The axon of a motor neuron running from the base of your spine to the muscle of your big toe is about a metre long, and in a tall person somewhat more.

An axon is a thin tube, typically between one fifth of a micrometre and twenty micrometres across, and it may branch into thousands of endings.

• The myelin sheath and the nodes of Ranvier.

Some axons, though by no means all, are wrapped in a fatty insulating spiral called myelin.

Myelin is not made by the neuron.

It is made by other cells, oligodendrocytes inside the brain and spinal cord, and Schwann cells outside them.

The wrapping is interrupted every millimetre or so by a bare gap called a node of Ranvier.

The electrical signal jumps from node to node rather than crawling along the whole membrane, which is why a myelinated axon can conduct at up to one hundred and twenty metres per second while an unmyelinated one manages half a metre per second.

That is the difference between pulling your hand off a hot stove and thinking about it afterwards.

• The axon terminals, also called boutons.

These are the branched endings of the axon, and each one is a transmitting station.

A terminal is packed with small membrane bubbles called synaptic vesicles, each holding a few thousand molecules of a chemical messenger.

• The synapse.

This is the junction at which one neuron passes its signal to another cell.

The great majority of synapses are chemical, meaning a molecule is released across a gap of about twenty nanometres, which is roughly one fiftieth of the width of the gap itself compared to a human hair.

A minority are electrical, meaning the two cells are directly connected by protein pores called gap junctions and current flows straight from one into the other.

A typical human cortical neuron makes and receives on the order of seven thousand synapses.

Across the whole brain the number of synapses is on the order of one hundred trillion to one quadrillion.


1.3 The Universal Machinery

Underneath the visible architecture, every neuron runs the same set of molecular machines.

• The membrane and the resting potential.

Every neuron holds an electrical voltage across its outer membrane, with the inside negative relative to the outside.

At rest this is usually between minus sixty and minus eighty millivolts.

A millivolt is a thousandth of a volt, so this is a tiny voltage across an extremely thin film, which makes the electric field inside the membrane enormous, on the order of one hundred thousand volts per centimetre.

The neuron spends a large part of its energy budget simply holding this voltage steady, ready to be spent.

• The ion channels.

These are protein tunnels through the membrane that let specific charged particles through.

Sodium, potassium, calcium and chloride are the four that matter most.

Some channels open in response to voltage, some in response to a chemical binding, some in response to stretch, heat, cold or acid.

The particular set of channels a neuron builds is one of the main things that makes it the type of neuron it is, because that set determines how it fires.

• The pumps.

The sodium-potassium pump throws three sodium ions out and pulls two potassium ions in, over and over, using one molecule of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) each time.

Adenosine triphosphate is the cell's universal energy currency.

This one pump consumes a large share of the brain's entire energy budget, and it is the reason the brain, at about two percent of body weight, burns about twenty percent of the body's resting energy.

• The receptors.

These are proteins that recognise a chemical messenger and respond.

Some are themselves channels and act within a thousandth of a second.

Others are slower, working through internal chemical relays, and act over seconds to minutes, changing the cell's behaviour rather than its immediate voltage.

• The vesicles and the release machinery.

Chemical messengers are packaged into vesicles and released when calcium floods into the terminal.

The release machinery is fast, precise and probabilistic: a given vesicle may or may not fuse on a given spike, and that uncertainty is itself part of the computation.

• The cytoskeleton and axonal transport.

A neuron with a metre-long axon has a logistics problem no other cell faces.

Proteins made in the soma must be delivered to terminals a metre away, and worn-out components must be hauled back.

Motor proteins called kinesin and dynein walk cargo along microtubule tracks in opposite directions, at speeds up to about four hundred millimetres per day.

A neuron is, among other things, a freight company.

• The mitochondria.

These are the cell's power plants, and neurons position them deliberately at the places that need the most energy: the terminals, the nodes of Ranvier, and the initial segment.

A neuron actively hauls its power supply to wherever the work is.


1.4 What a Neuron Does: The Standard Answer

The standard account of what a neuron does has four steps, and it is worth stating precisely before it is complicated.

First, the neuron receives.

Chemical messengers released by other cells bind to receptors on its dendrites and soma, opening channels and producing small local voltage changes called graded potentials.

A graded potential that pushes the cell towards firing is called excitatory, and one that pushes it away is called inhibitory.

These are small, they fade as they spread, and no single one is usually enough to do anything.

Second, the neuron integrates.

The thousands of small pushes and pulls arriving across the dendritic tree spread towards the cell body, adding and cancelling as they go.

Integration is done in space, meaning inputs arriving at different places combine, and in time, meaning inputs arriving at different moments combine if they are close enough together.

Third, the neuron decides.

At the axon initial segment, the summed voltage is compared against a threshold, typically around minus fifty-five millivolts.

If the sum reaches the threshold, the cell fires.

If it does not, nothing happens.

Fourth, the neuron transmits.

An action potential, also called a spike, is launched.

It is all-or-nothing, meaning it is always the same size regardless of how far above threshold the input was, and it travels the full length of the axon without weakening.

At the terminals it triggers calcium entry, which triggers vesicle fusion, which releases the messenger onto the next cell, and the cycle begins again there.

That is the standard answer, and every word of it is true.

It is also radically incomplete.


1.5 What Else a Neuron Does

If a neuron only integrated its inputs and fired occasionally, it would be a component.

It is not a component.

It is closer to a small, self-modifying, self-provisioning organisation that happens to also do signal processing.

What follows is the list of everything else it is doing while it does the thing the textbooks describe.

This section is the answer to the question that this book was written around, and it is the single most important section in Part One.

• It computes inside its dendrites, before the summing even happens.

Dendrites are not passive wires.

They carry their own voltage-gated channels, and a cluster of inputs arriving close together on one branch can trigger a local dendritic spike that the rest of the cell sees as a single large event.

This means each dendritic branch behaves as a small processing unit in its own right.

The consequence is startling: a single human cortical pyramidal neuron has been shown, computationally, to be equivalent to an artificial neural network several layers deep.

The neuron is not a node in a network.

The neuron is a network.

• It sends its own spike backwards.

When a neuron fires, the spike does not only travel down the axon.

A copy travels back up into the dendritic tree.

This back-propagating action potential is a broadcast message to every synapse on the cell, saying "I just fired".

Synapses that were active just before that message arrives get strengthened, and synapses that were not do not.

That is the physical basis of one of the best-established learning rules in biology, spike-timing-dependent plasticity, in which the order of events within a few thousandths of a second determines whether a connection grows or shrinks.

• It rewrites its own inputs.

A neuron continuously adjusts the strength of the synapses it receives, strengthening some and weakening others, over timescales from seconds to a lifetime.

This is called synaptic plasticity, and it is the leading candidate for the physical form of memory.

The neuron is not a fixed circuit element.

It is a circuit element that edits its own wiring diagram.

• It keeps its own firing rate in range.

If a neuron is over-stimulated for hours, it turns all of its incoming synapses down together, and if it is under-stimulated it turns them all up together.

This is called homeostatic synaptic scaling, and it is how a network that is constantly strengthening some connections avoids running away into a seizure.

The neuron is its own thermostat.

• It changes its own excitability.

Separately from its synapses, a neuron alters the number and distribution of its own ion channels, changing how easily it fires at all.

This is called intrinsic plasticity, and it means the neuron can change its own personality, not merely its connections.

It can even move its own trigger zone: the axon initial segment physically shifts along the axon in response to activity, tuning the cell's threshold over hours to days.

• It builds proteins locally, far from the nucleus.

Instructions in the form of messenger ribonucleic acid (mRNA) are shipped out into the dendrites and held there, dormant, next to individual synapses.

When that synapse is used, the local instructions are translated into protein on the spot.

This means a single synapse can be strengthened without waiting for a signal to travel to the nucleus and back, and without strengthening every other synapse on the cell.

The neuron has distributed manufacturing.

• It changes which of its genes are switched on.

Strong or repeated activity sends calcium signals to the nucleus, which switch on a set of genes called immediate early genes, with names such as Fos and Arc.

Those genes launch programmes that consolidate short-lived changes into permanent ones.

An experience, in other words, reaches down and alters which parts of the cell's own genome are being read.

• It rebuilds its own architecture.

Spines grow and retract over minutes.

Axonal branches extend and are pruned over days.

In some regions whole new synapses form during sleep and are removed the following day.

The physical shape of the cell is a slow-moving record of what has happened to it.

• It releases messengers from places other than its axon terminals.

Several neuron types release their chemical messenger from their dendrites, sending a signal backwards into the region that is talking to them.

Others release from the soma itself.

The one-way picture, in which information enters by dendrites and leaves by the axon, is a useful simplification rather than a law.

• It broadcasts without a partner.

Much chemical signalling in the brain is not delivered across a narrow gap to one named recipient.

It is released into the fluid between cells and diffuses to whatever receptors it reaches, over distances of micrometres and timescales of seconds.

This is called volume transmission, and it is how most of the brain's mood, arousal and attention chemistry works.

A neuron doing this is not making a phone call.

It is changing the weather.

• It talks backwards across the synapse.

The receiving cell can send messages back to the sending cell, using molecules that travel in reverse across the gap.

Endocannabinoids, the body's own versions of the active compounds in cannabis, are made on demand by the receiving cell and drift back to tell the sending cell to quieten down.

Nitric oxide, a gas, does something similar and can pass straight through membranes.

The synapse is a two-way conversation.

• It is wired directly to some of its neighbours.

Gap junctions connect the interiors of two cells so that current, and small molecules, pass directly between them.

Populations coupled this way behave partly as a single electrical sheet, which is how certain groups of neurons achieve near-perfect synchrony.

• It influences its neighbours by field alone.

The electrical activity of a population creates a field in the surrounding tissue, and that field slightly biases the excitability of nearby cells that are not connected to it at all.

This is called ephaptic coupling.

Neurons overhear each other.

• It orders its own blood supply.

When a patch of brain becomes active, blood flow to that patch increases within a second or two.

Neurons participate directly in commanding that increase, releasing signals that dilate nearby vessels.

This is called neurovascular coupling, and it is the physical basis of functional brain imaging.

Every brain scan ever published is a picture of neurons ordering lunch.

• It tells other cells to insulate it.

Electrical activity in an axon signals to nearby oligodendrocytes to wrap that axon in myelin.

Because myelin sets conduction speed, and conduction speed sets arrival time, this means a neuron participates in tuning the timing of its own output.

Learning a skill measurably changes myelination in the relevant tracts.

• It manages a relationship with the immune system.

Neurons display molecules that tell microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, whether to leave them alone or to prune their synapses.

A neuron that goes quiet is at greater risk of having its connections eaten.

Use it or lose it is not a metaphor here; it is a signalling pathway.

• It runs a metabolic partnership.

Astrocytes take up glucose from the blood, convert it, and hand lactate to neurons as fuel during heavy activity.

Neurons and astrocytes also share the recycling of glutamate, the main excitatory messenger, in a loop that keeps the messenger from accumulating to toxic levels.

No neuron feeds itself alone.

• It maintains itself for a lifetime.

Most neurons in your brain are as old as you are and will never be replaced.

They must therefore run continuous quality control: clearing damaged proteins, replacing worn-out mitochondria, repairing DNA, and disposing of waste.

Much of that clearance happens during sleep, when the spaces between brain cells widen and fluid flushes through.

A neuron is a machine that must run for eighty years without being switched off.

• Some of them are sensors in their own right.

Certain neurons directly detect carbon dioxide, acidity, glucose, sodium concentration, temperature, stretch, or the presence of a hormone in the blood.

For these cells the input is not another neuron.

The input is the state of the body.

• Some of them are clocks.

Certain neurons fire rhythmically with no input at all, generating their own beat from their own channel machinery.

The cells that drive your breathing do this, and so do the cells that keep your twenty-four hour rhythm.

If every input to these cells were removed, they would keep going.

• Some of them are glands.

Certain neurons release their product not onto another cell but into the bloodstream, where it travels the whole body as a hormone.

At that point the distinction between the nervous system and the endocrine system stops being meaningful.

So the honest answer to the question is this.

A neuron does take inputs from its dendrites, calculate whether to fire, and send a signal down its axon.

It also computes locally in its branches, teaches its own synapses, rewrites its own connection strengths, regulates its own excitability, manufactures proteins on site, edits its own gene expression, rebuilds its own shape, signals backwards and sideways and by diffusion and by field, orders its own blood, commissions its own insulation, negotiates with the immune system, shares a metabolism with its neighbours, maintains itself for decades, and in some cases senses the body directly, keeps time, or secretes hormones into the blood.

The spike is the part we can measure most easily.

It is not the part that matters most.


1.6 What Makes One Type Differ From Another

Given that all neurons share the parts listed above, the question of what makes a type is the question of which knobs are turned, and how far.

Nine independent settings do the work, and every type in this book can be located on all nine at once.

• Shape: how many processes leave the cell body, and how they branch.

• Direction of information flow: toward the central nervous system, away from it, or between other neurons.

• Effect on the target: excite it, inhibit it, or modulate it.

• Chemical messenger: which molecule, or which combination, it releases.

• Reach: whether the axon stays local or travels to a distant structure.

• Firing pattern: the rhythm and style with which it produces spikes.

• Location: which region, layer, nucleus or ganglion it occupies.

• Developmental origin: which patch of embryonic tissue it was born from.

• Gene expression: which set of genes it has switched on, which is the newest scheme and now the dominant one in research.

A single cell has an answer to all nine questions simultaneously, and none of the nine answers is more correct than the others.

This is why there is no single agreed number of neuron types, and why any catalogue, including this one, is a considered position rather than a measurement.


1.7 How the Entries Are Organised

Every type in Parts Two onwards is given the same twenty aspects, in the same order.

The order runs from what you could see under a microscope, through what you could measure with an electrode, to what an engineer would need in order to build it.

• What it is and what it does.

• Structure and morphology.

• Size.

• Structural subtypes.

• Where it lives.

• Number.

• Electrical properties.

• Synaptic connections.

• Axon characteristics.

• Neurotransmitters.

• Myelination.

• Metabolic demands.

• Plasticity.

• Support system, meaning the specific glial cells around it and what they do differently.

• How it colours its region, meaning what flavour the region would lose if this one cell type were removed.

• Beyond integrate and fire, meaning everything from Section 1.5 that applies especially to this type.

• Systems interface, written as an engineering specification with inputs, outputs, state, parameters, time constants and failure mode.

• English Readable Code, giving the type's characteristic computation as pseudocode.

• Code sample, giving the same computation as real, runnable code.

• What makes it unmistakable.

Where an aspect genuinely does not apply to a given type, it is marked Not Applicable, and the reason is always given, so that a reader never mistakes an aspect that does not apply for one that was merely forgotten.


1.8 A Note on English Readable Code

The pseudocode in this book is written in English Readable Code (ERC), a formal notation that expresses algorithmic logic as structured English sentences which can be mechanically translated into any programming language.

It sits between informal pseudocode, which cannot be translated by machine, and a full programming language, which is harder for a newcomer to read.

Every English Readable Code procedure in this book opens with a line stating its purpose and closes with the line "The procedure is complete."

Each instruction is a complete English sentence on its own line.

The bodies of conditionals, loops and functions are indented by four spaces.

A reader who has never programmed can read these procedures as English and understand exactly what the cell does.

A coding agent can translate them into working software without guessing.


1.9 The Neuron Budget, and the Hundred Billion Question

Almost every popular account of the brain says that it contains one hundred billion neurons.

That figure has no clear source.

It appears to have entered circulation as a round-number estimate in the mid-twentieth century and to have been repeated ever since because it is memorable.

When the counting was finally done properly, by dissolving whole brains into a uniform suspension and counting nuclei, the answer came out lower.

Azevedo and colleagues, in 2009, reported about eighty-six billion neurons in the adult male human brain, with about sixteen point three billion in the cerebral cortex and about sixty-nine billion in the cerebellum.

A 2025 paper in the journal Brain has argued that even this figure is quoted with far more precision than the underlying evidence supports, since it rests on a small number of donated brains.

This book therefore uses a budget that sums to roughly eighty-seven billion neurons for the entire nervous system, brain and body together, and states the uncertainty honestly wherever a number is given.

The regional budget is as follows, and every per-type count in Parts Two onwards is allocated inside it.

• Cerebellum: about 69.0 billion, which is about eighty percent of all the neurons in the brain packed into about ten percent of its mass.

• Cerebral cortex: about 16.3 billion.

• Enteric nervous system, the nerve net in the wall of the gut: about 400 million.

• Retina, both eyes together: about 260 million.

• Spinal cord: about 200 million.

• Brainstem: about 200 million.

• Basal ganglia: about 200 million.

• Hippocampal formation: about 150 million.

• Thalamus and diencephalon: about 60 million.

• Amygdala, olfactory cortex and claustrum: about 50 million.

• Olfactory system, including the sensory sheet in the nose: about 22 million.

• Autonomic ganglia: about 20 million.

• Hypothalamus: about 10 million.

• Dorsal root ganglia: about 10 million.

• Cranial sensory ganglia: about 3 million.

• Auditory and vestibular ganglia: about 110 thousand.

Two facts jump out of that list, and both are worth pausing on.

The first is that the cerebellum, a structure most people have never heard of, contains four out of every five neurons you own.

The second is that the hypothalamus, which controls hunger, thirst, temperature, sleep, stress, growth and reproduction, does all of it with about ten million cells, which is roughly one neuron in every nine thousand.

Influence in the nervous system is not proportional to headcount.


PART TWO: CEREBRAL CORTEX - THE OUTER SHEET OF THE BRAIN

CHAPTER 1. L2/3 IT (Layer 2/3 Intra-Telencephalic) (Context-Weaving Neuron) (COG)

Press a finger to your forehead and you are one skull's thickness from the busiest gossip network in biology, a sheet of cells whose only job is to talk to other parts of the same two hemispheres.

Context names what it adds: a signal from the senses means nothing alone, and this cell supplies everything else the cortex knows at that instant.

Weaving names how - sideways, across columns and areas, pulling many threads into one cloth. It also reflects the fact that this is the most plastic cell in the cortex, because weaving must be learned.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L2/3 IT neuron is an excitatory pyramidal cell of the upper cerebral cortex, the folded sheet on the outside of the brain, and IT means intratelencephalic: its axon reaches nearby cortex, distant cortex, the opposite hemisphere and the striatum, but never leaves the two cerebral hemispheres.

That restriction makes it the cortex's associative layer, where a signal from one patch is compared with signals from many others.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A classic pyramid: triangular cell body, thick apical dendrite rising into a tuft in layer 1, and a skirt of basal dendrites, all studded with dendritic spines, the tiny knobs that each hold one excitatory synapse.

Human cells of this type carry roughly three times the dendritic length of matching macaque and mouse cells in temporal cortex.

SIZE:

The cell body is 12 to 25 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, the apical tree measures about 7,920 micrometres of wire plus or minus 538, and spine estimates run to 25,000 to 30,000 per cell, about 12,700 of them basal.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Patch-seq, which pairs electrical recording with single-cell gene reading, found five human L2/3 IT types named for marker genes, LTK, GLP2R, FREM3, CARM1P1 and COL22A1, of which only three map onto mouse types.

Treat this as a depth-dependent family, not one uniform cell.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layers 2 and 3 of every neocortical area of both hemispheres, meaning all frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital, cingulate and insular neocortex.

Its tuft sits at an explicit interface, layer 1, the almost cell-free top layer where long-range feedback and neuromodulator fibres land, and its axon crosses a second interface, the grey to white matter boundary, on entering the corpus callosum.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 4.6 billion in both hemispheres, range 4.0 to 5.0 billion, moderate confidence, from the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons of which roughly 13.1 billion are excitatory and roughly 35 percent are L2/3 IT.

That is about 5 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the brain, and slightly less of the whole nervous system once spinal cord and peripheral ganglia are counted.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting minus 70 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, input resistance 40 to 120 megohms, time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds, firing regular and adapting, with an unusually fast action potential onset whose speed correlates with measured intelligence quotient in surgical donors.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from layer 4 below, from other L2/3 cells, from the opposite hemisphere, from layer 1 feedback and from local interneurons, while outputs go to layer 5, to other L2/3 cells, across the callosum and to the striatum.

Excitation lands on spines and inhibition on the shaft, soma and axon initial segment, placing the brake where it can veto output.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin local collaterals arch back into layers 2 and 3 at roughly 0.3 to 1 metre per second while myelinated callosal branches manage 3 to 10 metres per second, and the corpus callosum, holding more than 200 million axons, draws most of them from cells like this.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the brain's standard excitatory messenger, packaged by the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT1 encoded by SLC17A7, acting on fast AMPA and slower voltage-dependent NMDA receptors, with heavy modulation by acetylcholine, serotonin, noradrenaline and dopamine.

MYELINATION:

Myelin is the fatty insulating wrap made by oligodendrocytes that speeds conduction, and here it is famously patchy, with intermittent segments and long bare stretches along the proximal axon, better coverage on callosal branches and none on the thinnest collaterals.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

At a few hertz this class burns on the order of three billion molecules of adenosine triphosphate, the cell's energy currency, per second, split roughly 47 percent on action potentials, 34 percent on the postsynaptic effects of glutamate, 13 percent on resting voltage and 3 percent on recycling glutamate.

With tens of thousands of synapses the bill is dominated by synapses, not spikes.

PLASTICITY:

The most plastic excitatory cell in the cortex, showing long-term potentiation and depression set by pre- and postsynaptic timing within tens of milliseconds, spine turnover over hours to days, and homeostatic scaling of all its weights to hold its average rate near a target.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The human protoplasmic astrocyte here is far larger and more branched than the rodent version, one cell enwrapping on the order of a million synapses and clearing glutamate through the transporters EAAT1 and EAAT2.

Superficial cortex also holds the human-enriched interlaminar astrocyte, whose processes run from layer 1 down through layers 2 and 3, and microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, prune weak spines here using complement proteins.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one type and the cortex loses its associations: sensation would still arrive at layer 4 and commands would still leave from layer 5, but nothing would bind the hemispheres together or carry information sideways between areas.

The upper cortex would go from a conversation to a row of separate telephones.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The apical dendrites of human L2/3 IT cells generate graded calcium-mediated dendritic action potentials seen in no other species in this form: the response is largest just above threshold, near 240 picoamperes, and is damped by weaker drive near 200 picoamperes and stronger drive near 450 picoamperes.

That inverted tuning lets one dendrite compute exclusive or, firing for either input alone but not both, a classification textbooks said required a multi-layer network.

The cell also back-propagates spikes to tag active synapses, makes protein locally in its dendrites, releases retrograde endocannabinoid messengers that quieten presynaptic partners, and helps route blood to active cortex.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Basal excitation from layer 4 and neighbours 0 to 20 nanosiemens; apical tuft excitation from layer 1 feedback 0 to 15 nanosiemens; inhibition 0 to 30 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spikes to local L2/3, layer 5, contralateral cortex and striatum, never below the telencephalon.

• STATE.

◦ Somatic voltage, apical tuft voltage, dendritic calcium spike amplitude scaled 0 to 1, adaptation current, per-synapse weights.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 70, threshold minus 40, reset minus 60 millivolts; input resistance 40 to 120 megohms; dendritic spike window 0.20 to 0.45 nanoamperes peaking at 0.24; synapse count 8,000 to 30,000.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; AMPA decay 2 milliseconds; NMDA decay 100 milliseconds; adaptation 100 to 300 milliseconds; spine remodelling hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In schizophrenia, spine density on deep layer 3 pyramidal cells of dorsolateral prefrontal area 46 falls about 23 percent, concentrated in medium and large spines.

◦ Runaway recurrent excitation among these cells, when inhibition fails, is a substrate for focal seizures.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a layer two or three intratelencephalic neuron fires, using a graded dendritic calcium spike that computes exclusive or.
Create a number variable called low threshold with initial value 0.20.
Create a number variable called peak drive with initial value 0.24.
Create a number variable called high cutoff with initial value 0.45.
Create a number variable called total drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dendritic amplitude with initial value 0.
Set total drive to the sum of the basal current and the apical current in nanoamperes.
If total drive is less than or equal to low threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set dendritic amplitude to 0.
Else if total drive is less than or equal to peak drive is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the difference of total drive and low threshold.
    Set dendritic amplitude to the result.
Else if total drive is less than high cutoff is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the difference of high cutoff and total drive.
    Set dendritic amplitude to the result.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set dendritic amplitude to 0.
End the conditional block.
Check if dendritic amplitude is greater than 0.02.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the point is the shape of one curve and the truth table it produces, and Python shows both with no ceremony.

# Graded dendritic calcium action potential in a human L2/3 IT neuron.
# Amplitude peaks just above threshold and is damped by stronger drive
# (Gidon et al., Science 2020). That inverted tuning yields exclusive or.
LOW, PEAK, HIGH = 0.20, 0.24, 0.45   # nanoamperes
UNIT = 0.24                          # nanoamperes from one active pathway

def dendritic_spike(drive):
    if drive <= LOW or drive >= HIGH:
        return 0.0                               # silent outside the window
    if drive <= PEAK:
        return (drive - LOW) / (PEAK - LOW)      # rising flank
    return (HIGH - drive) / (HIGH - PEAK)        # damped by strong drive

print("a  b   drive(nA)  dCaAP  output")
for a in (0, 1):
    for b in (0, 1):
        amp = dendritic_spike(UNIT * (a + b))
        print(f"{a}  {b}   {UNIT * (a + b):8.2f}  {amp:5.2f}  {1 if amp > 0.5 else 0}")
print("plain sums:", [a + b for a in (0, 1) for b in (0, 1)])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A pyramidal cell in the upper third of the cortex whose axon never leaves the hemispheres, carrying three times the dendrite of its monkey and mouse counterparts, and whose apical dendrite can answer exclusive or on its own.

CHAPTER 2. L4 IT (Layer 4 Intra-Telencephalic) (Arrival-Amplifying Neuron) (COG)

Everything you have ever seen, heard or felt entered your conscious brain through a thin band of small round cells about two millimetres beneath your scalp.

Arrival names the moment it owns: the instant information from outside first touches the cortex.

Amplifying corrects the usual misconception - thalamic terminals supply only ten to twenty-three percent of its excitatory synapses, so the strength is built locally by these cells exciting one another, not handed to them by the thalamus.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L4 IT neuron is the cortex's front door, an excitatory cell in layer 4, the granular layer, so called because it looks like fine grains rather than obvious pyramids.

IT again means intratelencephalic, so the axon stays inside the hemispheres and usually inside its own cortical column, a vertical cylinder 300 to 600 micrometres wide, and its job is to take the driving input from the thalamus, the relay station at the centre of the brain, amplify it and hand a clean copy up to layer 2/3.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The spiny stellate cell has no apical dendrite at all, its dendrites radiating in a rough sphere like a sea urchin, making it the purest local integrator in the cortex, while the star pyramid keeps a short apical dendrite reaching only layer 3.

Both are densely spiny and both keep their dendrites inside their own column.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 8 to 15 micrometres across, the smallest excitatory neurons of the cortex, with a compact dendritic sphere of radius 100 to 200 micrometres holding roughly 1,500 to 4,000 micrometres of dendrite, a few thousand spines, and a local axon arbour usually under one millimetre wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Transcriptomics splits the population into L4 IT proper and an intermediate L4/5 IT group, while anatomically true spiny stellates dominate primary sensory cortex and are most extreme in primary visual cortex, where layer 4 divides into sublayers 4A, 4B, 4C alpha and 4C beta.

Human primary motor cortex was called agranular for a century, yet sequencing finds a layer 4 like type in it, so granular against agranular is a matter of degree.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 4 of all granular neocortex: primary visual cortex, primary auditory cortex, primary somatosensory areas 3b and 1, and the granular parts of parietal, temporal and prefrontal association cortex.

It is densest in primary visual sublayer 4C beta, target of the parvocellular relay of the lateral geniculate nucleus, and it sits exactly at the interface between thalamocortical input and cortical microcircuit, dendrites in the thalamic terminal field and axon terminals in layer 2/3.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 2.6 billion across both hemispheres, range 2.0 to 3.0 billion, moderate confidence, roughly 20 percent of the 13.1 billion excitatory cortical neurons and about 3 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the brain.

Confidence is limited because the layer 4 share swings from very high in primary visual cortex to near zero in motor cortex.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting minus 70 to minus 75 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, input resistance high at 100 to 200 megohms because the cell is small, time constant short at 8 to 15 milliseconds, and firing regular with strong fast adaptation, so a maintained input gives a brief burst then a slow train and the layer reports change rather than steady state.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The counterintuitive fact of layer 4 is that the thalamus supplies only a small minority of its excitatory synapses, a few percent in cat primary visual cortex and commonly quoted as 5 to 10 percent, with the rest from other layer 4 cells.

Thalamic synapses win anyway because they fire in synchrony, land close to the soma and are individually strong, and outputs are dominated by a dense projection to layer 2/3 directly above.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single thin axon rises vertically with most branches ending within about 300 micrometres horizontally of the parent cell, conducting slowly at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second yet delivering to layer 2/3 in only 1 to 3 milliseconds because the distance is so short.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate loaded by VGLUT1, acting on AMPA and NMDA receptors, with layer 4 notable for NMDA receptors of both high and low single-channel conductance and for thalamic synapses among the most reliable glutamate release sites in the brain.

Acetylcholine and noradrenaline gate how strongly the thalamus drives the layer during attention and arousal.

MYELINATION:

Mostly unmyelinated, because a short axon inside its own column gains almost nothing in speed for a large metabolic cost, whereas the thalamic axons arriving here are thickly myelinated right up to their terminals, producing the pale band visible to the naked eye in primary visual cortex, the stria of Gennari.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Layer 4 of primary sensory cortex has the highest resting glucose uptake and the highest density of mitochondria and of the enzyme cytochrome oxidase of any cortical layer, so tightly bound to this population that anatomists use the stain to reveal ocular dominance columns and barrels.

Per cell the demand is modest; per cubic millimetre it is the highest in the cortex.

PLASTICITY:

This is the classic critical period cell, its thalamocortical synapses strongly plastic in early life and much less so later, which is why a squint or cataract in the first years permanently reshapes visual cortex while the same insult in an adult does not.

Adult layer 4 keeps short-term depression as an automatic gain control and can still remap slowly after peripheral injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Layer 4 is wrapped in perineuronal nets, lattices of chondroitin sulphate proteoglycan laid down as the critical period closes, and these nets are the physical brake on plasticity: dissolve them with the enzyme chondroitinase and juvenile plasticity returns.

Astrocytes here carry the highest density of glutamate transporters in the cortex, because synchronous thalamic volleys would otherwise blur the map by spilling glutamate onto neighbouring columns, and the capillary bed is the densest of any layer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cortex goes blind, deaf and numb while remaining fully alive, because the driving sensory signal would have no amplifier and no organised entry point.

The barrels, the ocular dominance columns and the tonotopic gradients would dissolve; layer 4 is what gives primary sensory cortex its grain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Layer 4 dendrites integrate their spine inputs almost linearly, and in vivo calcium measurements in single spines show signals adding without cooperativity between neighbours, with a rise of about 85 milliseconds plus or minus 53 and a decay of about 412 milliseconds plus or minus 240.

Linear summation makes layer 4 an honest adder rather than a pattern detector, exactly what a first stage should be.

These cells are also gap-junction coupled in development, they instruct the maturation of surrounding interneurons, and their activity drives the vascular response that magnetic resonance imaging records as the blood oxygen level dependent signal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Thalamocortical excitation, 5 to 10 percent of excitatory synapses but synchronous; model as 40 to 100 synapses with high release probability.

◦ Local recurrent excitation from other L4 IT cells, about 90 percent of synapses, asynchronous and weak, plus parvalbumin inhibition 1 to 2 milliseconds behind the volley.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Strong vertical projection to layer 2/3 of the same column, weaker ones to layers 5 and 6 and back into layer 4.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, adaptation current, short-term depression variable for the thalamic synapse scaled 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 70, threshold minus 45, reset minus 60 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 200 megohms; thalamic synapse count 40 to 100; local synapse count 600 to 1,500.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds; adaptation 60 to 120 milliseconds; thalamic depression recovery 200 to 500 milliseconds; critical period, in humans, the first months to years of life.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Deprivation in the critical period, as in amblyopia after childhood squint or cataract, permanently shrinks the layer 4 territory of the deprived eye and cannot be reversed in adulthood.

◦ Loss of thalamic drive to this layer is a common final path in cortical blindness and cortical deafness.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show how a small synchronous thalamic minority drives a layer four neuron while a large asynchronous local majority does not.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value minus 70.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called thalamic drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called local drive with initial value 9.
Create a number variable called time now with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called volley is on with initial value false.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Calculate the sum of time now and 0.1.
    Set time now to the result.
    Set volley is on to true when time now lies between 10 and 25 and false otherwise.
    If volley is on is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set thalamic drive to 30.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set thalamic drive to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of thalamic drive and local drive.
    Set membrane voltage to the result of one leaky integration step towards that sum minus adaptation.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to minus 45.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to minus 60.
        Calculate the sum of adaptation and 2.
        Set adaptation to the result.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole argument is a controlled comparison, thalamic volley present against absent, and Python puts the two runs side by side in two lines.

# Layer 4 IT: a small, synchronous thalamic minority beats a large,
# asynchronous cortical majority. Voltages in mV, time in ms.
import random

N_THAL, N_LOCAL = 60, 900            # synapse counts
W_THAL, W_LOCAL = 0.50, 0.02         # mV of drive per synapse

def run(thalamus_on):
    random.seed(1)                   # deterministic run
    v, adapt, spikes = -70.0, 0.0, []
    for step in range(600):          # 60 ms at 0.1 ms resolution
        t = step * 0.1
        thal = N_THAL * W_THAL if (thalamus_on and 10.0 <= t < 25.0) else 0.0
        local = N_LOCAL * W_LOCAL * random.random()    # never synchronised
        v += 0.1 * (-(v + 70.0) + thal + local - adapt) / 10.0
        adapt += 0.1 * (-adapt) / 90.0
        if v >= -45.0:
            v, adapt = -60.0, adapt + 2.0
            spikes.append(round(t, 1))
    return spikes

print(f"thalamic share of synapses: {100.0 * N_THAL / (N_THAL + N_LOCAL):.1f} percent")
print("with the thalamic volley:", run(True))
print("without the volley:     ", run(False))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The smallest excitatory neuron in the cortex, often with no apical dendrite at all, packed at record density in a thin band, wrapped in a perineuronal net, and the only cortical cell that receives the thalamus as a driver rather than a suggestion.

CHAPTER 3. L5 IT (Layer 5 Intra-Telencephalic) (Action-Proposing Neuron) (COG)

Halfway down the cortical sheet sits a pyramid that could reach the spinal cord but never does, choosing instead to whisper to the other hemisphere and to the machinery of habit.

Action names where its output goes: it carries the local conclusion into the striatum, the machinery that selects among possible choices.

Proposing names exactly what it can do and marks what it cannot: it informs the choice, it never executes it.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L5 IT neuron is an excitatory pyramidal cell in layer 5, mostly its upper half, layer 5a, and IT means intratelencephalic, so its axon stays entirely inside the two cerebral hemispheres.

That single restriction separates it from its famous neighbour the L5 ET cell and makes this the cortex's deep associative and habit-forming channel, linking cortical areas across both hemispheres and delivering a bilateral copy of cortical activity to the striatum, the deep structure that learns which actions are worth repeating.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A slender-tufted pyramid whose apical dendrite often stops short of layer 1, and where it arrives the tuft is thin rather than the great bush of its extratelencephalic neighbour, while the basal dendrites spread widely in layer 5.

The geometry weights this cell towards local and feedforward input rather than the long-range feedback that lands in layer 1.

SIZE:

Cell body 15 to 22 micrometres across, larger than a layer 4 cell and smaller than a layer 5 extratelencephalic cell, with 5,000 to 9,000 micrometres of dendrite carrying several thousand to about 15,000 spines, and an axon adding a local arbour of one to two millimetres plus a callosal branch of 10 to 20 centimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Transcriptomics splits the layer 5 intratelencephalic population into several types, and human cortex adds a distinctive deep type, L5/6 IT Car3, closely related to the claustrum, a thin sheet of grey matter buried beside the cortex.

Anatomically the useful split is between cells whose main output crosses the callosum and cells whose main output descends to the striatum, though most do both.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 5, chiefly layer 5a, throughout the neocortex of both hemispheres, including motor, premotor, somatosensory, visual, auditory, parietal, temporal, insular and prefrontal areas, continuing without a break into layer 5b and the top of layer 6.

It occupies an important interface: the layer 5a to 5b boundary is where the intratelencephalic and extratelencephalic output systems meet, two populations living side by side that behave like different animals.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 1.3 billion in both hemispheres, range 1.0 to 1.7 billion, moderate confidence, roughly 10 percent of the 13.1 billion excitatory cortical neurons and about 1.5 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the brain.

The uncertainty comes mainly from where one draws the line between layer 5 IT and layer 6 IT, a boundary that is transcriptomically graded rather than sharp.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting minus 72 millivolts, threshold minus 45 millivolts, input resistance 60 to 150 megohms, time constant 12 to 20 milliseconds, regular spiking and adapting, almost never bursting.

Its most diagnostic feature is what it lacks: far less hyperpolarisation-activated sag current, carried by HCN channels, than the extratelencephalic cell beside it, so a negative current makes its voltage drop and stay down where the neighbour's sags back up within a hundred milliseconds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from layers 2/3 and 4 above, from other layer 5 IT cells, from the opposite hemisphere, from higher-order thalamus onto the apical dendrite, and from local inhibitory cells.

The single most important wiring fact is an asymmetry: L5 IT cells connect strongly and frequently onto L5 ET cells while L5 ET cells almost never connect back, so the deep cortex is a one-way street and any simulation with a symmetrical matrix here will produce the wrong dynamics.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Medium calibre at 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres, with myelinated callosal branches conducting at 3 to 10 metres per second so a signal takes 10 to 30 milliseconds to cross between hemispheres, and a thin corticostriatal branch whose fine terminal field sprinkles the striatum lightly rather than concentrating on one spot.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate packaged by VGLUT1, acting on AMPA and NMDA receptors, and its corticostriatal terminals are the main excitatory input to the striatal medium spiny neuron, the site where dopamine from midbrain fibres gates whether a co-active cortical and striatal pair strengthens or weakens.

MYELINATION:

Callosal and corticostriatal branches are myelinated, patchy near the soma and continuous once in white matter, while local collaterals inside layer 5 are mostly bare, an intermediate investment that makes this cell cheaper to run than its extratelencephalic neighbour, which must insulate a metre-long wire.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate to high, dominated by maintaining a long branched axon supplied from a soma that may be twenty centimetres away, with axonal transport at a few hundred millimetres per day meaning a protein can take a day or more to reach a callosal terminal.

At 1 to 10 hertz the cell burns a few billion molecules of adenosine triphosphate per second, with the axon and its synapses, not the spikes, taking the largest share.

PLASTICITY:

Its corticostriatal synapses show dopamine-dependent long-term potentiation and depression and are the leading candidate substrate for habit learning and skill acquisition, while callosal synapses show ordinary spike-timing-dependent plasticity and the axon itself adds and prunes striatal boutons over weeks of motor learning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing dramatic distinguishes the glia in layer 5a from those elsewhere in the cortex, and it is worth saying that plainly so no reader hunts for a specialisation that is not there.

The one real point concerns white matter: because this axon spends most of its length there, most of its oligodendrocyte partners are white matter oligodendrocytes rather than the grey matter ones wrapping its first millimetre, and the two populations differ in gene expression and injury response, so a white matter disease hits the axon while sparing the dendrites.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take this cell away and the deep cortex becomes a broadcasting station with no correspondents: layer 5 could still command muscles, but it could not tell the other hemisphere what it was doing, build habits in the striatum, or compare deep activity across areas.

Movements would become clumsy and one-sided, and practice would stop producing automaticity.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The thin apical tuft still supports NMDA receptor spikes, local regenerative events in single branches that let a cluster of nearby synapses count for far more than their sum, while back-propagating action potentials invade the basal dendrites reliably and the apical trunk less so, setting a ceiling on how far up a synapse can be tagged as recently active.

It releases the retrograde messenger 2-arachidonoylglycerol, an endocannabinoid, from its dendrites to suppress incoming inhibition for seconds after strong activity, takes up lactate supplied by astrocytes, and contributes to local blood flow control through nitric oxide.

Across weeks of learning it physically rebuilds its striatal terminals, a computation with no equivalent in an integrate and fire equation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Feedforward excitation from layers 2/3 and 4, 0 to 20 nanosiemens; recurrent and callosal excitation, 0 to 15 nanosiemens.

◦ Higher-order thalamic excitation onto the apical dendrite, 0 to 8 nanosiemens; inhibition 0 to 30 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spikes to contralateral and ipsilateral cortex, to both striata, and critically to L5 ET cells; no output below the telencephalon, which must be a hard constraint.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, adaptation current, apical NMDA spike flag, per-synapse weights, callosal delay line holding spikes in flight.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 72, threshold minus 45, reset minus 60 millivolts; input resistance 60 to 150 megohms; connection probability L5 IT to L5 ET of order 0.1 to 0.2 locally, L5 ET to L5 IT near zero.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 12 to 20 milliseconds; adaptation 100 to 400 milliseconds; callosal delay 10 to 30 milliseconds; corticostriatal plasticity seconds to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Huntington's disease the corticostriatal terminals of these cells degenerate early, before striatal cell death is complete, and restoring cortical input slows the disease in animal models.

◦ Demyelination of the callosal branch, as in multiple sclerosis, breaks bimanual coordination while leaving each hemisphere individually competent.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to demonstrate the one way wiring rule between layer five intratelencephalic cells and layer five extratelencephalic cells.
Create a number variable called weight from intratelencephalic to extratelencephalic with initial value 0.90.
Create a number variable called weight from extratelencephalic to intratelencephalic with initial value 0.02.
Create a list variable called drive order with initial value empty list.
Add the text intratelencephalic to the end of drive order.
Add the text extratelencephalic to the end of drive order.
Create a number variable called partner spikes with initial value 0.
For each cell name in drive order, perform the following operations.
    Set partner spikes to 0.
    Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
        Set the voltage of cell name to the result of one leaky integration step under injected current.
        Check if the voltage of cell name is greater than or equal to minus 45.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set the voltage of cell name to minus 60.
            Calculate the sum of the synaptic input of the partner cell and the outgoing weight of cell name.
            Set the synaptic input of the partner cell to the result.
        End the conditional block.
        Check if the voltage of the partner cell is greater than or equal to minus 45.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set the voltage of the partner cell to minus 60.
            Calculate the sum of partner spikes and 1.
            Set partner spikes to the result.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Display partner spikes to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the finding is a two-by-two connectivity asymmetry, and a dictionary keyed by ordered pairs states that asymmetry more honestly than a matrix of numbers.

# Layer 5 IT drives layer 5 ET, but not the reverse (Kiritani et al., 2012).
# Two leaky integrate-and-fire cells; only the connection matrix differs.
W = {("IT", "ET"): 0.90, ("ET", "IT"): 0.02}   # deliberately asymmetric
DT, TAU_M, TAU_SYN = 0.1, 13.0, 5.0            # ms
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -70.0, -45.0, -60.0    # mV
DRIVE, PSP = 32.0, 40.0                        # injected mV, synaptic mV

def run(driven):
    v = {"IT": V_REST, "ET": V_REST}
    syn = {"IT": 0.0, "ET": 0.0}
    fired = {"IT": 0, "ET": 0}
    for _ in range(2000):                      # 200 ms
        for k in ("IT", "ET"):
            inj = DRIVE if k == driven else 0.0
            v[k] += DT * (-(v[k] - V_REST) + inj + syn[k]) / TAU_M
            syn[k] += DT * (-syn[k]) / TAU_SYN
        for k in ("IT", "ET"):
            if v[k] >= V_TH:
                v[k] = V_RESET
                fired[k] += 1
                other = "ET" if k == "IT" else "IT"
                syn[other] += PSP * W[(k, other)]
    return fired

print("inject current into the L5 IT cell:", run("IT"))
print("inject current into the L5 ET cell:", run("ET"))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A slender-tufted pyramid in layer 5 with almost no sag current, whose axon crosses the corpus callosum and rains lightly on both striata, and which drives its giant neighbour without ever being driven back by it.

CHAPTER 4. L5 ET (Layer 5 Extra-Telencephalic) (Body-Commanding Neuron) (COG)

In 1874 Vladimir Betz described the largest neurons in the human brain, cells big enough to see with the naked eye, and every voluntary movement you make begins in cells of their family.

Body names its target zone, as this is the only cortical excitatory cell whose axon leaves the forebrain, reaching down to the motor systems of the brainstem, spinal cord, superior colliculus, and pons.

Commanding names the one thing no other cell in this family can do - turn a thought into a movement.

It includes the giant Betz cells.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L5 ET neuron is the cortex's only direct line out, ET standing for extratelencephalic, meaning the axon leaves the telencephalon, the pair of cerebral hemispheres, and descends to the thalamus, superior colliculus, pontine nuclei, red nucleus, medulla and, in a subset, the spinal cord.

Its older name is pyramidal tract neuron, and its giant subset in primary motor cortex, the Betz cells, forms part of the direct cortico-motoneuronal pathway, a single-synapse connection from cortex to the spinal motor neurons that move the hand.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A thick-tufted pyramid: large soma, a stout apical dendrite running unbroken to layer 1 and exploding there into a broad tuft, oblique dendrites off the trunk, and a heavy basal skirt.

Tuft and soma are separated by up to two millimetres of dendrite, making the cell two electrically semi-independent computers joined by a cable.

SIZE:

Ordinary L5 ET somata are 20 to 35 micrometres across, while Betz cells are the giants at 60 to 110 micrometres in their long axis, visible without magnification in a stained section.

Total dendritic length runs to 15,000 to 30,000 micrometres with tens of thousands of spines, and the axon of a corticospinal Betz cell can approach one metre, among the longest cells in the body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The divisions are by target and they overlap, because most cells branch to several: corticospinal, corticobulbar, corticopontine, corticotectal, corticorubral, and layer 5 corticothalamic cells driving higher-order thalamic nuclei.

Betz cells make up only about 10 percent of layer 5 pyramidal neurons in primary motor cortex and contribute only about 2 to 3 percent of the axons in the corticospinal tract.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 5b of the neocortex, immediately beneath the layer 5 intratelencephalic population, concentrated above all in primary motor cortex, Brodmann area 4, in the anterior bank of the central sulcus, with Betz cells largest in the medial leg and trunk representation, plus substantial populations in premotor and supplementary motor cortex, somatosensory areas 3a, 3b, 1 and 2, and visual and auditory cortex.

The cell straddles three interfaces: its tuft in layer 1 among the feedback fibres, its soma in layer 5b, and its axon crossing into the internal capsule and then out of the telencephalon entirely at the cerebral peduncle.

NUMBER:

This number is genuinely uncertain: transcriptomic proportions would put layer 5 extratelencephalic cells at roughly 1 percent of cortical excitatory neurons, about 130 million, while classical axon counts point lower, the human corticospinal tract carrying a little over one million axons per side, about 700,000 of them myelinated.

A defensible working figure is 40 to 130 million in both hemispheres, best estimate about 60 million, low confidence, which is about 0.07 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the brain.

Betz cells specifically number roughly 30,000 to 40,000 per hemisphere, a classical count not substantially revised since.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Low input resistance at 20 to 60 megohms because the cell is large and leaky, resting minus 68 to minus 75 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, and intrinsically bursting rather than regularly adapting.

The diagnostic feature is a large sag: hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated channels, chiefly HCN1, pack into the apical dendrite at rising density towards the tuft, so a negative current makes the voltage dip then creep back up.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives layer 2/3 and layer 5 intratelencephalic input on its basal and perisomatic dendrites, long-range feedback and non-specific thalamic input on its layer 1 tuft, somatostatin inhibition on its distal dendrites and parvalbumin basket and chandelier inhibition at soma and axon initial segment, with Betz cells carrying an exceptionally dense perisomatic basket.

Its outputs reach the spinal ventral horn, the medullary and pontine reticular formation, the pontine nuclei feeding the cerebellum, the superior colliculus, the red nucleus, the striatum by collateral and the higher-order thalamus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thick at 2 to 5 micrometres for the fastest corticospinal fibres and up to 10 to 20 micrometres including myelin, conducting at up to about 70 metres per second, the highest of any central axon, giving a cortex to lumbar delay of roughly 20 milliseconds, with collaterals branching off at each level so one spike is announced simultaneously at many stations.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate loaded by VGLUT1, forming powerful AMPA and NMDA synapses directly onto spinal motor neurons and interneurons at the far end, and the cell expresses high levels of neurofilament heavy chain, a structural protein whose appearance in blood is now used as a biomarker of its degeneration.

MYELINATION:

The most heavily myelinated cortical output axon in the body, with long internodes, the myelinated stretches between nodes of Ranvier, and a g-ratio, axon diameter divided by total fibre diameter, near the optimum of 0.6 to 0.7.

The neuron partly builds its own insulation, because activity in the axon recruits oligodendrocyte precursor cells and increases myelination according to use.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Extreme, and central to its pathology: a cell maintaining up to a metre of thick, high-conductance axon with hundreds of thousands of channels at every node, shipping every protein from one distant soma, lives permanently close to its energy ceiling.

Mitochondrial density in these axons is among the highest in the nervous system, and any drop in adenosine triphosphate supply hits the most distal terminals first, exactly the pattern seen in disease.

PLASTICITY:

Plasticity at its inputs supports motor learning and the cortico-motoneuronal connection can be strengthened through practice across days to weeks, while the axon sprouts new collaterals within the spinal cord after partial injury, which is the anatomical basis of much recovery after stroke.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Betz cells attract an unusually dense sheath of astrocyte processes and a striking accumulation of perineuronal net material.

In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis the oligodendrocytes supporting these axons downregulate their myelinating genes and the surrounding microglia shift into an endolysosomal reactive state, changes that may precede the neuron's death, while a single-nucleus multiomic atlas of that disease has nominated an astrocyte population expressing the gene WDR49 as potentially neuroprotective, so the glia here are part of the disease rather than bystanders.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and primary motor cortex becomes a beautifully organised map of movements that cannot move anything, keeping its somatotopy, its cerebellar and basal ganglia inputs and its internal chatter while no instruction reaches a muscle.

Clinically that is the upper motor neuron syndrome, weakness, loss of fine finger movement, spasticity and a positive Babinski sign, with the muscles themselves intact; the flavour lost is agency.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the cell in which dendritic computation was discovered: the tuft in layer 1 holds a calcium spike initiation zone, and when a spike back-propagating from the soma arrives while the tuft is depolarised by feedback, the two coincide and trigger a long calcium plateau that floods back to the soma and turns a single spike into a high-frequency burst.

This backpropagation-activated calcium spike firing makes the cell a coincidence detector between its base and its top, typically needing a train of about four action potentials at around 100 hertz, and appearing reliably only when the apical trunk exceeds roughly 350 micrometres.

The cell also controls local blood flow, instructs myelination of its own axon, and through its brainstem collaterals informs the cerebellum of every command before the movement has happened.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Perisomatic and basal excitation from L5 IT and L2/3 IT cells, 0 to 25 nanosiemens; apical tuft excitation from layer 1, 0 to 15 nanosiemens.

◦ Distal somatostatin and perisomatic parvalbumin inhibition, 0 to 40 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spikes to spinal ventral horn, reticular formation, pontine nuclei, superior colliculus, red nucleus, higher-order thalamus and striatum.

◦ Burst against single spike must be an explicit output flag, because downstream targets respond very differently to the two.

• STATE.

◦ Somatic voltage, apical tuft voltage, calcium plateau timer, sag current activation, time since last somatic spike.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 70, threshold minus 45, reset minus 58 millivolts; input resistance 20 to 60 megohms; tuft plateau threshold about 8 millivolts; conduction velocity 40 to 70 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Somatic membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds; apical tuft 25 to 40 milliseconds; sag activation 50 to 200 milliseconds; calcium plateau 30 to 50 milliseconds; coincidence window 5 to 30 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis these neurons degenerate selectively; sequencing of 79,169 single nuclei from human cortex found THY1-positive extratelencephalic neurons expressing that disease's risk genes at significantly higher levels than other cortical neurons, ill or not, and switching on protein-homeostasis and stress-response programmes in patients.

◦ Internal capsule stroke cuts the axon while the soma survives; hereditary spastic paraplegia kills the longest axons from their distal ends first.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to detect coincidence between perisomatic drive and apical tuft drive in a layer five extratelencephalic neuron and to turn that coincidence into a burst.
Create a number variable called somatic voltage with initial value minus 70.
Create a number variable called tuft depolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called plateau remaining with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called time since last spike with initial value 1000.
Create a number variable called burst spike count with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called tuft is loaded with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called spike was recent with initial value false.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
    Set tuft depolarisation to the result of one leaky integration step towards the layer one input.
    Check if tuft depolarisation is greater than 8.
    Set tuft is loaded to the result.
    Check if time since last spike is less than 6.
    Set spike was recent to the result.
    Check if both tuft is loaded and spike was recent are true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau remaining to 40.
    End the conditional block.
    Set somatic voltage to the result of one leaky integration step including any plateau current.
    Check if somatic voltage is greater than or equal to minus 45.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set somatic voltage to minus 58.
        Set time since last spike to 0.
        Calculate the sum of burst spike count and 1.
        Set burst spike count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display burst spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a whole-cortex simulation will instantiate tens of millions of these two-compartment cells and this inner loop must run with nothing between it and the processor.

#include <stdio.h>

/* Two-compartment Betz-cell model: soma plus apical tuft. A tuft calcium
   plateau, triggered only when tuft depolarisation coincides with a recent
   somatic spike, converts steady firing into a burst. mV and ms. */
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.05, tau_s = 12.0, tau_d = 30.0;
    const double v_rest = -70.0, v_th = -45.0, v_reset = -58.0;
    const double d_th = 8.0, plateau_ms = 40.0, bac_drive = 14.0;
    double v = v_rest, d = 0.0, plateau = 0.0, since_spike = 1000.0;
    int early = 0, late = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 2000; i++) {                        /* 100 ms */
        double t = i * dt;
        double drive_s = (t >= 20.0 && t < 90.0) ? 30.0 : 0.0;  /* basal */
        double drive_d = (t >= 30.0 && t < 60.0) ? 14.0 : 0.0;  /* layer 1 */
        d += dt * (-d + drive_d) / tau_d;
        if (plateau <= 0.0 && d > d_th && since_spike < 6.0)
            plateau = plateau_ms;                           /* coincidence */
