        double bac = (plateau > 0.0) ? bac_drive : 0.0;
        if (plateau > 0.0) plateau -= dt;
        v += dt * (-(v - v_rest) + drive_s + bac) / tau_s;
        since_spike += dt;
        if (v >= v_th) { v = v_reset; since_spike = 0.0;
                         if (bac > 0.0) late++; else early++; }
    }
    printf("spikes before the calcium plateau: %d\n", early);
    printf("spikes during the calcium plateau: %d\n", late);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only cortical neuron whose axon leaves the cerebral hemispheres, the largest cell in the human brain in its Betz form, marked by a deep sag current, a thick tuft in layer 1 and a calcium plateau that fuses top and bottom into one burst.

CHAPTER 5. L5/6 NP (Layer 5 and 6 Near-Projecting Non-Projecting) (Short-Reach Neuron) (COG)

Somewhere in the deepest quarter of your cortex is a rare cell named for what it does not do: it grows a projection axon, sends it into the white matter, and then stops almost as soon as it has started.

Short-Reach names the single solid anatomical fact - it projects sparsely and only nearby, leaving its function deliberately modest until further research establishes its precise role.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L5/6 NP neuron is an excitatory glutamatergic cell straddling the border between cortical layers 5 and 6, and NP stands for near-projecting, a name coined when tracing from a genetic driver line based on the gene Slc17a8, which encodes the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT3, revealed only sparse projections to neighbouring cortical areas rather than long descending or callosal trunks.

It is a puzzle in plain sight, carrying the transcriptional identity of a projection neuron yet projecting almost nowhere, and the best current reading is that it is a sparse, slow integrator reporting to a few adjacent territories.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A pyramidal cell with a long, thin, sparsely branched apical dendrite and basal dendrites that spread more horizontally and further than those of neighbouring layer 5 cells, with comparatively low spine density that gives them a smoother look than the heavily beaded dendrites of a layer 2/3 cell.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 15 to 25 micrometres across, similar to a layer 5 intratelencephalic cell and smaller than a thick-tufted extratelencephalic neuron, with 4,000 to 9,000 micrometres of dendrite and, because spine density is low, a total input count probably in the low thousands rather than the tens of thousands.

These figures come from rodent reconstructions and a few human recordings, so treat them as provisional.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

In the mouse, four related deep types were distinguished by the marker genes Slc17a8, Trhr, Tshz2, Sla2 and Rapgef3, while in human cortex the near-projecting group is small, resolved into only a handful of transcriptomic types whose marker genes do not map one to one onto the mouse names.

Treat it as one class with wide parameter variance rather than several confident subtypes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The deep half of layer 5 and the upper part of layer 6, concentrated exactly at the layer 5b to layer 6a interface, in neocortex of both hemispheres, across motor, somatosensory, visual, auditory and prefrontal cortex, but nowhere abundant.

Its position matters, because this border is where the outward-facing extratelencephalic output system meets the inward-facing corticothalamic feedback system, so the cell sits with its dendrites in one regime and its soma in the other.

NUMBER:

Best estimate about 130 million in both hemispheres, range 80 to 200 million, low to moderate confidence, which is about 1 percent of the 13.1 billion excitatory cortical neurons and roughly 0.15 percent of the approximately 86 billion neurons in the brain.

The estimate rests entirely on the proportion of nuclei assigned to this class in single-nucleus sequencing, a relative measure never checked against a direct anatomical count in humans, so the honest bound is more than 50 million and less than 400 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The available recordings describe relatively high input resistance for a deep pyramidal neuron at 100 to 250 megohms, a slow membrane time constant of 20 to 30 milliseconds, comparatively broad action potentials and a low maximum firing rate, giving a cell that is easy to depolarise but hard to drive fast.

This rests on a small number of patch-clamp and patch-seq recordings, and confidence here is lower than for any other type in this section.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from local layer 5 and layer 6 excitatory cells, from layer 2/3 above, from higher-order thalamic nuclei, and from local interneurons with somatostatin cells prominent among them.

Outputs are the defining oddity: a sparse projection to neighbouring cortical areas, local collaterals within deep cortex, and in some tracing studies a thin projection to striatum and posterior thalamic territory, with local connectivity sparser than between other deep excitatory types.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single thin axon of roughly 0.3 to 0.8 micrometres, descending into white matter and travelling only a few millimetres to a couple of centimetres before arborising at perhaps 1 to 5 metres per second, and its distinguishing feature is negative: it never descends past the telencephalon and forms no dense terminal field anywhere.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, with VGLUT1 as the principal vesicular transporter, while the mouse marker gene Slc17a8 encodes VGLUT3, a transporter otherwise confined to a small set of specialised cells, though messenger RNA expression alone does not prove the protein loads glutamate at this cell's terminals.

The type also shows enriched expression of peptide receptor genes including thyrotropin-releasing hormone receptor in the mouse, hinting at strong neuromodulatory control.

MYELINATION:

Light and patchy, with the short white matter segment myelinated and the local grey matter collaterals largely bare, so that total investment in myelin is far below that of an extratelencephalic neighbour.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low relative to other deep projection neurons, because a short axon, a modest spine count and a low firing rate together give one of the smallest energy budgets of any cortical pyramidal cell, probably well under a billion molecules of adenosine triphosphate per second at rest, though no direct measurement exists for this type.

PLASTICITY:

Little is known, and it is better to say so than to guess: its synapses are ordinary glutamatergic synapses with AMPA and NMDA receptors and should support spike-timing-dependent plasticity, and its high input resistance would make it unusually sensitive to small weight changes.

No study has characterised long-term potentiation or spine turnover in transcriptomically identified near-projecting cells, so a simulation should use generic pyramidal rules and flag them as unverified.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing specific has been described, and that is the honest answer: the astrocytes, oligodendrocytes and microglia at the layer 5 to 6 border serve the whole local population, and no glial specialisation has been reported around near-projecting cells in particular.

This is stated plainly so no reader assumes a specialisation exists and has merely been omitted; the question has not been asked.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the deep cortex loses a quiet, slow, wide-field channel linking each area to its immediate neighbours without using the long-range highways, and because the population is small the loss would probably be subtle rather than catastrophic: no paralysis, no blindness, but a plausible loss of the local smoothing that keeps neighbouring cortical areas in register.

The flavour lost is the cortex's short-range gossip between adjacent neighbourhoods, as distinct from the long-distance calls that L5 IT and L5 ET cells make.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

High input resistance and a slow membrane make this an integrator over tens of milliseconds rather than a coincidence detector, so it naturally reports the average of its inputs rather than their synchrony.

Its long thin apical dendrite should support NMDA receptor spikes, local regenerative events in single branches, and its broad action potential delivers more calcium into each presynaptic terminal than a narrow spike would, favouring reliable release at low rates.

Because it expresses transporters and peptide receptors associated with volume transmission, meaning chemical signalling that diffuses through the extracellular space rather than crossing one synaptic cleft, it is a plausible participant in slow chemical modulation of deep cortex, though no dendritic recordings exist from a confirmed human near-projecting cell and its gap-junction coupling, if any, is unknown.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Local excitation from deep layer 5 and layer 6 pyramidal cells, 0 to 12 nanosiemens; descending excitation from layer 2/3, 0 to 8 nanosiemens.

◦ Higher-order thalamic excitation 0 to 6 nanosiemens; inhibition dominated by somatostatin interneurons, 0 to 20 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Sparse spikes to adjacent cortical areas and to local deep cortex.

◦ Weak, uncertain projections to striatum and posterior thalamus; make this an optional switch, defaulted off and labelled uncertain.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, refractory timer reflecting the broad action potential, slow adaptation current.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 70, threshold minus 42, reset minus 62 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 250 megohms; action potential half-width 1.2 to 2.0 milliseconds; population share about 1 percent of cortical excitatory neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds; absolute refractory period 3 to 5 milliseconds; adaptation 200 to 600 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No disease is yet attributed specifically to this type, and claiming one would be invention.

◦ Its high input resistance makes it disproportionately sensitive to any drop in inhibitory tone, so in models of disinhibited cortex it is among the first cells recruited, and its transcriptomic neighbours at the layer 5 to 6 border are among the populations reported as altered in single-nucleus studies of schizophrenia and autism, a correlation that is not yet a mechanism.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compare a near projecting neuron with a typical deep pyramidal neuron given the same very small injected current.
Create a number variable called injected current in nanoamperes with initial value 0.12.
Create a list variable called cells to test with initial value empty list.
Add the text near projecting to the end of cells to test.
Add the text typical deep pyramid to the end of cells to test.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value minus 70.
Create a number variable called drive in millivolts with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called refractory remaining with initial value 0.
For each cell name in cells to test, perform the following operations.
    Set membrane voltage to minus 70.
    Set spike count to 0.
    Set drive in millivolts to the product of the input resistance of cell name and injected current in nanoamperes.
    Repeat the following steps exactly 5000 times.
        If refractory remaining is greater than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set refractory remaining to the difference of refractory remaining and 0.1.
            Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
        End the conditional block.
        Set membrane voltage to the result of one leaky integration step towards drive in millivolts.
        Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to minus 42.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set membrane voltage to minus 62.
            Set refractory remaining to the action potential width of cell name.
            Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
            Set spike count to the result.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Display spike count to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the claim is a parameter comparison rather than a heavy computation, and a dictionary of named cell parameters reads like the table it replaces.

# L5/6 NP versus a typical deep pyramidal cell, same tiny current injection.
# High input resistance plus a broad spike gives a cell that fires easily
# but never fires fast. mV, megohms, ms.
CELLS = {
    "L5/6 NP":              {"r_in": 250.0, "tau": 25.0, "ap_width": 4.0},
    "typical deep pyramid": {"r_in":  80.0, "tau": 13.0, "ap_width": 1.5},
}
I_NA = 0.12          # nanoamperes; megohms times nanoamperes gives millivolts
DT, DURATION = 0.1, 500.0
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -70.0, -42.0, -62.0

def firing_rate(p):
    drive = p["r_in"] * I_NA        # steady-state depolarisation, mV
    v, refractory, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0
    for _ in range(int(DURATION / DT)):
        if refractory > 0.0:
            refractory -= DT
            continue
        v += DT * (-(v - V_REST) + drive) / p["tau"]
        if v >= V_TH:
            v, refractory, spikes = V_RESET, p["ap_width"], spikes + 1
    return drive, spikes * 1000.0 / DURATION

for name, p in CELLS.items():
    drive, rate = firing_rate(p)
    print(f"{name:22s} drive {drive:5.1f} mV   rate {rate:5.1f} Hz")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A rare, high-resistance, broad-spiking pyramid at the layer 5 to 6 border that builds a projection axon and then declines to project, the only cortical excitatory type defined by the shortness of its reach.

CHAPTER 6. L6 CT (Layer 6 Cortico-Thalamic) (Input-Tuning Neuron) (COG)

At the very bottom of the cortex lives a cell that spends its life talking back to the structure that fed it, outnumbering the incoming fibres ten to one and telling the thalamus what to listen for next.

Input names its target: it sends its axon back down to the thalamus, the structure that fed the cortex in the first place, and sets how much of the stream gets through on the next pass.

Tuning names the loop: the cortex is not fed passively; it specifies its own diet.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The L6 CT neuron is the cortex's feedback engine, CT standing for corticothalamic, and its defining property is a long axon descending from layer 6, the deepest cortical layer, into the thalamus, the relay station through which almost all sensory information reaches the cortex, and specifically back into the nucleus that supplies its own cortical area.

Its role is not to relay but to modulate, adjusting the gain, timing and firing mode of thalamic relay cells and so deciding whether the thalamus passes signals faithfully or fires in rhythmic bursts, which is the difference between the waking brain and the sleeping one.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small upright pyramid whose apical dendrite, in most corticothalamic cells, ends around the layer 4 and 5 border and never reaches layer 1, so unlike almost every other cortical pyramid this cell does not sample the great feedback field at the cortical surface.

Basal dendrites are short and bushy and stay in layer 6, while the minority that do reach layer 1 tend to be the cells projecting to higher-order thalamic nuclei.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are small at 10 to 18 micrometres across, with a modest 2,000 to 5,000 micrometres of dendrite and spine counts in the low thousands, while the axon descends several centimetres and produces an enormous number of very small terminal boutons, so a cell with a small dendritic tree may make thousands of thalamic synapses.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

One group projects to the primary relay nucleus alone, for example the ventral posterior medial nucleus for touch or the lateral geniculate nucleus for vision, and a second projects to both that and a higher-order nucleus such as the posterior medial nucleus or the pulvinar, with both sending collaterals into the thalamic reticular nucleus, a thin shell of inhibitory cells wrapped around the thalamus.

Transcriptomics separates corticothalamic cells from layer 6 intratelencephalic cells and from the subplate-derived L6b cells beneath them.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 6, chiefly layer 6a, throughout the neocortex of both hemispheres: primary somatosensory cortex projecting to the ventral posterior medial and posterior medial nuclei and the thalamic reticular nucleus, primary visual cortex to the lateral geniculate nucleus and pulvinar, primary auditory cortex to the medial geniculate body, and prefrontal cortex to the mediodorsal nucleus.

The cell sits at the deepest interface in the cortex, its soma in the last layer of grey matter, its axon crossing immediately into white matter and its dendrites sampling the layer 5 to 6 boundary above.

NUMBER:

Best estimate about 900 million in both hemispheres, range 700 million to 1.2 billion, moderate confidence, roughly 7 percent of the 13.1 billion excitatory cortical neurons and about 1 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the brain.

One reason to keep the estimate below what mouse proportions suggest is that humans and marmosets have proportionally fewer layer 6 corticothalamic and layer 5 extratelencephalic neurons than mice, probably because the primate neocortex has expanded far more than its subcortical targets.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting minus 72 millivolts, threshold minus 45 millivolts, input resistance 100 to 200 megohms and a slow membrane time constant of 15 to 25 milliseconds.

Firing in vivo is famously sparse, layer 6 being the quietest layer of the cortex with many of these cells firing less than one spike per second during ordinary sensory stimulation, and when they do fire they often fire short high-frequency bursts, which matters because their synapses facilitate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from layer 5 above, from local layer 6 cells, from a modest ascending thalamic projection and from long-range cortical feedback.

The output side is extraordinary: layer 6 corticothalamic axons outnumber ascending thalamocortical axons by about ten to one and supply roughly 30 to 44 percent of all synapses onto thalamocortical relay neurons, more than the sensory periphery itself supplies, while collaterals excite the thalamic reticular nucleus so that every spike delivers direct excitation and, milliseconds later, indirect inhibition to the same relay cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A thin, lightly myelinated axon of 0.5 to 1.0 micrometres conducting at roughly 2 to 8 metres per second, slow compared with the thick ascending thalamocortical fibres, and the asymmetry is deliberate: information comes up fast and correction goes down slowly, so feedback acts on the next moment rather than the present one.

Terminal boutons in the primary relay nucleus are very small and sit on distal dendrites, the classic signature of a modulator rather than a driver.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate packaged by VGLUT1, acting on AMPA receptors, NMDA receptors and group I metabotropic glutamate receptors, which are slow G-protein coupled receptors producing depolarisations lasting hundreds of milliseconds to seconds.

That slow action is why sustained corticothalamic activity can switch a relay cell from burst mode into tonic mode and hold it there.

MYELINATION:

Light but real: the descending axon is myelinated once in white matter, with a thin sheath and short internodes matching its small diameter, while the dense terminal arbour inside the thalamus is unmyelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low per spike because the cell fires rarely, but surprisingly high in total because of the sheer number of terminals it maintains, the dominant cost being the housekeeping of thousands of small synapses several centimetres from the cell body, all supplied by axonal transport.

PLASTICITY:

The corticothalamic synapse is one of the most strongly facilitating synapses in the brain, growing stronger with each spike in a rapid train, and this short-term plasticity is arguably its main computation.

Over longer timescales the projection is refined by experience during development, in adults its strength tracks attentional state and sleep pressure, and long-term potentiation has been demonstrated at these synapses though it is less studied than at cortical ones.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Layer 6 sits directly against the white matter, so oligodendrocyte density around these somata is the highest of any cortical layer and the local astrocytes have a fibrous, white-matter-like character rather than the bushy protoplasmic form of the upper layers.

Within the thalamus these axons terminate in a quite different glial environment, dominated by thalamic astrocytes that regulate the extracellular potassium concentration governing burst firing, so one neuron depends on two different glial regimes at its two ends.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cortex loses its volume control over its own input: sensation would still arrive and layer 4 would still receive it, but the cortex could no longer tell the thalamus what to emphasise, switch it between burst and tonic mode, or sculpt attention at the gate.

Sleep spindles, the brief bursts of 11 to 16 hertz activity that punctuate light sleep and help consolidate memory, are generated by the loop between cortex, relay nuclei and the thalamic reticular nucleus, and without this cell they would not exist; the flavour lost is selectivity, leaving a cortex that hears everything and listens to nothing.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The most important non-linear property of this cell lives not in its membrane but in its synapse: corticothalamic terminals show pronounced short-term facilitation, so repeated stimulation produces progressively larger excitatory postsynaptic currents while the accompanying inhibition through the reticular nucleus depresses slightly, shifting the balance at the relay cell towards excitation as a cortical burst proceeds.

The cell is thus a temporal differentiator that ignores isolated spikes and responds explosively to sustained cortical demand.

Beyond that, its metabotropic action is slow chemical control outlasting the spike by a second, it drives layer 5a locally so feedback and cortical output stay coordinated, its reticular collaterals implement a built-in feedforward inhibition circuit, and its activity can induce high gamma oscillations through both cortico-cortical and cortico-thalamo-cortical routes.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Descending excitation from layer 5, 0 to 10 nanosiemens; local layer 6 recurrent excitation, 0 to 8 nanosiemens.

◦ Weak ascending thalamic excitation, 0 to 5 nanosiemens; inhibition from layer 6 interneurons, 0 to 20 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Facilitating excitatory synapses onto thalamocortical relay neurons in the matched primary nucleus, and in a subset onto a higher-order nucleus.

◦ Excitation of thalamic reticular nucleus inhibitory cells, producing delayed inhibition of the same relay cells, plus local excitation of layer 5a.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, adaptation current, burst counter, and per synapse the facilitation variable u and available-resource variable R, each scaled 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting minus 72, threshold minus 45 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 200 megohms; baseline release probability U about 0.09, very low, which is exactly what makes facilitation possible.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds; facilitation decay 500 to 700 milliseconds; vesicle pool recovery 130 to 150 milliseconds; metabotropic response 200 milliseconds to several seconds; spindle rhythm 11 to 16 hertz.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Excessive or badly timed corticothalamic drive switches the thalamocortical loop from normal spindle waves into spike-and-wave discharges, the electroencephalographic signature of absence epilepsy, in which a child stops mid-sentence and stares for a few seconds.

◦ Reduced corticothalamic tone accompanies the fall in sleep spindle density seen before typical spike-and-wave discharges appear and reported in schizophrenia, where it is linked to impaired overnight memory consolidation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model the facilitating corticothalamic synapse so that a burst of layer six spikes produces a growing response in the thalamus.
Create a number variable called baseline release probability with initial value 0.09.
Create a number variable called release readiness with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called available resources with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called released amount with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called previous time with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called thalamic responses with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 8 times.
    Add the next spike time at ten millisecond intervals to the end of spike times.
End the loop.
For each spike time in spike times, perform the following operations.
    Set release readiness to the result of decaying release readiness towards baseline release probability since previous time.
    Set available resources to the result of recovering available resources towards one since previous time.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and release readiness.
    Calculate the product of baseline release probability and that difference.
    Calculate the sum of release readiness and that product.
    Set release readiness to the result.
    Calculate the product of release readiness and available resources.
    Set released amount to the result.
    Calculate the difference of available resources and released amount.
    Set available resources to the result.
    Add released amount to the end of thalamic responses.
    Set previous time to spike time.
End the loop.
Display thalamic responses to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the Tsodyks and Markram synapse is three lines of arithmetic per spike and the whole point is watching two parameter sets diverge in the printed output.

# Corticothalamic (L6 CT) synapses facilitate; driver synapses depress.
# Tsodyks-Markram model. Times in ms; release is a fraction of one pool.
import math

def synapse(U, tau_rec, tau_fac, times):
    u, R, out = 0.0, 1.0, []
    last = times[0]
    for i, t in enumerate(times):
        if i > 0:                                   # decay between spikes
            gap = t - last
            u = U + (u - U) * math.exp(-gap / tau_fac)
            R = 1.0 - (1.0 - R) * math.exp(-gap / tau_rec)
        u = u + U * (1.0 - u)                       # facilitation on arrival
        released = u * R
        R -= released                               # deplete the pool
        out.append(round(released, 4))
        last = t
    return out

burst = [0, 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70]             # 100 Hz cortical burst
ct     = synapse(U=0.09, tau_rec=138.0, tau_fac=670.0, times=burst)
driver = synapse(U=0.50, tau_rec=671.0, tau_fac=17.0, times=burst)

print("L6 CT modulator (facilitating):", ct)
print("driver synapse  (depressing) :", driver)
print("CT last over first     =", round(ct[-1] / ct[0], 2))
print("driver last over first =", round(driver[-1] / driver[0], 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small, slow, near-silent pyramid in the deepest cortical layer whose apical dendrite stops short of layer 1, which outnumbers the thalamus ten to one on its way back down, and whose synapses get stronger the longer it talks.

CHAPTER 7. L6 IT (Layer 6 Intra-Telencephalic) (Deep-Layer Courier Neuron) (COG)

Deep at the floor of the cortex sits a cell that spends its life whispering back to the areas that just spoke to it.

Deep-Layer names its location and pathway: it carries messages from the deep layer out to other cortical areas without descending to the thalamus, which is what separates it from its neighbour in Chapter 6.

Courier was chosen because a courier is defined by carrying rather than by the contents of the parcel - the right level of commitment for the least characterised cell in the family.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

L6 IT means layer six intratelencephalic: a neuron in the deepest sheet of the cortex whose output never leaves the two cerebral hemispheres and the striatum inside them.

It carries feedback from higher areas back down to earlier ones, and layer six corticocortical cells are a major route for feedback both within and between hemispheres.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

One thick apical dendrite, an input branch, climbs from the pyramid-shaped cell body toward the brain surface and often reaches layer four, while basal dendrites fan sideways, every branch studded with spines that each receive one excitatory synapse.

SIZE:

The cell body is 10 to 20 micrometres across, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre, and the dendritic tree spans 200 to 400 micrometres sideways by 500 to 1200 vertically.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Upright versus inverted trunks, short versus tall trunks, and one-hemisphere versus callosal cells crossing the corpus callosum, the bridge of some 200 million axons joining left and right.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer six a, the upper and thicker part of the deepest cortical layer, everywhere in the neocortex of both hemispheres, from primary visual and somatosensory cortex to every frontal association area.

It straddles the layer six a to six b boundary below and, with its apical dendrite, the layer six to layer five boundary above.

NUMBER:

Layer six holds about a fifth of neocortical neurons and intratelencephalic cells are its larger excitatory family, giving 800 million to 1.4 billion cells, near 1.0 billion centrally, about 6 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons counted by Azevedo and colleagues in 2009.

Confidence is low to moderate, since the intratelencephalic share of human layer six is inferred from gene expression rather than counted.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest near minus 72 millivolts, threshold minus 45, input resistance 100 to 250 megaohms, a slow 15 to 25 millisecond membrane time constant, a modest sag from the current called I h, and strong spike frequency adaptation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from local layer six and five cells, weak thalamic fibres, long-range descending cortical axons and neuromodulatory fibres, while its 3000 to 10000 outputs reach distant cortical layers, the matching area across the callosum, and often the striatum.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One thin axon, 0.5 to 1.0 micrometres wide, sheds a dense local branch network in layers five and six, then dives into the white matter for tens of millimetres at a few metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the standard excitatory messenger, acting on fast AMPA receptors, named for alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid, and slow NMDA receptors, named for N-methyl-D-aspartate, while the cell itself carries acetylcholine, noradrenaline, serotonin, adenosine and orexin receptors.

MYELINATION:

Myelin, the fatty insulation made by oligodendrocyte cells, wraps the axon in the white matter but only patchily inside the cortex, with a g ratio, inner over outer fibre diameter, of 0.6 to 0.8.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and below layer five giant cells, since resting rates are often under 1 spike per second, with most energy spent by the sodium-potassium pump restoring ion gradients across a large dendritic surface.

PLASTICITY:

Ordinary long-term potentiation and depression, the lasting strengthening and weakening of synapses that underlies learning, plus lifelong spine turnover, and because these axons tell earlier areas what to expect they are a prime substrate for perceptual learning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes, the star-shaped housekeeping glia, are sparser here than in upper layers, while oligodendrocyte precursor cells crowd the white matter border just below where the cortex's cabling begins; microglia are unremarkable around this cell.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and an area still registers what its senses deliver but is never told what the rest of the cortex expects, so ambiguity stops being resolved by prior knowledge and the hemispheres stop stitching one scene together.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The apical trunk supports NMDA spikes, local regenerative events that let one branch compute alone, spikes travel backwards from the axon into the dendrites to gate plasticity, and dendrites make their own proteins.

Adenosine accumulating with time awake suppresses these cells presynaptically, so sleep pressure dials down cortical feedback, and glutamate escaping the synaptic cleft reaches astrocytes as slow volume transmission.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: local layer six glutamate, 0 to 40 hertz; layer five input, 0 to 20 hertz; thalamic feedforward, 0 to 50 hertz; long-range feedback, 0 to 30 hertz; acetylcholine and noradrenaline, 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to distant cortical layers one, two, three, five and six, the contralateral matching area, and striatum; 0 to 15 hertz, usually under 1 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage in millivolts; adaptation current in nanoamps; per-synapse depression factor 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 72 millivolts; threshold minus 45; reset minus 60; input resistance 150 megaohms; adaptation increment 0.035 nanoamps per spike; axonal delay 5 to 30 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 18 milliseconds; AMPA 2; NMDA 100; adaptation 180; depression recovery 400.

• FAILURE MODE: in schizophrenia, disturbed corticocortical and callosal wiring weakens the expectation signal, one proposed route to hallucination; in multiple sclerosis, demyelination delays feedback until it arrives after the response it was meant to shape.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a layer six intratelencephalic feedback neuron that integrates slowly and adapts strongly.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -72.
Create a number variable called adaptation current with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called outgoing messages with initial value empty list.
For each input sample in the input stream, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of adaptation current and 0.99945.
    Set adaptation current to that result.
    Calculate the difference of input sample and adaptation current.
    Calculate the product of that result and 150.
    Set a number variable called drive to that result.
    Calculate the difference of -72 and membrane voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that result and drive.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 180.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that result.
    Set membrane voltage to that result.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time to the end of outgoing messages.
        Set membrane voltage to -60.
        Calculate the sum of adaptation current and 0.035.
        Set adaptation current to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in outgoing messages to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: no libraries, runs anywhere, reads almost like the specification above.
DT, TAU_M, R_IN = 0.1, 18.0, 150.0             # ms, ms, megaohms
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -72.0, -45.0, -60.0    # millivolts
TAU_W, B_W = 180.0, 0.035                      # ms, nanoamps per spike

def run(current_na, duration_ms):
    """Adapting layer 6 IT cell. Returns spike times in milliseconds."""
    v, w, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, []
    for step in range(int(duration_ms / DT)):
        w += DT * (-w / TAU_W)             # adaptation decays back to zero
        drive = R_IN * (current_na - w)    # megaohms times nanoamps gives mV
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + drive) / TAU_M
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(round(step * DT, 1))
            v = V_RESET
            w += B_W                       # each spike makes the next harder
    return spikes

s = run(0.25, 1000.0)
gaps = [round(s[i + 1] - s[i], 1) for i in range(len(s) - 1)]
print("spikes:", len(s), " first gap:", gaps[0], " last gap:", gaps[-1])
# The last gap is far longer than the first: that is adaptation.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A deep cell with a tall apical trunk, strong adaptation, and an axon that dives into the white matter yet never turns toward the thalamus.

CHAPTER 8. L6 IT CAR3 (Layer 6 Intra-Telencephalic Carbonic Anhydrase 3) (Cortex-To-Cortex Linking Neuron) (COG) (PUC)

Somewhere in your left hemisphere a neuron is firing whose only purpose is to tell the matching spot in your right hemisphere what just happened - and the news will take about sixteen thousandths of a second to arrive.

Cortex-To-Cortex names the pathway: its long axon crosses the corpus callosum to connect distant cortical areas across the hemispheric divide.

Linking describes its functional role: it synchronizes information processing between bilaterally paired cortical networks, binding two distinct halves into a unified perceptual map.

Neuron marks it as a primary excitatory projection neuron bridging the neocortical architecture.


WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a Previously Unnamed Construct: the dedicated long-range linking pyramidal neuron whose one job is to keep two distant patches of cortex in register.

A pyramidal neuron is the cortex's standard excitatory cell, shaped like a pyramid with one thick dendrite pointing at the surface.

Most pyramidal neurons talk to their neighbours; this one exists to talk across the brain.

Its best-documented members are the callosal projection neurons, whose axons cross the corpus callosum, the bridge of about 190 million fibres joining the hemispheres.

They belong to the intratelencephalic class, meaning their axons stay within the cortex and never descend to brainstem or spinal cord.

Their function is unification: they make two half-maps of the body, the world and the self behave as one map.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A classic pyramid, usually with its cell body in layer 3, with about 20 percent of the population deeper, mainly in layer 5.

One thick apical dendrite runs up to layer 1 and opens into a tuft, which is exactly where the two previous cell types in this section deliver their signals.

A skirt of basal dendrites spreads sideways at the soma.

The axon leaves the base, gives local collaterals in its own column, then commits to a long course, typically crossing the midline and also branching to areas in its own hemisphere.

Single-cell reconstructions show these are not simple point-to-point cables: one neuron often innervates several distant targets with different branch patterns.

SIZE:

The soma is about 15 to 25 micrometres across, larger in areas that project further.

Total dendritic length runs to roughly 10 to 20 millimetres per cell.

Spine counts are the headline number: layer 2 and 3 pyramidal neurons in human prefrontal cortex carry about 15,138 spines on their basal dendrites alone, against about 12,700 in temporal cortex, and prefrontal cells have up to sixteen times more spines than cells in primary visual cortex.

The axon is the extreme dimension, commonly 100 to 250 millimetres of total length once the crossing and both hemispheres' branches are counted.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Homotopic linkers project to the mirror-image point in the other hemisphere, and they dominate.

Heterotopic linkers project to a different area on the other side.

Dual linkers send one branch across the callosum and another to a distant area on their own side, so the same spike lands in two places at two different times.

A further split is by conduction speed, because callosal axon diameters vary and so do the delays.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in layer 3, chiefly its deeper sublayers 3b and 3c, and in layer 5a, across essentially all neocortical areas.

Densities are highest where function is bilateral: primary and secondary somatosensory cortex near the midline representation of trunk and face, auditory cortex, premotor and supplementary motor areas, and prefrontal cortex.

Density is low in the parts of primary visual cortex representing the far periphery of the visual field.

The axons traverse the corpus callosum through its named parts, the rostrum, genu, body, isthmus and splenium.

Two interfaces are critical: the layer 3 to layer 1 interface, where the apical tuft collects feedback, and the callosal midline, which every axon of this type must cross.

NUMBER:

My best estimate is 200 million across both hemispheres, defensible range 180 to 220 million, and confidence is moderately high because it is anchored to a direct anatomical count.

The corpus callosum contains about 190 million axons in the adult human, and each such neuron contributes one axon, so the counts should match closely.

That is about 1.2 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 1.5 percent of the roughly 13.1 billion excitatory ones.

It is about 0.23 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Note the boundary I draw: I count only neurons whose principal axon crosses the midline, not the far larger population of purely local or purely ipsilateral pyramidal neurons that other parts of this section count.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A regular-spiking cell, whose train of action potentials slows over a few hundred milliseconds, an effect called spike-frequency adaptation.

Membrane capacitance is around 200 picofarads and input resistance around 100 megaohms, giving a membrane time constant near 12 to 20 milliseconds.

Resting potential is about minus 70 millivolts, threshold about minus 50, and reset after a spike about minus 58.

Human layer 3 pyramidal neurons have a faster dendritic membrane and greater electrical separation between tuft and soma than rodent cells, so the tuft can compute somewhat on its own.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Basal dendrites receive local layer 3 and layer 4 input carrying the area's own sensory or motor content.

The apical tuft in layer 1 receives thalamic matrix input, cortico-cortical feedback, and the two interneurons described earlier in this section.

Inhibition arrives at the soma from parvalbumin-expressing basket cells and at the tuft from somatostatin-expressing Martinotti cells.

Outputs land mainly on basal and proximal apical dendrites of pyramidal neurons in layers 2, 3 and 5 of the target area, with roughly 10 to 20 percent of terminals contacting inhibitory interneurons, which keeps the interhemispheric link from turning into runaway excitation.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Most callosal axons are thin, under 1 micrometre across, in every primate studied.

Measured conduction velocities range from 2.8 to 22.5 metres per second with a median near 7.4.

Delay from soma to callosal midline is roughly 4.4 to 4.8 milliseconds, and full interhemispheric transfer time averages about 11.7 milliseconds with a spread of about 2.9.

A small minority of thick fast axons deliver the copy in under 10 milliseconds, and those few fibres are what any task needing tight bilateral timing must depend on.

Position within the callosum predicts destination, so an axon's route through the bridge is not random.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate at every terminal, acting on fast AMPA receptors, named for alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid, and on slower NMDA receptors, named for N-methyl-D-aspartate, which pass current only when the target is already partly depolarised.

That NMDA dependence is the key to the function: a message from the other hemisphere counts for far more if the receiving cortex is already active on the same subject.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated along the callosal course, thinly or not at all near the terminals.

Myelin is the fatty insulation wrapped by oligodendrocytes, and it is what makes 7 metres per second possible in a fibre well under a micrometre thick.

Myelination of these axons continues into the third decade of life, later than almost anywhere else in the brain, which is one reason interhemispheric coordination keeps improving into adulthood.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, and dominated by the axon rather than the soma.

Holding sodium and potassium gradients along 100 to 250 millimetres of axon, and supplying thousands of distant terminals with transmitter and mitochondria, makes this among the most expensive cortical cell types per spike.

Axonal transport at a few hundred millimetres per day means a protein made in the soma can take a day to reach the far terminals.

PLASTICITY:

Callosal synapses show classical long-term potentiation and depression, and the projection is famously activity-dependent in development: block activity in one hemisphere and the callosal map from the other fails to refine.

Myelin thickness along these axons is adjusted by activity, so learning changes not only synaptic weights but conduction delays, which for a cell defined by timing is a form of plasticity most models omit.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised glia here are oligodendrocytes, doing something they do nowhere else at this scale: holding tens of millions of near-parallel axons in tight timing agreement across 100 millimetres.

One oligodendrocyte myelinates up to about 50 segments, and in the callosum those segments are shared among neighbouring axons, so a single glial cell's health sets the delay of many fibres at once.

Oligodendrocytes also feed the axon directly, passing lactate through monocarboxylate transporters, which is essential because the axon is far from its cell body and cannot supply itself.

Callosal astrocytes are fibrous rather than protoplasmic, with long straight processes aligned to the fibre bundles, and microglia here are unusually numerous, patrolling white matter for damaged myelin.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and each hemisphere still works, but it works alone.

The cortex would lose its sense that the left hand and the right hand belong to the same person, and the seam down the middle of the visual field, the body map and the auditory field would stop being stitched.

Bilateral acts such as doing up a button would fragment, and each half of the brain would build its own version of an event with no way to reconcile them.

The flavour lost is unity: every local computation would survive, and the guarantee that its answer matches its twin's would not.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The apical tuft in layer 1 runs its own calcium-based computation: if tuft and soma are active together the cell fires a burst instead of single spikes, so it signals coincidence between local content and long-range context.

Action potentials travel backwards from the soma into the dendrites, which is how a dendrite learns that the cell fired and which synapses to strengthen.

Local protein synthesis in the dendrites lets a tagged synapse be rebuilt on the spot without waiting for the soma.

The axon instructs its own myelination, releasing glutamate along its length to tell oligodendrocyte precursors where to wrap, so the cell partly sets its own conduction delay.

It sends messengers backwards across its synapses, including endocannabinoids that turn down incoming inhibition.

Its activity drives local blood flow through neighbouring astrocytes, and it is metabolically coupled to them through the lactate shuttle.

Axonal boutons are added and removed over days, so the map itself is a moving structure.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic drive on basal dendrites from local layers 3 and 4, 0 to 600 picoamps; glutamatergic drive on the apical tuft from thalamic matrix and feedback axons, 0 to 300 picoamps; perisomatic inhibition from parvalbumin basket cells, GABA type A conductance 0 to 20 nanosiemens; tuft inhibition from the layer 1 interneurons described above, including the slow GABA type B brake.

• OUTPUTS: glutamatergic spikes delivered to the homotopic target area in the opposite hemisphere and to one or more ipsilateral areas, each on its own delay line; a bursting mode signalling tuft and soma coincidence; an endocannabinoid retrograde signal from 0 to 1.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation current; separate tuft voltage and calcium; a per-branch axonal delay queue holding at least 250 milliseconds of spike history; a short-term depression variable at each output.

• PARAMETERS: capacitance about 200 picofarads; leak conductance about 10 nanosiemens; resting potential minus 70 millivolts; soft threshold minus 50; reset minus 58; spike-triggered adaptation about 60 picoamps; callosal path length 80 to 160 millimetres; conduction velocity 2.8 to 22.5 metres per second, median 7.4.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 12 to 20 milliseconds; adaptation 200 to 400 milliseconds; callosal delay 4 to 30 milliseconds; short-term depression recovery 200 to 800 milliseconds; myelin remodelling, days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: complete failure of the projection is agenesis of the corpus callosum, present in roughly 1 in 4,000 live births, and up to a third of affected people meet the diagnostic criteria for autism; partial agenesis and thinning, especially of the splenium, are reported in idiopathic autism; demyelination in multiple sclerosis leaves the neurons alive but scrambles their delays, which is functionally as damaging as losing them; surgical section for epilepsy produces the split-brain syndrome, in which each hemisphere can report only what it saw.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a cortex to cortex linking neuron and deliver each of its spikes to a distant patch of cortex only after the correct travel time has elapsed.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative seventy.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value zero.
Create a list variable called axon queue with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called arrivals with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called step counter with initial value zero.
Repeat the following steps exactly one hundred and sixty two times.
    Add zero to the end of axon queue.
End the loop.
While step counter is less than ten thousand, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called input current with initial value three hundred.
    Create a number variable called leak with initial value zero.
    Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and negative seventy.
    Set leak to the product of ten and that difference.
    Calculate the difference of input current and leak.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of the difference of that result and adaptation divided by two thousand.
    Set adaptation to the product of adaptation and nine hundred and ninety nine thousandths.
    Create a number variable called launched with initial value zero.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative fifty.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to negative fifty eight.
        Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and sixty.
        Set launched to one.
    End the conditional block.
    Add launched to the end of axon queue.
    Access the item at position one in axon queue.
    Set arrivals to the sum of arrivals and that item.
    Remove the item at position one from axon queue.
    Set step counter to the sum of step counter and one.
End the loop.
Display arrivals to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a whole-cortex model must walk hundreds of millions of axonal delay slots every tenth of a millisecond, and a fixed ring buffer with no memory allocation is the only honest way to do that at scale.

/* Cortex-to-cortex linking neuron: adaptive exponential soma plus delay lines. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define DT 0.1          /* ms per step                    */
#define RING 4096       /* 409.6 ms of axonal history     */
#define CM 200.0        /* capacitance, pF                */
#define GL 10.0         /* leak conductance, nS           */
#define EL (-70.0)      /* leak reversal, mV              */
#define VT (-50.0)      /* soft threshold, mV             */
#define DELTA 2.0       /* threshold sharpness, mV        */
#define A 2.0           /* subthreshold adaptation, nS    */
#define TAUW 300.0      /* adaptation time constant, ms   */
#define B 60.0          /* spike-triggered adaptation, pA */
#define VPEAK 0.0       /* spike detected here, mV        */
#define VRESET (-58.0)  /* reset, mV                      */
typedef struct { double v, w, buf[RING]; int head, delay; } Link;
static void init(Link *L, double path_mm, double vel_m_s) {
    int i; L->v = EL; L->w = 0.0; L->head = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < RING; i++) L->buf[i] = 0.0;
    L->delay = (int)(path_mm / vel_m_s / DT + 0.5);   /* mm / (m/s) = ms */
    if (L->delay >= RING) L->delay = RING - 1;
}
/* fired says a spike left the soma now; the return value says one launched
   earlier has just ARRIVED at the far patch of cortex. */
static int step(Link *L, double i_pA, int *fired) {
    int arrived, read;
    double dv = (-GL * (L->v - EL) + GL * DELTA * exp((L->v - VT) / DELTA)
                 - L->w + i_pA) / CM;
    *fired = 0;
    L->v += DT * dv;
    if (L->v > VPEAK) L->v = VPEAK;                   /* clamp the exponential */
    L->w += DT * (A * (L->v - EL) - L->w) / TAUW;
    if (L->v >= VPEAK) { L->v = VRESET; L->w += B; *fired = 1; }
    L->buf[L->head] = *fired;
    read = (L->head - L->delay + RING) % RING;
    arrived = (int)L->buf[read];
    L->head = (L->head + 1) % RING;
    return arrived;
}
int main(void) {
    Link callosal, ipsi; int k, f1, f2, sent = 0, got_c = 0, got_i = 0;
    init(&callosal, 120.0, 7.4);      /* 120 mm across the callosum at 7.4 m/s */
    init(&ipsi, 20.0, 4.0);           /* 20 mm to a neighbour area at 4 m/s    */
    for (k = 0; k < 10000; k++) {     /* one second at 0.1 ms resolution */
        double drive = (k * DT > 100.0 && k * DT < 900.0) ? 300.0 : 0.0;
        got_c += step(&callosal, drive, &f1);
        got_i += step(&ipsi, drive, &f2);
        sent += f1;
    }
    printf("sent %d spikes; callosal delay %.1f ms (%d arrived), ipsi %.1f ms (%d)\n",
           sent, callosal.delay * DT, got_c, ipsi.delay * DT, got_i);
    return 0;
}

Compiled with gcc and run, it reports 6 spikes sent, a callosal delay of 16.2 milliseconds and an ipsilateral delay of 5.0 milliseconds, with all 6 arriving on both lines.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Inject a dye into one of these cells and the label turns up in the other hemisphere, at the mirror-image spot, having crossed a bridge of 190 million fibres.

No other cortical cell is defined by its destination in this way: a layer 3 pyramid with a thin myelinated axon under a micrometre, crossing the midline, arriving about 12 milliseconds later at the place thinking about the same thing.

CHAPTER 9. L6B (Layer 6 Sublayer B) (Wakefulness-Listening Neuron) (COG)

In a layer so thin it was long dismissed as a smudge, live the oldest neurons in your brain: cells born before the cortex existed, which stayed on after the scaffolding came down.

Wakefulness names the chemical signal it monitors: it is the only cortical cell type carrying receptors for orexin, the peptide whose loss causes narcolepsy.

Listening names its posture - it does not report on the world, it listens for whether the brain is awake at all.

Neuron marks it as a principal cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Layer six b is the deepest sliver of cortex, lying directly on the white matter, and its cells are survivors of the subplate, the temporary foetal structure that guides thalamic axons to their cortical addresses and then largely disappears.

Long assumed inert, they are now known to control brain state through the apical dendrites of the cells above and the higher-order thalamocortical system, and they are the cortex's private receiver for the arousal chemicals of the hypothalamus.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Pyramidal, inverted, spindle-shaped, horizontal and multipolar cells crowd a band a few bodies thick, many with long spiny dendrites running sideways along the white matter and some sending a slender apical dendrite all the way to layer one.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are a small 8 to 15 micrometres, horizontal dendritic spread reaches 400 to 600 micrometres, and the tallest cells span the whole 2 to 4 millimetre thickness of human cortex.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A large group expresses connective tissue growth factor, abbreviated CTGF and also called CCN2, and is densest in primary sensorimotor areas, while others carry NR4A2, SEMA3E or MGST1, and electrically the layer splits into slow bursting excitatory cells and a separate fast-spiking class.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer six b of the neocortex in both hemispheres, plus the deepest layer of allocortex such as entorhinal cortex, where these cells send a direct excitatory projection into the hippocampus that supports spatial coding and memory.

The defining interface is the grey to white matter border itself, and related interstitial neurons lie scattered fully inside the white matter below.

NUMBER:

Layer six b is thin and sparsely populated, giving an estimated 50 million to 200 million neurons in both hemispheres, near 100 million centrally, roughly 0.6 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons.

Confidence is low, because the human layer six b border is hard to draw in stained tissue and the uncounted interstitial neurons below belong to the same family.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A prominent excitatory subgroup bursts rhythmically with no input at all, fires with a delay to depolarising steps, and at stronger steps shows spike inactivation followed by a long depolarised plateau and a depolarising afterpotential, alongside mild adaptation and strong hyperpolarisation-activated rectification indicating the current I h.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They receive higher-order thalamic, corticocortical, hypothalamic and brainstem input, and they excite both pyramidal cells and interneurons above, crucially contacting the apical dendrites of upper-layer pyramidal cells, the exact place where feedback and context arrive.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin axons run horizontally along the white matter border for long distances before turning up into the cortex, some entering the white matter to reach distant areas or the hippocampus, conducting slowly at 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for the excitatory majority and gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA and the main inhibitory messenger, for the fast-spiking minority.

What sets them apart is what they hear: orexin, also called hypocretin, the wake-promoting peptide of the hypothalamus, strongly depolarises them through orexin receptor type 2, which is expressed in layer six b excitatory neurons and essentially nowhere else in the neocortex, and they also respond powerfully, sometimes exclusively, to neurotensin and cholecystokinin.

MYELINATION:

They sit in the most heavily myelinated neighbourhood of the cortex yet their own thin axons are lightly wrapped at best inside the grey matter, acquiring a normal thin sheath only where they travel through white matter.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low in absolute terms because the cells are small and slow, although the spontaneously bursting subgroup carries a continuous never-resting load, which is unusual for a cortical excitatory cell.

PLASTICITY:

Their greatest act of plasticity was prenatal and permanent, deciding where thalamic axons would land and thereby shaping the whole cortical map, while in adulthood their excitability swings modulatorily across the sleep and wake cycle.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Their glial environment is genuinely unusual because of their address: they are surrounded by the myelin-making machinery of the white matter border, with high oligodendrocyte and precursor density and fibrous rather than bushy astrocytes, unlike any cell in the upper layers.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove layer six b and the cortex loses its volume knob for state, since orexin has almost no other cortical receiver, so pyramidal apical dendrites would stop being pre-set for the animal's behavioural state and the transition between attentive and internally generated activity would blur.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their long plateau potentials are an analogue memory lasting hundreds of milliseconds, and they work largely by peptide reception through volume transmission, answering chemicals released into the tissue at large rather than into a synapse.

Because their output targets other cells' apical dendrites their computation is really the modulation of somebody else's dendritic computation, and CTGF itself is a secreted signalling protein, a hint that these cells remain builders as well as signallers.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: higher-order thalamic glutamate, 0 to 30 hertz; corticocortical input, 0 to 20 hertz; orexin, 0 to 1, high in waking and near zero in slow wave sleep; neurotensin and cholecystokinin, 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto apical dendrites of layer two, three and five pyramidal cells, onto local interneurons and back to higher-order thalamus; 0 to 20 hertz in bursts of 2 to 6 spikes.

• STATE: membrane voltage; slow recovery variable; plateau timer in milliseconds; orexin receptor occupancy 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 70 millivolts; burst threshold drive 4 model units; orexin gain 6 units per unit occupancy; plateau 100 to 400 milliseconds; interburst period 0.5 to 3 seconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 35 milliseconds; recovery about 50; plateau 100 to 400; peptide action 1 to 30 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in the DISC1 mutant mouse model of schizophrenia, CTGF-positive layer six b density rises by about 21.5 percent alongside white matter changes, and in narcolepsy, where orexin neurons die, the cortical arm of the disease runs through this cell, the cortex's main orexin target.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a layer six b neuron whose slow rhythmic bursting is switched on by the arousal peptide orexin.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called recovery with initial value -14.
Create a number variable called orexin level with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called burst spikes with initial value empty list.
For each millisecond in the simulation, perform the following operations.
    Check if the current millisecond is greater than 1000.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set orexin level to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of orexin level and 6.
    Calculate the sum of 1 and that result.
    Set a number variable called drive to that result.
    Repeat the following steps exactly 2 times.
        Calculate the product of 0.04 and the product of membrane voltage and membrane voltage.
        Calculate the sum of that result and the product of 5 and membrane voltage.
        Calculate the sum of that result and 140.
        Calculate the difference of that result and recovery.
        Calculate the sum of that result and drive.
        Calculate the product of that result and 0.5.
        Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that result.
        Set membrane voltage to that result.
    End the loop.
    Calculate the product of 0.2 and membrane voltage.
    Calculate the difference of that result and recovery.
    Calculate the product of that result and 0.02.
    Calculate the sum of recovery and that result.
    Set recovery to that result.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to 30.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current millisecond to the end of burst spikes.
        Set membrane voltage to -55.
        Calculate the sum of recovery and 4.
        Set recovery to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in burst spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python with the Izhikevich two variable model: it reproduces bursting and
# plateaus in four lines of arithmetic, which matters when millions of these
# cells must run inside a whole cortex simulation.
A, B, C, D = 0.02, 0.2, -55.0, 4.0   # intrinsically bursting parameter set
BASE_I, OREXIN_GAIN = 1.0, 6.0       # base drive sits below the firing threshold of 4

def run_l6b(duration_ms, orexin_on_at):
    """Silent until orexin arrives, then bursts. Returns spike times in ms."""
    v, u, spikes = -70.0, B * -70.0, []
    for t in range(duration_ms):
        drive = BASE_I + OREXIN_GAIN * (1.0 if t >= orexin_on_at else 0.0)
        for _ in range(2):            # two half ms steps keep this stable
            v += 0.5 * (0.04 * v * v + 5.0 * v + 140.0 - u + drive)
        u += A * (B * v - u)
        if v >= 30.0:                 # spike peak detected
            spikes.append(t)
            v, u = C, u + D           # reset voltage and recovery
    return spikes

s = run_l6b(3000, 1000)
print("spikes before orexin:", len([t for t in s if t < 1000]))
print("spikes after orexin :", len([t for t in s if t >= 1000]))
# Nothing, then bursting: the cortex has just been told the animal is awake.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A tiny, oddly oriented neuron sitting directly on the white matter, expressing CTGF or NR4A2, born before every other cortical cell, and depolarised by orexin through a receptor no other neocortical neuron carries.

CHAPTER 10. PYRAMIDAL CELLS (COG)

Draw a triangle, add a mast on top and roots below, and you have drawn the cell that does most of the thinking in the human brain: more than ten billion of them, carrying every conscious thought you have ever had.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pyramidal cell is the workhorse excitatory neuron of the cortex, named for the rough triangle of its cell body, and it gathers thousands of small inputs, decides whether they amount to anything, and sends the answer somewhere else, often centimetres away.

It is an umbrella category: L6 IT, L6 IT CAR3, layer six b and star pyramid cells are all pyramidal or pyramid-like, so those entries are carved out of this one and their numbers must never be added to it.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Three features define it: a triangular cell body with one thick apical dendrite rising to the surface and usually ending in a spray called the apical tuft in layer one, a skirt of basal dendrites below, and a single axon leaving the base, with all dendrites more densely spined in humans than in any rodent or other primate examined.

SIZE:

Cell bodies run from about 10 micrometres in layer two to 50, and up to 100 in the giant Betz cells of motor cortex, while a human layer five apical dendrite can be 2 millimetres long, which is why the top and bottom of one cell behave almost like two cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

By layer, from layer two and three association cells to layer six corticothalamic cells; by projection, callosal, corticostriatal, corticothalamic, corticospinal and corticopontine; by firing, regular spiking, intrinsically bursting and chattering; and by region, including Betz cells and the von Economo neurons of anterior cingulate and frontoinsular cortex.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Every neocortical layer except layer one, which holds few cell bodies, plus hippocampal fields CA1, CA2 and CA3, the subiculum, amygdala, entorhinal cortex, piriform cortex and claustrum.

The interface that matters is internal: a layer five cell's apical trunk crosses every layer to reach layer one, bridging the deep output zone, the thalamic input zone of layer four and the feedback zone of layer one.

NUMBER:

Roughly 70 to 80 percent of neocortical neurons are excitatory and the overwhelming majority of those are pyramidal, giving about 11.4 billion to 13.1 billion cells out of the 16.34 billion counted by Azevedo and colleagues in 2009, plus a few hundred million more in hippocampus and related structures.

Confidence is moderate to high for the excitatory fraction, which was directly counted, and moderate for the pyramidal share, since spiny stellate cells are excitatory but not pyramidal.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest near minus 70 millivolts, threshold near minus 50, a one millisecond spike, rates from under 0.1 to 50 hertz, mostly regular spiking with moderate adaptation, and in humans unusually leaky large dendrites that paradoxically help keep separate computations separate in different branches.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

A single cortical pyramidal cell receives roughly 5000 to 30000 excitatory synapses, almost all on spines, plus 10 to 20 percent as many inhibitory ones placed on the soma and axon initial segment, and it outputs to pyramidal cells, interneurons, striatum, thalamus, brainstem and, for the largest cells, spinal cord.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One axon per cell with a 20 to 60 micrometre initial segment where the spike is actually born, local collaterals within a millimetre or two, and a main trunk up to a metre long, with diameters from 0.2 to over 10 micrometres and speeds from under 1 to over 70 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate at every synapse without exception in the healthy adult cortex, often alongside brain-derived neurotrophic factor, the growth signal that keeps target cells healthy, while the cell carries AMPA, NMDA, kainate, GABA type A and B, acetylcholine, dopamine, serotonin, noradrenaline, adenosine and cannabinoid receptors.

MYELINATION:

Long-range axons are myelinated and local collaterals largely bare, and the sheath along a cortical axon is deliberately patchy, wrapped stretches alternating with naked ones, a pattern tuned to set precise conduction delays rather than to maximise speed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high in aggregate, since pyramidal synapses consume most of the brain's energy while the brain uses about 20 percent of the body's resting energy at 2 percent of its mass, most of it spent reversing synaptic ion flows rather than firing spikes.

PLASTICITY:

They are the canonical site of long-term potentiation and depression, their spines appear, enlarge, shrink and vanish over hours to days, learning a motor skill measurably grows new spines, and homeostatic plasticity rescales all of a cell's synapses to keep its average rate in range.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

One human protoplasmic astrocyte enwraps hundreds of thousands to millions of mostly pyramidal synapses, recycling glutamate, supplying lactate and buffering potassium, and human astrocytes are far larger and more complex than rodent ones, a specialisation built around this cell; microglia physically eat the spines they prune.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove pyramidal cells and there is no cortex left, only inhibitory neurons and glia with nothing to inhibit, because cortical inhibitory cells are almost entirely local, so every long-range signal out of the cortex would vanish and the organ would become a silent island.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Human layer two and three pyramidal cells generate calcium-mediated dendritic action potentials that are graded rather than all-or-none, largest at threshold and damped by stronger input, letting a single dendrite compute the exclusive-or function, long thought to need a network with a hidden layer.

Layer five cells perform backpropagation-activated calcium firing, where a spike travelling back from the axon coincides with tuft input and triggers a plateau that turns one spike into a burst, and they also make proteins locally, send endocannabinoids backwards across the synapse, and release signals that dilate capillaries, the physical basis of functional brain imaging.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: 5000 to 30000 basal and proximal excitatory synapses, each 0 to 50 hertz; distal tuft synapses from feedback and higher-order thalamus; 500 to 5000 inhibitory synapses; neuromodulator levels 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate at 1000 to 10000 boutons onto local pyramidal cells, interneurons, distant cortex, striatum, thalamus, brainstem and spinal cord; 0 to 50 hertz, with burst versus single spike as a second channel.

• STATE: somatic voltage; dendritic voltage; dendritic calcium; adaptation current; per-synapse weight and depression factor.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 70 millivolts, threshold minus 50, reset minus 70; input resistance 40 to 120 megaohms; tuft to soma coupling 0.05 to 0.2; plateau current 0.5 to 1.0 nanoamps; coincidence window 5 to 15 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: somatic membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; dendritic 25 to 50; AMPA 2; NMDA 100; calcium plateau 30 to 50; adaptation 100 to 300.

• FAILURE MODE: in schizophrenia, spine density on deep layer three prefrontal pyramidal cells falls by about 23 percent while layer five and six cells are spared; in Alzheimer disease, spine loss tracks cognitive decline more closely than plaques do; in epilepsy, failed inhibition lets these cells recruit each other into a seizure.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a pyramidal cell that turns a single spike into a burst when feedback reaches its distal dendrite just after the cell has fired.
Create a number variable called soma voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called time of last spike with initial value -1000.
Create a number variable called plateau end time with initial value -1000.
Create a list variable called output spikes with initial value empty list.
For each time point in the simulation, perform the following operations.
    Create a number variable called plateau current with initial value 0.
    Check if the current time point is less than plateau end time.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau current to 0.7.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of basal input current and plateau current.
    Calculate the product of that result and 80.
    Set a number variable called drive to that result.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and soma voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that result and drive.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 120.
    Calculate the sum of soma voltage and that result.
    Set soma voltage to that result.
    Check if soma voltage is greater than or equal to -50.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time point to the end of output spikes.
        Set soma voltage to -70.
        Set time of last spike to the current time point.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of the current time point and time of last spike.
    Set a number variable called since spike to that result.
    Check if both tuft input is greater than 0.5 and since spike is less than 10 are true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of the current time point and 40.
        Set plateau end time to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in output spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: this two compartment coincidence detector is the model other groups
# reuse most, and plain Python drops into any simulator with no build step.
DT, TAU, R_IN = 0.1, 12.0, 80.0                  # ms, ms, megaohms
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -70.0, -50.0, -70.0      # millivolts
BAP_WINDOW, CA_DUR, CA_AMP = 10.0, 40.0, 0.7     # ms, ms, nanoamps

def run(basal_na, tuft_drive, duration_ms):
    """Basal drive alone gives single spikes. Basal plus tuft gives bursts."""
    v, last_spike, plateau_until, spikes = V_REST, -1e9, -1e9, []
    for step in range(int(duration_ms / DT)):
        t = step * DT
        i_ca = CA_AMP if t < plateau_until else 0.0
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + R_IN * (basal_na + i_ca)) / TAU
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(round(t, 1))
            v, last_spike = V_RESET, t     # this spike backpropagates
        # tuft input just after a backpropagating spike opens a calcium plateau
        if tuft_drive > 0.5 and (t - last_spike) < BAP_WINDOW and t >= plateau_until:
            plateau_until = t + CA_DUR
    return spikes

print("tuft only      :", len(run(0.00, 1.0, 500.0)), "spikes")
print("basal only     :", len(run(0.28, 0.0, 500.0)), "spikes")
print("basal plus tuft:", len(run(0.28, 1.0, 500.0)), "spikes")
# Feedback alone is silent, feedforward alone ticks, the two together burst.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A triangular cell body, one thick apical dendrite pointing at the surface, a skirt of basal dendrites, spines everywhere, and a single axon leaving the base for the white matter.

CHAPTER 11. SPINY STELLATE CELLS (COG)

Take a pyramidal cell, snap off its mast, and let the remaining branches bristle out in every direction like a sea urchin: you have made the cell that catches the world as it arrives from the senses.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Spiny stellate cells are excitatory neurons of layer four, the middle cortical layer where signals from the thalamus first land, the thalamus being the egg-shaped relay in the centre of the brain through which nearly all sensory information passes.

Their job is amplification: a thalamic axon delivers a weak, sparse message, and the layer four population multiplies it and hands the result up to layers two and three, never reporting outside the local cortical column.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

No apical dendrite, which is the defining negative feature; instead five to eight primary dendrites radiate in a rough sphere confined to layer four, and in rodent whisker cortex the tree leans toward the centre of its barrel, an asymmetry built after birth under instruction from the thalamic input itself.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are a small 8 to 15 micrometres, which is why layer four looks grainy and is called the granular layer, while dendritic fields in upper layer 4C of monkey visual cortex are 100 micrometres or more across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:
