In primate primary visual cortex, cells of layer 4C alpha receive the magnocellular stream carrying fast coarse colour-blind motion information while those of layer 4C beta receive the parvocellular stream carrying slow fine colour-sensitive detail, and spiny stellate neurons also occupy layers 4A and 4B.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer four, and nowhere else, of primary sensory cortex: layers 4A, 4B, 4C alpha and 4C beta of primary visual cortex, also called V1, in the occipital lobe; layer four of primary somatosensory cortex in the postcentral gyrus; and layer four of primary auditory cortex.

They are rare or absent in agranular cortex such as primary motor cortex, and the interface that matters is the layer four to layer three boundary, across which the amplified signal is passed.

NUMBER:

Human primary visual cortex holds about 140 million neurons per hemisphere, so roughly 280 million in both, and adding layer four of somatosensory and auditory cortex gives an estimated 150 million to 400 million spiny stellate cells, near 250 million centrally, about 1.5 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons.

Confidence is low to moderate, because the stellate share of human layer four has never been counted directly and human layer four holds proportionally more pyramidal cells than rodent layer four does.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Regular spiking with moderate adaptation, resting near minus 70 millivolts and firing near minus 45, with a short 8 to 12 millisecond membrane time constant that makes the cell a coincidence detector rather than a slow integrator, and both high-conductance and low-conductance NMDA receptors.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Thalamic synapses are the famous input but a minority, estimated at 18 to 188 per neuron in primate visual cortex, the rest coming from other layer four spiny cells, which is why the layer amplifies; over 90 percent of its own contacts land on spines and shafts of excitatory neurons in layers four and two and three.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon stays local, arborising densely inside its own barrel or column and supplying output to virtually all layers of that column while projecting most strongly to layers two and three, and almost never entering the white matter.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, received through thalamic synapses that depress strongly, so the second and third pulse of a rapid train produce far smaller responses than the first, making the cell a detector of change rather than of steady input.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none, because the axon never leaves the local grey matter, making this one of the few cortical excitatory cells for which myelination is honestly negligible, even though the thalamic axons arriving on it are heavily myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High per unit volume, since layer four of primary sensory cortex has the densest synapses and the strongest cytochrome oxidase staining of any cortical layer, that mitochondrial enzyme being used as a stain precisely because it marks metabolic intensity.

PLASTICITY:

Their dendritic asymmetry is sculpted by thalamic input during a critical period in the first days and weeks of life, and depriving an animal of retinal input then permanently changes layer four dendritic shape, after which layer four becomes among the least plastic parts of the cortex.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Layer four astrocytes face the heaviest glutamate load in the cortex and carry correspondingly high levels of the glutamate transporters EAAT1 and EAAT2, the layer has the densest capillary network in the cortex, and in rodents astrocyte processes help define barrel boundaries.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove spiny stellate cells and primary sensory cortex loses its amplifier: the thalamus still delivers its messages, but as a whisper too faint to drive layers two and three, and fine detail is lost first, because fine detail depends on weak sparse drive being multiplied by local recurrence.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their signature computation is recurrent amplification, a network property no single-cell model captures, since the layer's output depends on loop gain across thousands of these cells and slightly higher gain would make the layer seize.

Their two classes of NMDA receptor give dendritic branches supralinear summation, their depressing thalamic synapses make them compute the derivative of their input, and their tight coupling to local blood flow makes them a principal generator of the functional brain imaging signal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: 18 to 188 thalamocortical synapses, each 0 to 100 hertz with strong depression; 1000 to 3000 recurrent layer four synapses at 0 to 20 hertz; inhibition from a small fast-spiking pool arriving 1 to 2 milliseconds later.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto layer two and three pyramidal cells, other layer four spiny cells, and weakly onto layers five and six of the same column; 0 to 40 hertz, sparse at rest.

• STATE: membrane voltage; excitatory conductance; inhibitory conductance; per-synapse depression resource 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 70 millivolts; threshold minus 45; reset minus 60; excitatory reversal 0; inhibitory reversal minus 80; depression factor per pulse 0.75; inhibition delay 1.5 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 9 milliseconds; excitatory conductance 1.5; inhibitory conductance 6; depression recovery 300 to 500; critical period days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: if feedforward inhibition fails the input window widens and the recurrent loop runs away, the classic route into a focal sensory seizure; if critical period input is missing, as in congenital cataract, the dendritic geometry is set wrong for life.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a layer four spiny stellate cell that answers the first volley of a thalamic burst and then falls silent as its synapses depress and inhibition arrives.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called excitatory conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inhibitory conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synaptic resource with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called output spikes with initial value empty list.
For each time step in the simulation, perform the following operations.
    Check if a thalamic volley arrives at this time step.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of 5 and synaptic resource.
        Calculate the sum of excitatory conductance and that result.
        Set excitatory conductance to that result.
        Calculate the product of synaptic resource and 0.75.
        Set synaptic resource to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if a thalamic volley arrived 1.5 milliseconds ago.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of inhibitory conductance and 6.
        Set inhibitory conductance to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the quotient of excitatory conductance divided by 15.
    Calculate the difference of excitatory conductance and that result.
    Set excitatory conductance to that result.
    Calculate the quotient of inhibitory conductance divided by 60.
    Calculate the difference of inhibitory conductance and that result.
    Set inhibitory conductance to that result.
    Calculate the product of excitatory conductance and the difference of 0 and membrane voltage.
    Set a number variable called excitatory drive to that result.
    Calculate the product of inhibitory conductance and the difference of -80 and membrane voltage.
    Set a number variable called inhibitory drive to that result.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and membrane voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that result and excitatory drive.
    Calculate the sum of that result and inhibitory drive.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 90.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that result.
    Set membrane voltage to that result.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time step to the end of output spikes.
        Set membrane voltage to -60.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in output spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: sensory cortex needs millions of these cells at 0.1 ms resolution, and C
   gives the tightest inner loop. Build with: cc -std=c99 -o stel stel.c      */
#include <stdio.h>
#define STEPS 5000                /* 500 ms at 0.1 ms per step */

int main(void) {
    const double DT = 0.1, V_REST = -70.0, V_TH = -45.0, V_RESET = -60.0;
    const double TAU_M = 9.0, TAU_E = 1.5, TAU_I = 6.0, TAU_REC = 400.0;
    const double E_E = 0.0, E_I = -80.0, W_E = 5.0, W_I = 6.0;
    double v = V_REST, ge = 0.0, gi = 0.0, res = 1.0;
    int spikes = 0, volleys = 0, refractory = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < STEPS; s++) {
        if (s < 1000 && s % 100 == 0) {  /* 100 Hz thalamic burst for 100 ms */
            ge += W_E * res;             /* scaled by the resource left */
            res *= 0.75;                 /* these synapses depress hard */
            volleys++;
        }
        /* feedforward inhibition from fast spiking cells, 1.5 ms behind */
        if (s >= 15 && (s - 15) < 1000 && (s - 15) % 100 == 0) gi += W_I;
        ge -= DT * ge / TAU_E;
        gi -= DT * gi / TAU_I;
        res += DT * (1.0 - res) / TAU_REC;          /* slow recovery */
        if (refractory > 0) { refractory--; v = V_RESET; continue; }
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + ge * (E_E - v) + gi * (E_I - v)) / TAU_M;
        if (v >= V_TH) { spikes++; v = V_RESET; refractory = 30; } /* 3 ms */
    }
    printf("volleys %d, stellate spikes %d\n", volleys, spikes);
    return 0;   /* fewer spikes than volleys: it reports onset, not duration */
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small spiny star-shaped excitatory neuron with no apical dendrite, living only in layer four of a primary sensory area, its dendrites stopping at the layer four border and its axon never leaving the column.

CHAPTER 12. STAR PYRAMID CELLS (COG)

Halfway between the star and the pyramid sits a cell that could not decide: it kept the triangular body and the upward mast, but cut the mast short, so it reaches just far enough to touch the layer above.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Star pyramid cells are excitatory neurons of layer four that keep an apical dendrite but a markedly short one, which gives their branching a star-like look while leaving a recognisable mast, and they receive thalamic input and relay it upward as spiny stellate cells do.

The difference is decisive: because the apical dendrite pokes into layer two and three it forms synapses with superficial axons the spiny stellate cannot reach, so this cell can weigh what the senses deliver now against what the cortex above already believes.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small pyramidal cell body with basal dendrites spreading through layer four exactly like a stellate's, plus an apical dendrite whose branching stays inside layer four except for its distal part, which extends into layer two and three and may end in a small tuft or simply taper.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 10 to 18 micrometres, slightly larger than neighbouring stellates, and the apical dendrite is typically 100 to 400 micrometres long, an order of magnitude shorter than the 1 to 2 millimetre mast of a layer five cell.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

By apical reach, cells stopping at the layer four to three border versus cells penetrating well into layer three, and by ending, a small tuft versus a simple taper; star pyramids are one of three layer four excitatory morphologies alongside spiny stellate and full pyramidal cells, forming a continuum rather than three sealed boxes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer four of sensory and association cortex in both hemispheres, including primary visual cortex where star pyramids are a recognised class in circuit reconstructions, primary somatosensory cortex where they amplify thalamic input alongside spiny stellate cells, and primary auditory cortex.

The defining interface is the layer four to layer three boundary, which this cell straddles with its own dendrite rather than merely with an axon.

NUMBER:

In samples of connected spiny neuron pairs from rodent layer four, roughly 80 percent were spiny stellate cells and the remainder star pyramids, and since human layer four outside primary visual cortex is thin and stellate-poor the balance shifts further toward this type, giving an estimated 100 million to 400 million cells, near 200 million centrally, about 1.2 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons.

Confidence is low, because the line between a star pyramid and a small layer four pyramidal cell is a judgement made by eye, and because these cells sit inside the pyramidal umbrella already counted, so this figure is a breakdown of that total and must not be added to it.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Regular spiking with moderate adaptation, resting near minus 70 millivolts and firing near minus 47, with a 10 to 15 millisecond membrane time constant slightly slower than a stellate's because the extra apical membrane adds capacitance, and excitatory connections involving layer four pyramidal cells show short-term depression.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Thalamocortical axons reach the basal and proximal dendrites, recurrent excitation comes from other layer four spiny cells, fast-spiking interneurons deliver feedforward inhibition, and uniquely for a layer four cell the apical dendrite receives direct input from horizontal and feedback axons in layer three.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon leaves the cell body base and ramifies within the home column, projecting most strongly to layers two and three, and unlike the spiny stellate a proportion send a collateral beyond the column, giving layer four a modest long-range output it would otherwise lack.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, with the standard cortical excitatory pharmacology of AMPA and NMDA receptors at its inputs and GABA receptors for inhibition; nothing peculiar has been reported for this type, and it is more useful to say so plainly than to invent a distinction.

MYELINATION:

Local collaterals are unmyelinated and the minority of cells sending an axon out of the column acquire thin myelin only on that segment, so myelination is minimal overall, as for all layer four excitatory cells.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High per unit volume in line with the rest of layer four, the most metabolically active layer of sensory cortex, with the apical dendrite adding surface area and therefore a continuous pumping cost that a spiny stellate does not pay.

PLASTICITY:

Layer four pyramidal neurons show robust dendritic spine plasticity in vivo after input deprivation, striking because layer four as a whole is the least plastic cortical layer, which makes star pyramids the layer four cells that can still be rewired by adult experience.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

They share the specialised layer four glial environment of very high astrocyte glutamate transporter levels and a dense capillary bed, with one difference: because the apical dendrite passes into layer three, one cell is served by two astrocyte territories with different transporter densities and vascular supply.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove star pyramids and layer four becomes a pure relay with no opinion, still amplifying and forwarding the thalamic message but losing the one cell that weighs sensation against the state of the layer above, so the earliest meeting of expectation and sensation moves one synapse later.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Their short apical dendrite makes them gain modulators rather than adders, because apical input in layer three depolarises the trunk and unblocks NMDA receptors on the basal tree, multiplying the effect of thalamic input instead of adding to it.

Layer four pyramidal neuron dendritic bursting underlies the post-stimulus alpha rhythm of visual cortex, the roughly 10 hertz oscillation appearing after a visual stimulus ends, so this cell is a rhythm generator as well as a relay.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: thalamocortical glutamate to basal dendrites, 0 to 100 hertz with depression; recurrent layer four input, 0 to 20 hertz; apical input from layer three as a modulation level 0 to 1; inhibition delayed 1.5 milliseconds.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto layer two and three pyramidal cells, other layer four spiny cells, layers five and six, and a minority projection beyond the column; 0 to 40 hertz plus a dendritic burst mode.

• STATE: somatic voltage; apical trunk depolarisation; adaptation current; synaptic depression resource; gain factor derived from apical input.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 70 millivolts; threshold minus 47; reset minus 60; input resistance 70 megaohms; apical gain coefficient 0.8, so full apical drive nearly doubles the effect of basal drive; adaptation increment 0.02 nanoamps per spike.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 12 milliseconds; apical modulation 60; adaptation 200; depression recovery 400; alpha rhythm period about 100.

• FAILURE MODE: because their dendritic bursting generates a cortical alpha rhythm, dysfunction here shows up as abnormal resting rhythms rather than blindness, and after chronic sensory loss their spines remodel extensively, so their wiring drifts furthest from the original map.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a layer four star pyramid cell in which input on the short apical dendrite from layer three multiplies the effect of thalamic input on the basal dendrites.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called adaptation current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called apical level with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called output spikes with initial value empty list.
For each time point in the simulation, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of layer three input and apical level.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 600.
    Calculate the sum of apical level and that result.
    Set apical level to that result.
    Calculate the product of apical level and 0.8.
    Calculate the sum of 1 and that result.
    Set a number variable called gain to that result.
    Calculate the product of thalamic current and gain.
    Calculate the difference of that result and adaptation current.
    Calculate the product of that result and 70.
    Set a number variable called drive to that result.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and membrane voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that result and drive.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 120.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that result.
    Set membrane voltage to that result.
    Calculate the quotient of adaptation current divided by 2000.
    Calculate the difference of adaptation current and that result.
    Set adaptation current to that result.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -47.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time point to the end of output spikes.
        Set membrane voltage to -60.
        Calculate the sum of adaptation current and 0.02.
        Set adaptation current to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in output spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the point of this cell is a single multiplication, and Python lets the
# gain term stand out with no language ceremony around it.
DT, TAU_M, TAU_AP, TAU_W = 0.1, 12.0, 60.0, 200.0   # all in milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -70.0, -47.0, -60.0         # millivolts
R_IN, GAIN_K, B_W = 70.0, 0.8, 0.02   # megaohms, gain factor, nA per spike

def run(thalamic_na, layer3_input, duration_ms):
    """Layer 3 input scales the thalamic drive rather than adding to it."""
    v, w, apical, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0.0, []
    for step in range(int(duration_ms / DT)):
        apical += DT * (layer3_input - apical) / TAU_AP  # slow apical charging
        gain = 1.0 + GAIN_K * apical                     # the multiplication
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + R_IN * (thalamic_na * gain - w)) / TAU_M
        w -= DT * w / TAU_W
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(round(step * DT, 1))
            v, w = V_RESET, w + B_W
    return spikes

print("thalamic alone     :", len(run(0.36, 0.0, 1000.0)), "spikes")
print("thalamic plus L3   :", len(run(0.36, 1.0, 1000.0)), "spikes")
print("L3 with no thalamus:", len(run(0.00, 1.0, 1000.0)), "spikes")
# Layer 3 input alone does nothing: it is a multiplier, not a driver.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A spiny excitatory cell in layer four with a real apical dendrite far too short for a pyramidal cell and far too long for a stellate, ending in layer three, and the only layer four cell that owns dendrite in two layers at once.

CHAPTER 13. BETZ CELLS (COG)

You can see one with the naked eye on a stained slide, and the single wire it sends out may be longer than your leg.

Gloss: the motor cortex's giant, fastest line to the spinal cord.

These are among the largest neurons in the central nervous system at up to about one hundred micrometres of soma diameter, and they send axons down the corticospinal tract, though they supply only about one to two percent of its fibres.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Betz cell is the largest neuron in the human brain measured by cell body volume, sitting in the motor strip and sending one command fibre straight down into the spinal cord.

Vladimir Betz described these giants in 1874, and clinicians call them upper motor neurons: they are what a doctor is probing when they tap your knee and watch the reflex.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a huge pyramid, and unlike an ordinary pyramidal neuron it throws its lower branches out in a full circle around the soma rather than only sideways.

A dendrite is a branch that collects incoming signals, and a spine is the tiny knob where most excitatory contacts land; the Betz soma and the first stretch of every dendrite are completely bare of spines, so the nearest inputs are inhibitory contacts placed directly on the cell surface.

One thick apical dendrite climbs toward the brain surface, and some Betz cells send a taproot dendrite downward into the white matter, the cabling layer beneath the cortex.

SIZE:

Betz measured roughly 60 by 120 micrometres and Brodmann recorded 53 by 106 micrometres, where a micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

Reconstruction gives a mean cell body volume near 86,685 cubic micrometres, and soma diameters of 70 to 100 micrometres are routinely quoted, putting the biggest at the edge of unaided human vision.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There is no accepted formal subtype scheme, but Betz cells vary with the body part they control: those in the leg region of the paracentral lobule have the longest axons and the most pronounced taproot dendrites, while hand and face cells sit in denser clusters.

About seventy five percent are concentrated in the upper third of the motor strip, the part commanding trunk and lower limb.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 5b of the primary motor cortex, which is Brodmann area 4, on the anterior bank of the central sulcus, the crown of the precentral gyrus, and the anterior paracentral lobule on the inner face of each hemisphere.

They do not form a continuous sheet but sit alone or in small columnar nests, and the critical interface is the layer 5b to layer 6 boundary where the axon leaves the cortex and enters the white matter.

NUMBER:

The counts disagree by a factor of four and you should know it: Lassek's classic 1940 count gave about 34,000 in area 4 of one hemisphere and the older literature settles on 25,000 to 40,000 per hemisphere, while a recent stereological estimate gives about 125,290 per hemisphere.

The full plausible span is therefore 50,000 to 250,000 across both hemispheres, which against the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons reported by Azevedo and colleagues in 2009 is at most 0.0015 percent, or fifteen cells in every million.

Confidence is high on the order of magnitude and low on the exact value, and one review places them at roughly one in ten of the pyramidal neurons in layer 5 of the giant cell zone.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Primate Betz cells show a firing pattern rodents simply do not have: given a steady command they fire briefly, fall almost silent, then accelerate to a high sustained rate, described in the literature as an early cessation of firing followed by a delayed sustained acceleration in spike frequency and magnitude.

They are among the earliest cortical cells to become active, discharging roughly 40 to 100 milliseconds before a movement begins.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from primary somatosensory cortex, premotor and supplementary motor areas, the thalamus, and dense local recurrents within layer 5.

Because the soma is spine free, the closest and most powerful controllers are parvalbumin expressing basket cells, a fast inhibitory type that wraps the cell body in a basket of contacts; the main output is the spinal ventral horn, where in humans a fraction of Betz axons contact motor neurons directly.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single Betz axon runs about 60 to 70 centimetres from the paracentral lobule to the lumbar enlargement, and over a metre in tall adults.

Its axons are the thickest in the corticospinal tract, the great descending motor bundle, conducting faster than 70 metres per second, yet they make up only about 1 to 3 percent of the roughly one million fibres in each medullary pyramid.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the brain's main excitatory chemical messenger.

The cell also carries receptors for acetylcholine, serotonin and dopamine, so arousal and reward systems tune its excitability even though it releases none of those chemicals itself.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated, meaning the axon is wrapped in a fatty insulating sheath made by oligodendrocytes, glial cells whose only job is insulation.

The sheath is thick in proportion to the very large axon diameter, which is exactly why the conduction speed is so high.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Extreme, and the transcriptome shows it: Betz cells are unusually rich in messenger molecules for oxidative phosphorylation, the mitochondrial process that makes cellular fuel, including the ATP synthase subunit ATP5F1B.

Maintaining a metre of thick axon plus a giant soma means an enormous continuous bill for pumping ions and shipping proteins.

PLASTICITY:

Motor learning changes the strength of the inputs onto Betz dendrites rather than the cell itself, and long term potentiation, the lasting strengthening of a synapse after repeated use, is readily induced in layer 5 motor cortex.

The cell count does not increase with practice; the wiring onto it is what changes when you learn an instrument.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The perisomatic astrocytes here face an unusual load, because one Betz spike dumps a large amount of potassium into a small space, and the oligodendrocytes serving Betz axons build some of the thickest sheaths in the central nervous system over the longest distance.

In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, cluster around Betz cells early, one of the first visible cortical signs of the disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove Betz cells and the motor cortex loses its fastest, most direct line to the muscles.

What remains is a slower, committee driven motor system that still moves the body but loses the crisp, precisely timed onset of a skilled voluntary act.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The circumferential basal dendrites let a Betz cell sample its whole neighbourhood rather than a strip of it, and back propagating action potentials, spikes travelling backwards from soma into dendrites, tag recently active branches for strengthening.

The taproot dendrite dips into the white matter and can be contacted by axons that never enter grey matter at all.

Long distance axonal transport is a computation of its own, because proteins made in the soma take days to reach the far end, so the cell must anticipate its own needs.

The very high energy demand couples it tightly to astrocytes, which supply lactate as fuel, and to local blood vessels, which dilate in response to its activity.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: excitatory glutamate from primary somatosensory cortex, premotor cortex, supplementary motor area, motor thalamus and local layer 5 recurrents, as a current in the range 0 to 3 nanoamperes.

◦ Perisomatic inhibition from parvalbumin basket cells, a fractional shunt from 0 to 1.

◦ Acetylcholine, serotonin and dopamine levels, each 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: single spikes to spinal ventral horn motor neurons and interneurons, the red nucleus, and the pontine nuclei, with 8 to 15 milliseconds conduction delay to lumbar targets.

• STATE: membrane voltage, spike triggered potassium brake, delayed persistent inward current, intracellular calcium, axonal transport queue.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential near minus 70 millivolts, threshold near minus 50 millivolts, membrane time constant 10 to 15 milliseconds, soma volume near 86,685 cubic micrometres, conduction velocity above 70 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 15 milliseconds, spike frequency adaptation 1 to 3 seconds, delayed acceleration onset 100 to 200 milliseconds, premovement lead 40 to 100 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and primary lateral sclerosis the cell shrinks, loses dendrites and dies, producing weakness, spasticity and brisk reflexes; in hereditary spastic paraplegia the long axon degenerates from its far end first.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the two phase discharge of a Betz cell during a sustained voluntary movement command.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called potassium brake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called late inward current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called voltage change with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step counter with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
While step counter is less than 10000, repeat the following steps.
    If step counter is greater than or equal to 1200, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage change to the difference of 26 and late inward current.
        Set late inward current to the sum of late inward current and the quotient of voltage change divided by 3000.
    End the conditional block.
    Set potassium brake to the difference of potassium brake and the quotient of potassium brake divided by 20000.
    Set voltage change to the difference of -70 and membrane voltage.
    Set voltage change to the sum of voltage change and 32.
    Set voltage change to the sum of voltage change and late inward current.
    Set voltage change to the difference of voltage change and potassium brake.
    Set voltage change to the quotient of voltage change divided by 120.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and voltage change.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -50, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -70.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
        If potassium brake is less than 28, then perform the following actions.
            Set potassium brake to the sum of potassium brake and 6.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step counter to the sum of step counter and 1.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a Betz cell in a whole brain simulation is one of millions of fixed step integrations per millisecond, and a tight deterministic loop is what that kernel actually looks like.

#include <stdio.h>

/* Betz cell: integrate and fire soma plus two slow currents.
   A spike triggered, saturating potassium current silences the cell
   early; a delayed persistent inward current drives the late climb. */
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.1;        /* ms per step                      */
    const double tau_m = 12.0;    /* membrane time constant, ms       */
    const double v_rest = -70.0, v_th = -50.0;   /* mV                */
    const double cmd = 32.0;      /* steady movement command, mV      */
    const double da = 6.0, a_cap = 28.0, tau_a = 2000.0;  /* K+ brake */
    const double r_max = 26.0, tau_r = 300.0, r_onset = 120.0; /* PIC */
    double v = v_rest, a = 0.0, r = 0.0;
    int bin[8] = {0,0,0,0,0,0,0,0};
    int s;
    for (s = 0; s < 10000; s++) {          /* 1000 ms of movement */
        double t = s * dt;
        if (t >= r_onset) r += dt * ((r_max - r) / tau_r);
        a -= dt * (a / tau_a);
        v += dt * ((v_rest - v + cmd + r - a) / tau_m);
        if (v >= v_th) {                   /* spike */
            v = v_rest;
            a += da;
            if (a > a_cap) a = a_cap;      /* the brake saturates */
            bin[(int)(t / 125.0)]++;
        }
    }
    for (s = 0; s < 8; s++)
        printf("%4d to %4d ms: %3d Hz\n", s*125, s*125+125, bin[s]*8);
    return 0;
}

Compiled and run, this prints 24, 8, 8, 32, 48, 64, 64, 64 hertz across eight windows: a brief opening volley, near silence, then the delayed sustained climb.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the human cortex is that big.

A pyramid with a cell body approaching one tenth of a millimetre across, spine free basal dendrites radiating in a full circle, sitting in a small nest in layer 5b of the precentral gyrus, is a Betz cell and nothing else.

CHAPTER 14. MEYNERT CELLS (COG)

Deep in the visual cortex, spaced out like street lamps, sit large lonely neurons that shout the same message to two completely different parts of the brain at once.

Gloss: visual cortex's long reach to motion areas and midbrain.

Single Meynert cells at the layer five and six border of the primary visual cortex send divergent collaterals both to the motion area V5 and to the superior colliculus, while their precise computational role is still openly described as elusive.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Meynert cell is a giant, isolated pyramidal neuron in the deep layers of primary visual cortex, described by Theodor Meynert in the 1860s and known ever since as the solitary cell of Meynert because it sits alone rather than in a crowd.

Its job is to take a strong motion signal and broadcast it simultaneously to the midbrain, which moves the eyes, and to the motion analysing cortex, which tells you what is moving and where.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each cell has one apical dendrite reaching upward and an unusually large basal spread that is lopsided, extending much further to one side than the other, and that asymmetry is thought to give the cell its preference for motion in one direction.

The soma and proximal dendrites are bare, but further out a single Meynert cell carries about 36,000 spines, unevenly distributed across its branches.

They stain strongly with silver, a property called argyrophilia, because they are stuffed with neurofilaments, the internal protein cables that give a large neuron its shape.

SIZE:

Large by the standards of visual cortex and the biggest neurons in it, though nowhere near the scale of a Betz cell; precise human soma measurements are scarce, so the honest statement is that they are several times the volume of a neighbouring layer 5 pyramid.

The spacing is better documented than the diameter: in the part of cortex serving central vision they sit a minimum of about 110 micrometres apart.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two populations are distinguished by depth: the inner Meynert cells lie at the border between layers 5 and 6 and project to both the superior colliculus and the motion area, while the outer group described in layer 4B projects to the motion area alone.

Both are strongly direction selective and both show the lopsided lateral dendritic field.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Primary visual cortex, Brodmann area 17, also called striate cortex, lining the calcarine sulcus on the inner face of each occipital lobe.

The classic position is deep layer 5 at the interface with layer 6, which the older literature often simply calls layer 6, and the inner cells lie under the cytochrome oxidase interblobs, the pale patches between the colour processing pillars, itself an interface between two functional compartments of area 17.

NUMBER:

Density is known and total number is not, so here is the arithmetic: in the macaque these cells reach about 8,000 per square centimetre of cortical surface in the region serving the very centre of vision, falling to about 625 per square centimetre in surrounding perifoveal cortex.

Human primary visual cortex covers roughly 2,400 square millimetres per hemisphere, which is 24 square centimetres, and most of that is not foveal, so an average density near the perifoveal figure gives tens of thousands per hemisphere, of the order of 100,000 across both.

That is well under 0.001 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and confidence is low because the estimate is extrapolated from monkey densities.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Regular spiking pyramidal behaviour with strong direction selective tuning, meaning the cell responds vigorously to motion one way and barely at all to motion the other way.

Their large size and heavy myelination make them fast, well placed to deliver an early motion signal to structures that must react quickly.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the magnocellular stream, the fast motion sensitive channel of the visual pathway, arriving through layer 4C alpha and the local circuitry above it.

Outputs go by divergent axon collaterals, branches of the same axon, to the superior colliculus in the midbrain and to area V5, also called MT, on the posterior bank of the superior temporal sulcus, so the same spike reaches an eye movement controller and a perceptual motion analyser at nearly the same moment.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single thick axon leaves the cortex and splits, one branch descending to the midbrain and the other running forward through white matter to the motion area.

This branching is the defining functional feature: one cell, one decision, two very different consequences.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the main excitatory messenger, at every one of its targets.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated on both collaterals, consistent with a cell whose value lies in getting a motion signal to the midbrain before the eye has moved too far.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for a visual cortical neuron, because of the large soma, the 36,000 spines and two long myelinated output branches.

They shrink measurably after visual deprivation, which shows their upkeep is activity dependent rather than fixed.

PLASTICITY:

Dendritic size and spine number depend on visual experience, and depriving an animal of vision reduces both.

Once the critical period of early life closes, the direction preference itself is largely fixed.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing dramatically unusual is documented for the glia around Meynert cells, and it is better to say so plainly than to invent a specialisation.

The one point worth making is that these cells sit in the most metabolically active cortex in the brain, so astrocyte and capillary density around them is already exceptionally high compared with cortex elsewhere.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take Meynert cells out and area 17 keeps its exquisite analysis of edges, colour and depth but loses its fast hotline to the eye movement system.

The flavour lost is urgency: the reflexive snap of your eyes toward something that just moved in the corner of your vision.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The asymmetric dendritic tree performs a spatial computation before the soma sees anything, because motion sweeping along the long axis of the tree recruits inputs in a sequence that summates far better than motion the other way.

Back propagating spikes into that long lateral dendrite let the cell mark which branch just contributed.

Axonal divergence is itself a computation, because the cell cannot send different messages to the colliculus and the motion area and therefore enforces a shared assumption between a reflex system and a perceptual one.

Activity dependent shrinkage after deprivation shows the cell continuously remodels its own structure to match how much work it is getting.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: magnocellular driven excitation from layer 4C alpha and local layer 4B, as a directional motion energy vector over eight directions, each 0 to 1.

◦ Local inhibition from layer 5 and 6 interneurons, a fractional shunt from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: identical spikes on two collaterals, one to the superior colliculus and one to area V5 or MT, with conduction delays of roughly 2 to 5 and 4 to 8 milliseconds respectively.

• STATE: membrane voltage, per branch activation of the asymmetric lateral tree, adaptation level, spine count as a slow structural state.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential near minus 65 millivolts, threshold near minus 48 millivolts, preferred direction in degrees, direction selectivity index 0 to 1, about 36,000 spines, minimum spacing 110 micrometres near the fovea.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, direction tuning window 20 to 60 milliseconds, structural remodelling after deprivation days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss or shrinkage after early visual deprivation degrades reflexive orienting to moving targets, and damage to this output limb contributes to the residual motion sensitivity of blindsight, where a person denies seeing anything yet still responds to movement.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a Meynert cell has detected motion in its preferred direction and, if so, to fire one spike into two very different destinations at the same time.
Create a number variable called preferred energy with initial value 1.0.
Create a number variable called opposite energy with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called voltage change with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called colliculus messages with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called area MT messages with initial value empty list.
While clock is less than 100, repeat the following steps.
    Set drive to the difference of preferred energy and opposite energy.
    Set drive to the product of drive and 40.
    Set voltage change to the difference of -65 and membrane voltage.
    Set voltage change to the sum of voltage change and drive.
    Set voltage change to the quotient of voltage change divided by 15.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and voltage change.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -48, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -65.
        Add clock to the end of colliculus messages.
        Add clock to the end of area MT messages.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in colliculus messages to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the point of this cell is a tuning curve and a two way broadcast, and Python lets both be read at a glance without ceremony.

import math

DIRECTIONS = [i * 45.0 for i in range(8)]   # eight motion directions, degrees
PREFERRED = 90.0                            # set by the lopsided lateral dendrite

def dendrite_weight(theta):
    # rectified cosine tuning produced by the asymmetric dendritic field
    return max(0.0, math.cos(math.radians(theta - PREFERRED)))

def meynert_step(v, energy, dt=1.0):
    tau, v_rest, v_th, gain = 15.0, -65.0, -48.0, 40.0
    drive = gain * sum(w * e for w, e in
                       zip((dendrite_weight(d) for d in DIRECTIONS), energy))
    v += dt * ((v_rest - v + drive) / tau)
    if v >= v_th:
        return v_rest, {"superior_colliculus": 1, "area_MT": 1}
    return v, {"superior_colliculus": 0, "area_MT": 0}

energy = [0.0] * 8
energy[2] = 1.0            # motion at 90 degrees, the preferred direction
v, n = -65.0, 0
for t in range(100):
    v, out = meynert_step(v, energy)
    n += out["superior_colliculus"]
print("spikes in 100 ms to BOTH targets:", n)

tuning = [dendrite_weight(d) for d in DIRECTIONS]
pref, null = tuning[2], tuning[6]
print("tuning curve:", [round(x, 2) for x in tuning])
print("direction selectivity index:", round((pref - null) / (pref + null + 1e-9), 3))

Run, this prints 11 spikes in 100 milliseconds delivered to both targets, and a direction selectivity index of 1.0.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A very large, silver loving, solitary pyramid at the layer 5 to 6 border of the calcarine cortex, standing well clear of its neighbours, with a basal dendritic field that leans hard to one side.

No other cell in area 17 is both that big and that alone.

CHAPTER 15. VON ECONOMO NEURONS (VENS), ALSO CALLED SPINDLE NEURONS, ROD CELLS, OR CORKSCREW CELLS (COG)

They look like a stretched cigar with one thick wire out of each end; they appear in only a handful of large-brained species, and they die first in the dementia that destroys a person's manners before it touches their memory.

Big, fast spindle output cell of social and gut-feeling cortex.

These cells sit in layer five of the frontoinsula and anterior cingulate cortex, average roughly four and a half times the volume of neighbouring layer five pyramidal neurons, and align transcriptomically with extratelencephalic neurons that project below the cortex.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A von Economo neuron is a large, slender, top to bottom symmetrical projection neuron found in just two patches of cortex, described systematically by Constantin von Economo in the 1920s.

They sit inside the salience network, the circuit that decides which of the thousand things happening to you right now actually matters, and they appear to carry a fast summary of that decision to distant targets.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is elongated and roughly spindle shaped, and unlike a pyramid it is nearly symmetrical about its middle, with one thick dendrite leaving the top, one leaving the bottom, very little branching and few spines on the proximal parts.

That simplicity is the whole design: instead of a bushy tree sampling thousands of separate opinions, a von Economo neuron samples two narrow columns of tissue and reports quickly.

SIZE:

They are large, and the identification criterion used by stereologists is that a candidate must be at least as big as the neighbouring layer 5 pyramidal neurons.

Typical descriptions give an elongated soma several tens of micrometres long and only ten to fifteen micrometres wide, giving the characteristic stick like profile.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes are recognised, and variation is regional rather than categorical.

Cells in the frontoinsula are on average slightly larger and more numerous than those in the anterior cingulate, and there are consistently more in the right hemisphere than the left.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 5, specifically layer 5b, of the anterior cingulate cortex, which is Brodmann areas 24, 32 and 33 on the inner face of the frontal lobe, and of the frontoinsular cortex, the agranular front end of the insula buried under the frontal and temporal lobes, with small numbers reported in dorsolateral prefrontal area 9.

The frontoinsular site is itself an interface, sitting where the insula meets the frontal operculum and where signals from the body meet signals about the outside world.

NUMBER:

Careful stereology gives a density rather than a total: in non demented controls these cells number about 501 per 10,000 layer 5 neurons in the anterior cingulate, with a ninety five percent confidence interval of 398 to 604, so about five percent of the layer 5 neurons in the sampling frame.

Working that upward is necessarily rough, but if the anterior cingulate and frontoinsula together hold a few hundred million neurons and layer 5 is a modest fraction of those, the total falls somewhere between one hundred thousand and one million across both hemispheres.

Against the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons that is at most about 0.006 percent, and confidence in the total is low while confidence in the five percent density is good.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Direct recordings from human von Economo neurons do not exist, so this must be stated as unknown.

Transcriptomic work places them firmly with layer 5 extratelencephalic projecting excitatory neurons, the deep output class that also contains Betz cells, and their expression of the alpha 1A adrenoceptor and dopamine D3 receptor means excitability should be strongly set by the arousal chemicals bathing the tissue.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive on two narrow vertical columns rather than a spreading tree, one from the superficial layers and one from the deep layers and white matter.

Outputs are long range and extratelencephalic, meaning they leave the cerebral hemispheres altogether, with likely targets in the brainstem and other subcortical autonomic centres, plus long corticocortical branches.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single long axon leaving from the base of the spindle.

The extratelencephalic transcriptomic signature implies a thick, fast, subcortically directed fibre, though the human axon has not been traced end to end and its diameter is unmeasured.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, as an excitatory projection neuron, but the striking finding is a monoamine handling profile.

About 92 percent carry vesicular monoamine transporter 2, the pump that loads monoamines such as dopamine, noradrenaline and serotonin into synaptic vesicles, about 82 percent carry the theta subunit of the type A receptor for gamma aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory messenger, and they also express the alpha 1A adrenoceptor and the dopamine D3 receptor.

Critically they lack the enzymes that make monoamines and the transporters that recapture them, so they package monoamines they did not make, a mode of signalling nobody has yet explained.

MYELINATION:

Myelinated, consistent with a long range projection neuron, with thickness unquantified in humans.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Presumed high, in line with other large layer 5 projection neurons, though not directly measured.

Their selective vulnerability in disease is the clearest indirect evidence, because cells that die first in a metabolic or protein handling crisis are usually the ones living closest to the edge.

PLASTICITY:

Their number is developmentally plastic in a striking way: they first appear around the 36th week after conception, are rare at birth, increase over the first eight months of life, and reach adult numbers by about four years of age.

That timetable maps neatly onto the years in which a child learns to read other people.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing distinctive is established for the glia surrounding von Economo neurons in health.

In disease the picture changes sharply, with microglial activation and abnormal protein deposits, including tau and TDP-43, appearing preferentially in and around these cells in frontotemporal lobar degeneration.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove von Economo neurons and the anterior cingulate and insula still register the state of the body, but they lose their fast reporter.

The flavour lost is social immediacy: the instant flush of embarrassment, the pull of another person's distress, the internal alarm that stops you saying the thing you were about to say.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The bipolar geometry is a computation, because it forces the cell to compare a superficial input stream with a deep one along a single axis instead of averaging hundreds of branches.

The vesicular monoamine transporter 2 finding raises the real possibility of non classical release, in which the cell secretes a monoamine scavenged from its surroundings, a form of volume transmission that would affect a whole neighbourhood rather than a single partner.

Expression of the alpha 1A adrenoceptor makes the cell a gain control device whose sensitivity is set by the ambient noradrenaline level, so the same bodily signal produces a bigger response when you are already aroused.

They also express activating transcription factor 3 and interleukin 4 receptor, tying them to stress driven gene transcription and to immune signalling inside the cortex.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: interoceptive prediction error from insular cortex, 0 to 1, meaning how far the body's actual state departs from what was expected.

◦ Autonomic arousal from anterior cingulate and brainstem, 0 to 1.

◦ Ambient noradrenaline, dopamine and serotonin concentration, each 0 to 1, acting on the receptor set rather than on a synapse.

• OUTPUTS: spikes on a long extratelencephalic axon to brainstem autonomic nuclei and to distant cortical nodes of the salience network.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation level, superficial dendrite activation, deep dendrite activation, vesicular monoamine load.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential near minus 68 millivolts, threshold near minus 47 millivolts, adrenergic gain factor 1.0 to 2.5, density about 5 percent of layer 5 neurons, right hemisphere excess of roughly 20 to 30 percent.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds, adaptation 200 to 300 milliseconds, monoamine tone over seconds, developmental population growth over months to four years.

• FAILURE MODE: in behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia they are lost selectively and early, with reported reductions of about 53 percent in neuropathologically early cases and up to 74 percent relative to neighbouring neurons, producing loss of empathy, disinhibition and loss of self awareness while memory is initially spared; in Alzheimer's disease they survive even into advanced stages.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a von Economo neuron report a sudden bodily surprise, with the size of the report set by how much noradrenaline is bathing the tissue.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -68.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called noradrenaline with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called error with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Set gain to the sum of 1 and the product of 1.5 and noradrenaline.
While clock is less than 400, repeat the following steps.
    Set error to 0.05.
    If both clock is greater than or equal to 100 and clock is less than 160 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set error to 0.9.
    End the conditional block.
    Set drive to the sum of the product of 26 and error and 3.2.
    Set drive to the difference of the product of drive and gain and adaptation.
    Set drive to the sum of drive and the difference of -68 and membrane voltage.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of drive divided by 18.
    Set adaptation to the difference of adaptation and the quotient of adaptation divided by 220.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -47, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -68.
        Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and 6.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting part is a single multiplicative gain term set from outside the cell, and a plain dictionary of state makes that dependence impossible to miss.

def make_ven():
    return {"v": -68.0, "adapt": 0.0}

def ven_step(cell, error, arousal, noradrenaline, dt=1.0):
    TAU_M, TAU_A, V_REST, V_TH = 18.0, 220.0, -68.0, -47.0
    # ADRA1A sets the gain from outside. The cell loads monoamines with
    # VMAT2 but synthesises none, so its tone is imported, not generated.
    gain = 1.0 + 1.5 * noradrenaline
    drive = gain * (26.0 * error + 8.0 * arousal) - cell["adapt"]
    cell["v"] += dt * ((V_REST - cell["v"] + drive) / TAU_M)
    cell["adapt"] -= dt * (cell["adapt"] / TAU_A)
    if cell["v"] >= V_TH:
        cell["v"] = V_REST
        cell["adapt"] += 6.0        # strong spike frequency adaptation
        return 1
    return 0

for na in (0.0, 0.3, 0.6, 1.0):
    cell, total, before = make_ven(), 0, 0
    for t in range(400):
        err = 0.9 if 100 <= t < 160 else 0.05   # a sudden bodily surprise
        f = ven_step(cell, err, 0.4, na)
        total += f
        if t < 100:
            before += f
    print("noradrenaline %.1f -> %2d spikes, %d of them before the surprise"
          % (na, total, before))

Run, this prints 1, 3, 4 and 6 spikes for rising noradrenaline and none before the surprise: the same bodily event is reported six times more loudly in an aroused brain.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A long, thin, cigar shaped cell body with exactly one thick dendrite out of the top and one out of the bottom, no basal skirt, in layer 5 of the anterior cingulate or the frontoinsula.

Turn the picture upside down and it looks the same, which no pyramidal neuron ever does.

CHAPTER 16. FORK CELLS (COG)

Its apical dendrite splits into two equal prongs like a tuning fork, and it will not speak unless both prongs agree.

Twin-pronged partner of the spindle cell in feeling-aware cortex.

Fork cells share layer five of the frontoinsula and a single transcriptomic cluster with von Economo neurons.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A fork cell is the second unusual large neuron of layer 5 in the frontoinsular and anterior cingulate cortex, the constant companion of the von Economo neuron.

Where a von Economo neuron has one thick dendrite going up, a fork cell has one that divides near the soma into two roughly equal branches, and it shares its neighbour's chemistry, territory and fate in disease, which is why the two are almost always counted together.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

One single large basal dendrite descends from the bottom of the cell body, while from the top an apical dendrite rises a short way and then bifurcates into two prongs of similar calibre that ascend in parallel toward the cortical surface.

The soma is large and shares the elongated look of a von Economo neuron, but the divided apical shaft is the diagnostic feature and it is visible in a routine silver or Nissl stain.

SIZE:

Comparable to a von Economo neuron and to the larger layer 5 pyramidal neurons around it, which is the criterion used to identify it in counting studies.

Exact human soma dimensions are not well tabulated and should be treated as unknown rather than guessed.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None are recognised.

The only reliable variation is the height at which the apical shaft divides, ranging from a split almost at the soma to one several tens of micrometres above it.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 5 of the frontoinsular cortex, the agranular anterior insula, and layer 5 of the anterior cingulate cortex, Brodmann areas 24, 32 and 33, interleaved with von Economo neurons in exactly the same laminar position and markedly more affected on the right side of the brain in disease.

The relevant interface is the layer 1 to layer 2 boundary, because that is where the two prongs of the apical dendrite terminate and collect long range feedback.

NUMBER:

No independent total exists for fork cells, and inventing one would be dishonest.

What is documented is that in the counting frames used by Seeley's group in the frontoinsula, fork cells are consistently found alongside von Economo neurons and are the rarer of the two.

A defensible bound is therefore fewer than the von Economo population, so of the order of one hundred thousand across both hemispheres, well under 0.001 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, with low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unrecorded in humans, so genuinely unknown.

Their expression of the alpha 1A adrenoceptor and vesicular monoamine transporter 2 means excitability should be set by ambient monoamines in the same way as their von Economo neighbours.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The two apical prongs sample the superficial layers, which carry feedback from higher areas and from the thalamic matrix, a diffuse thalamic system that spreads context across wide swathes of cortex, while the single basal dendrite samples the deep local circuit and the incoming white matter.

Outputs are long range projections, in company with the von Economo neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One axon from the base of the soma.

Nothing quantitative is published on human fork cell axon diameter or conduction velocity, and it should be modelled as similar to the von Economo neuron until measured.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate as the released transmitter.

About 84 percent of fork cells carry vesicular monoamine transporter 2 and about 84 percent carry the theta subunit of the type A receptor for gamma aminobutyric acid, essentially the same proportions as von Economo neurons, and like them they express the alpha 1A adrenoceptor while lacking every enzyme needed to synthesise a monoamine.

MYELINATION:

Myelinated as a projection neuron, with no quantitative human data available.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Presumed high and unmeasured.

The strongest indirect evidence is vulnerability, because fork cells and von Economo neurons degenerate in synchrony in early behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia, and cells that fail together usually share a metabolic burden.

PLASTICITY:

Not directly studied.

Because fork cells appear in the same regions and are counted in the same postnatal windows as von Economo neurons, the reasonable working assumption is a similar late developmental arrival, but this is an assumption and not a finding.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

No distinctive glial specialisation is documented around fork cells in health.

In frontotemporal lobar degeneration caused by particular variants of the microtubule associated protein tau gene, tau aggregates preferentially inside fork cells and von Economo neurons, which implicates the local astrocytic and microglial clearance machinery in their selective death.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Fork cells give the frontoinsula a coincidence detector.

Take them away and the region can still report that something in the body has changed and that something in the world has changed, but it loses the cell that insists both must be true before the alarm is raised.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The divided apical shaft creates two electrically semi independent compartments, each capable of generating its own local calcium spike, a slow regenerative event confined to a dendrite.

Two prongs mean the cell can implement a logical AND across two separate patches of superficial cortex, then require the basal dendrite to agree before the soma fires.

Back propagating action potentials reach both prongs and can license strengthening in whichever one was active, so the cell can learn which pairs of contexts are worth linking.

Like von Economo neurons, the vesicular monoamine transporter 2 content raises the possibility of dendritic or somatic release of scavenged monoamines into the surrounding volume.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: superficial layer 1 feedback onto apical prong A, 0 to 1, and onto apical prong B, 0 to 1, from separate cortical sources.

◦ Deep local and white matter input onto the single basal dendrite, 0 to 1.

◦ Ambient noradrenaline setting gain, 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: spikes on one long range axon shared with the von Economo neuron population, targeting brainstem and distant cortex.

• STATE: prong A activation, prong B activation, basal activation, apical calcium flag, somatic voltage.

• PARAMETERS: prong calcium threshold near 0.55 in normalised units, somatic threshold near 0.70, basal weight 0.5, apical calcium weight 0.6.

• TIME CONSTANTS: apical prong integration 10 to 15 milliseconds, basal integration 5 to 8 milliseconds, dendritic calcium spike 20 to 50 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: selective loss alongside von Economo neurons in early behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia, right side worse, correlating with the severity of anatomical, functional and behavioural symptoms; preserved in Alzheimer's disease even at advanced stages.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a fork cell fire only when both prongs of its divided apical dendrite are loaded at once and its single basal dendrite agrees.
Create a number variable called left prong with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called right prong with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called basal branch with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called branch input with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called soma voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 300, repeat the following steps.
    Set branch input to 0.05.
    If both clock is greater than or equal to 100 and clock is less than 150 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set branch input to 1.0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set left prong to the sum of left prong and the quotient of the difference of branch input and left prong divided by 12.
    Set right prong to the sum of right prong and the quotient of the difference of branch input and right prong divided by 14.
    Set basal branch to the sum of basal branch and the quotient of the difference of branch input and basal branch divided by 6.
    Set soma voltage to the product of 0.5 and basal branch.
    If both left prong is greater than 0.55 and right prong is greater than 0.55 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set soma voltage to the sum of soma voltage and 0.6.
    End the conditional block.
    If soma voltage is greater than 0.7, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because the two prongs are naturally two small objects with independent state, and a dependency free script makes the coincidence rule obvious to any reader.

function branch(tau) { return { v: 0.0, tau: tau }; }
function integrate(b, input, dt) { b.v += dt * ((input - b.v) / b.tau); return b.v; }

const CA_TH = 0.55;   // calcium spike threshold inside one apical prong
const SOMA_TH = 0.70; // somatic firing threshold

function trial(leftOn, rightOn, basalOn) {
  const L = branch(12.0), R = branch(12.0), B = branch(6.0);
  let spikes = 0;
  for (let t = 0; t < 300; t++) {
    const l1L = (leftOn  && t >= 100 && t < 140) ? 1.0 : 0.05;
    const l1R = (rightOn && t >= 110 && t < 150) ? 1.0 : 0.05;
    const loc = (basalOn && t >= 120 && t < 130) ? 1.0 : 0.10;
    const a1 = integrate(L, l1L, 1.0);
    const a2 = integrate(R, l1R, 1.0);
    const bs = integrate(B, loc, 1.0);
    // the fork reports only when BOTH prongs are loaded together
    const apicalCa = (a1 > CA_TH && a2 > CA_TH) ? 1.0 : 0.0;
    if (0.5 * bs + 0.6 * apicalCa > SOMA_TH) spikes += 1;
  }
  return spikes;
}
console.log("both prongs + basal  :", trial(true,  true,  true));
console.log("one prong  + basal   :", trial(true,  false, true));
console.log("both prongs, no basal:", trial(true,  true,  false));

Run, this prints 21, 0 and 0: the cell is completely silent unless all three conditions hold at once.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large layer 5 neuron in the anterior insula or anterior cingulate with exactly one fat basal dendrite below and an apical shaft that forks into two matched prongs above.

No pyramidal neuron has a symmetrically divided apical trunk, and no von Economo neuron has any apical branching at all.

CHAPTER 17. CAJAL-RETZIUS CELLS (NON-COG)

They build the cortex; they tell every other neuron where to stop climbing, and then almost all of them die before you are born.

Early layer-one cell whose reelin signal stacks the cortex.

These transient marginal zone neurons secrete the glycoprotein reelin, and without it, as in the reeler mouse, the inside-out stacking of cortical layers fails entirely.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Cajal-Retzius cell is a temporary neuron lying just under the surface of the developing brain that secretes a protein called reelin, and it is named for Santiago Ramon y Cajal, who described it in 1890, and Gustaf Retzius, who described it in 1893.

Reelin is the stop signal telling each wave of young neurons climbing up from the deep brain where to halt, and it is why the cortex is built inside out, with the oldest cells deepest and the youngest nearest the surface.

Without it the layers are inverted and scrambled, which in people is the condition called lissencephaly with cerebellar hypoplasia.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The classic form is a horizontal cell with an oval body and one thick dendrite running parallel to the brain surface, ending in fine tufted branches, plus a long horizontal axon running sideways within the topmost sheet of tissue.

In humans the picture is richer than in a mouse, because the human marginal zone is far thicker and contains several distinct reelin producing shapes alongside the classic horizontal one.

SIZE:

Small to medium, with a cell body typically ten to twenty micrometres across, a fraction of the size of the giant projection cells described elsewhere in this chapter.

The horizontal axon is disproportionately long for such a small cell, spreading its signal far along the surface.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Human studies at midgestation distinguish several reelin producing morphotypes in the marginal zone rather than one uniform cell.

By origin there are also several populations, arising from the cortical hem, the septum, the ventral pallium at the pallial to subpallial boundary, and the thalamic eminence, and a further human specialty is the subpial granular layer of Ranke, a transient sheet of small cells found in human but not rodent cortex.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The marginal zone, the outermost band of the developing cortex, which becomes layer 1 of the mature neocortex, across the whole neocortical surface, plus the hippocampus, where they are required for the hippocampal fissure to form, and the entorhinal cortex.

Their position is itself the point: they sit at the interface between the pia mater, the delicate membrane covering the brain, and the top of the cortical plate, exactly where the migrating neurons arrive.

NUMBER:

In the fetal marginal zone they are the dominant neuron of that layer, dense enough to establish a reelin gradient across the whole cortical surface, but in the adult they are a small remnant and no reliable adult total has been published.

A defensible upper bound comes from layer 1 as a whole, which holds well under one percent of cortical neurons, so the surviving population must be under ten million, under 0.06 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and the true figure is very likely far smaller.

Most human Cajal-Retzius cells begin degenerating around gestational weeks 23 to 24, though persistent cells have been documented in people as old as 28.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They fire action potentials but with immature properties: a high input resistance, meaning a small current produces a large voltage change, and slow, broad spikes.

Early in development they are depolarised rather than quietened by gamma aminobutyric acid, the transmitter that becomes purely inhibitory later, so the usual brake acts as an accelerator, and their spontaneous activity helps drive the earliest patterned activity in the cortical sheet.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They receive both glutamatergic and gamma aminobutyric acid mediated inputs within the marginal zone and are contacted by the earliest ingrowing afferent fibres.

Their most important output is not a synapse at all but the secretion of reelin into the extracellular space, acting on the apical processes of every migrating neuron below them, although they also make conventional contacts onto the apical tufts of arriving pyramidal neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single long axon running horizontally within the marginal zone, sometimes for several millimetres, keeping both the reelin signal and the electrical signal spread evenly across the surface.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the transmitter of the classic Cajal-Retzius cell, but the functionally decisive secretion is not a transmitter at all: it is reelin, a very large extracellular protein released constitutively rather than at a synapse.

They also express the transcription factor p73 and the calcium binding protein calretinin, which are the markers used to identify them.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable, and here is why: Cajal-Retzius axons run entirely within the marginal zone during a period before myelination of the cortex has begun, and the cells are largely gone before oligodendrocytes arrive in force.

There is no myelinated Cajal-Retzius axon to describe, so this is a genuine absence rather than a gap in the record.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest for electrical work, because the cell is small and fires slowly.

The unusual load is secretory, because reelin is a very large glycoprotein and continuously manufacturing and exporting it demands a well developed endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus, the cell's protein factory and packaging plant.

PLASTICITY:

The whole population is plastic in the deepest sense, because its job is finished and then it is deleted by programmed cell death in late fetal and early postnatal life, after which a subpopulation of gamma aminobutyric acid producing interneurons takes over reelin production in smaller amounts.

Persistence varies by region, with high numbers lingering in the medial entorhinal cortex long after they have vanished from neighbouring cortex.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The critical partner is not a mature glial cell but radial glia, the long scaffolding cells stretching from ventricle to pial surface that act as climbing ropes for migrating neurons, and Cajal-Retzius cells sit at the pial end of every one of those ropes and terminate the climb.

Later, microglia clear the dying Cajal-Retzius cells, one of the earliest large scale clearance jobs in the brain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and there is no cortex to colour.

Without the reelin gradient, migrating neurons stop too early or overshoot, layer 1 fills with cells that should be deeper, and the neat inside out sequence collapses into a jumble, which is exactly what is seen in the reeler mouse and in human reelin mutations.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything this cell does is beyond spiking, because its main output is volume transmission of a secreted protein, a signal that diffuses through tissue and reaches many cells at once rather than travelling down a wire to one partner.

Reelin binds receptors on migrating neurons and triggers a phosphorylation cascade inside them, so the Cajal-Retzius cell is reprogramming other cells' internal chemistry rather than merely exciting them.

The cells are coupled to one another within the marginal zone network, and their early spontaneous activity helps synchronise the first cortical rhythms.

Finally, their own programmed death is an instructed, timed event, and delaying it disrupts the maturation of layer 1 inhibitory circuits.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic and gamma aminobutyric acid mediated synaptic input within the marginal zone, 0 to 1.

◦ Positional signals from the cortical hem and pial surface during migration into the marginal zone.

◦ A developmental clock input in gestational weeks, which triggers programmed death.

• OUTPUTS: reelin secreted into the extracellular space as a scalar concentration field in arbitrary units, plus conventional glutamatergic synapses onto arriving apical tufts.

• STATE: reelin synthesis rate, local reelin concentration in each depth slice, membrane voltage, survival flag.

• PARAMETERS: reelin diffusion coefficient, reelin degradation rate, stop threshold concentration, soma diameter 10 to 20 micrometres, onset of degeneration near gestational week 23.

• TIME CONSTANTS: reelin diffusion across the cortical thickness in minutes to hours, migration of one neuron in days, population lifetime in weeks to months, rare persistence in decades.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of reelin signalling produces inverted cortical layering, and in humans reelin mutations cause lissencephaly with cerebellar hypoplasia, while reduced reelin has been reported in schizophrenia, bipolar disorder and autism.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to build a reelin gradient from the pial surface and use it to stop each successive wave of migrating neurons, producing the inside out order of the cortical layers.
Create a list variable called reelin with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called level with initial value 20.
Repeat the following steps exactly 40 times.
    Add level to the end of reelin.
    Set level to the product of level and 0.8.
End the loop.
Create a number variable called stop depth with initial value 39.
Create a number variable called probe with initial value 0.
While probe is less than 40, repeat the following steps.
    If the item at position probe in reelin is less than 0.35, then perform the following actions.
        Set stop depth to the difference of probe and 1.
