        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    Set probe to the sum of probe and 1.
End the loop.
Create a list variable called settled depths with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called wave with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called final depth with initial value 0.
While wave is less than 6, repeat the following steps.
    Set final depth to the sum of stop depth and the difference of 5 and wave.
    Add final depth to the end of settled depths.
    Set wave to the sum of wave and 1.
End the loop.
Display settled depths to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is a small diffusion problem with a rule attached, and Python lists let the diffusion step and the stop rule sit side by side without any library.

DEPTH, D, DECAY, STOP = 40, 0.25, 0.01, 0.35

def run(secreting):
    reelin = [0.0] * DEPTH          # slice 0 is the pia, slice 39 the ventricle
    settled = []
    cells = [{"pos": 39.0, "born": 150 + i * 70, "state": "waiting"}
             for i in range(6)]
    for t in range(1500):
        if secreting:
            reelin[0] += 1.0        # Cajal-Retzius cells secrete at the surface
        nxt = list(reelin)
        for i in range(1, DEPTH - 1):
            nxt[i] = reelin[i] + D * (reelin[i-1] - 2*reelin[i] + reelin[i+1])
        nxt[0] = reelin[0] + D * (reelin[1] - reelin[0])
        nxt[-1] = reelin[-1] + D * (reelin[-2] - reelin[-1])
        reelin = [c * (1.0 - DECAY) for c in nxt]
        for c in cells:
            if c["state"] == "waiting" and t >= c["born"]:
                c["state"] = "climbing"
            if c["state"] != "climbing":
                continue
            c["pos"] -= 0.08                    # climbing the radial glial rope
            if c["pos"] <= 0.0:
                c["pos"], c["state"] = 0.0, "in the marginal zone"
            elif reelin[int(c["pos"])] >= STOP:
                c["state"] = "settled"
                for old in settled:
                    old["pos"] += 1.0           # the plate grows toward the pia
                settled.append(c)
    return [round(c["pos"], 1) for c in cells]

print("reelin present:", run(True))
print("reelin absent :", run(False))

Run, this prints [25.0, 24.0, 23.0, 22.0, 21.0, 20.0] with reelin, a perfect inside out stack, and all zeros without it, every cell piled into the marginal zone.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small horizontal neuron lying flat in the very top sheet of fetal cortex, with one thick dendrite parallel to the surface, a long horizontal axon, and staining positive for both reelin and the transcription factor p73.

Nothing else in the brain lies down flat right under the pia and hands out a stop signal to everyone below it.

CHAPTER 18. SUBPLATE NEURONS (First-Born Wiring-Guide Neuron) (NON-COG)

For a few months before birth, the busiest circuit in the human brain is one that will mostly be gone by your first birthday.

Subplate is an address and nothing more, naming a temporary shelf of tissue beneath the growing cortex rather than any work the cell does.

First-Born records the hardest fact about them: they are among the very first cortical neurons generated, appearing around embryonic days eleven to thirteen in the mouse, long before the layer four cells they serve have even migrated into place.

Wiring-Guide names the job proved by lesion - incoming thalamic axons pause in the subplate for weeks in a documented waiting period, and removing these neurons from kitten visual cortex prevents thalamic axons from segregating in layer four and blocks ocular dominance columns from forming.

Neuron marks it as a fundamental cortical cell, though most of these early-born cells persist as the core of adult layer six b rather than simply dying.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Subplate neurons are the first cortical neurons to be born and the first to become functional, occupying a temporary layer just below the developing cortex, and their job is to run the cortex before the cortex can run itself.

Incoming fibres from the thalamus, the great relay station at the centre of the brain, park in the subplate for weeks in what is called the waiting period, forming working synapses there before they are allowed up into the cortical plate.

During that time subplate neurons amplify spontaneous activity, teach layer 4 how to respond, and are absolutely required for the formation of ocular dominance columns, the stripes of cortex serving each eye.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are a mixed bag rather than a single shape, with polymorphic, inverted pyramidal, fusiform, multipolar and horizontal forms all described, and they mature early, so at a stage when cortical plate neurons still look primitive the subplate neurons already have elaborate dendrites and clear axons.

In humans the layer they occupy is the thickest transient compartment of the whole fetal brain, several times thicker than the cortical plate above it at its peak.

SIZE:

Medium sized, commonly quoted in the range of ten to thirty micrometres for the soma across the different morphological types.

The survivors in adult white matter are small to medium neurons scattered thinly through the cabling.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

By chemistry rather than shape there are two great divisions.

One is excitatory and glutamatergic, marked by connective tissue growth factor and the nuclear receptor NURR1, and it is these cells that mostly persist as layer 6b; the other is inhibitory, using gamma aminobutyric acid, and includes cells containing neuropeptide Y, somatostatin, and nitric oxide synthase, the enzyme that makes the gas nitric oxide.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Before birth, the subplate zone, immediately beneath the cortical plate and above the intermediate zone, across the entire neocortex and thickest under the association areas of the frontal and temporal lobes.

After birth the survivors live in two places, layer 6b, the very deepest sliver of grey matter, and the subcortical white matter as interstitial neurons, so their address is defined by an interface, the grey matter to white matter boundary, which is precisely why they are so easy to overlook.

NUMBER:

The adult survivors have been counted properly: Sedmak and Judas in 2019 measured about 594 million white matter interstitial neurons in the whole adult human subcortical white matter, with a standard deviation of about 265 million, and published estimates run from about 450 to 670 million.

That is roughly 1.3 to 3.5 percent of the number of cortical neurons, and it lies alongside rather than inside the 16.34 billion neocortical figure, because these cells are counted in white matter, not in the cortical sheet, while the layer 6b survivors do fall inside that total and are a small fraction of one percent of it.

Only about 10 to 20 percent of the original fetal subplate population survives, so the fetal population must have been several billion, and confidence in the adult figure is good while confidence in the fetal one is poor.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They mature electrically far earlier than any other cortical neuron, so they can spike while the cortical plate is still silent, and they generate and amplify spindle bursts, brief oscillatory volleys that sweep the immature cortex and are the fetal brain's main form of self stimulation.

They have long membrane time constants and high input resistance, so small inputs produce large responses, which suits an amplifier.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They receive the first thalamocortical axons, the first callosal axons crossing between the hemispheres, and the first ingrowing fibres from the brainstem carrying acetylcholine, serotonin and noradrenaline.

They project upward into the cortical plate, particularly onto layer 4, and some send pioneer axons the other way, down to the thalamus, laying the first track that later cortical axons follow, while adult survivors keep sparse long range links between neighbouring cortical columns through the white matter.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Two directions matter: ascending axons deliver amplified thalamic drive into layer 4 during the waiting period, and descending pioneer axons reach the thalamus before any cortical plate axon does.

Those pioneers act as a guide wire for the entire corticothalamic system.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Both of the cortex's main transmitters, depending on subtype: glutamate for the excitatory majority and gamma aminobutyric acid for the inhibitory minority.

Peptides are prominent, especially neuropeptide Y and somatostatin, and a subset produces nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses freely through tissue and dilates blood vessels.

MYELINATION:

Largely unmyelinated during fetal life, because myelination of the human cortex barely begins before birth.

The adult interstitial survivors sit inside heavily myelinated white matter but their own thin axons are mostly unmyelinated, which makes them electrically slow neighbours in a very fast neighbourhood.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High relative to their neighbours during the waiting period, because they are the only electrically active cells in an otherwise quiet cortex.

That early activity is the leading explanation for why they are so selectively killed by a shortage of oxygen or blood in premature infants.

PLASTICITY:

They are the substrate of the first great wave of cortical plasticity and then largely delete themselves, with programmed cell death removing the majority in late fetal and early postnatal life and the timing of that death setting the closing edge of the earliest critical periods.

Survivors retain some plasticity, and abnormal survival or misplacement is one of the more reproducible findings in the brains of people with schizophrenia.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here behave differently from glia in the cortical plate above, because the subplate is rich in extracellular matrix molecules, the scaffolding gel between cells, which makes it a permissive corridor where growing axons can pause and wait rather than the tightly packed plate above.

Microglia colonise the subplate early, clear the vast numbers of dying subplate neurons, and are also the cells whose activation in preterm brain injury damages the survivors.

Around adult interstitial neurons, oligodendrocytes dominate the neighbourhood entirely, so these neurons live as isolated grey matter cells inside a white matter world.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove subplate neurons before birth and the cortex above them never wires up properly: thalamic axons fail to find their targets, ocular dominance columns never form, and layer 4 never learns its job, so what is lost is not a flavour in an adult region but the entire recipe.

In the adult, removing the interstitial survivors would take away a thin, slow, peptide rich cross talk running through the white matter between cortical areas.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The subplate is the clearest case in the brain of a cell type whose function is to be a temporary scaffold and then disappear on schedule.

They instruct other neurons through activity, since blocking subplate firing prevents ocular dominance columns from forming even though the axons are physically present.

The nitric oxide producing subset controls local blood flow directly, coupling early neural activity to the growing vasculature, while the pioneer axons perform structural guidance, a computation written in geometry rather than in spikes.

In rodents, layer 6b neurons are among the very few cortical cells directly excited by orexin, also called hypocretin, the peptide that keeps you awake, which hints at a role in arousal, though whether the human survivors share this is untested.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: thalamocortical axon activity, 0 to 1, including spontaneous retinal waves before the eyes open.

◦ Callosal input from the opposite hemisphere, 0 to 1.

◦ Brainstem neuromodulator tone for acetylcholine, serotonin and noradrenaline, each 0 to 1.

◦ A developmental clock in gestational weeks, which sets the handover fraction.

• OUTPUTS: amplified drive to layer 4 of the cortical plate, descending pioneer axon activity to thalamus, and nitric oxide released into the local tissue volume.

• STATE: membrane voltage, burst count, handover fraction from 0 to 1, survival flag, surrounding extracellular matrix density.

• PARAMETERS: amplification gain about 3.0 falling to 0 across the handover, burst threshold near 1.2 in normalised units, survival fraction 0.10 to 0.20, adult interstitial population about 594 million.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds, spindle burst 0.5 to 3 seconds, waiting period several weeks, handover across gestational weeks 22 to 32, programmed death over months.

• FAILURE MODE: hypoxic and ischaemic injury in premature infants kills subplate neurons selectively and is a leading cause of cerebral palsy and later cognitive impairment; abnormal numbers and placement of interstitial neurons are reported in schizophrenia; focal cortical dysplasia and some epilepsies involve retained clusters of these cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run the subplate as a waiting room and amplifier for thalamic axons and then to hand its job over to layer four.
Create a number variable called subplate voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called handover with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called week with initial value 24.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called thalamic input with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called burst count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive to layer four with initial value 0.
If week is greater than 22, then perform the following actions.
    Set handover to the quotient of the difference of week and 22 divided by 10.
End the conditional block.
Set gain to the product of 3 and the difference of 1 and handover.
While clock is less than 500, repeat the following steps.
    Set thalamic input to 0.
    If the remainder of clock divided by 100 is less than 20, then perform the following actions.
        Set thalamic input to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set subplate voltage to the sum of subplate voltage and the quotient of the difference of the product of gain and thalamic input and subplate voltage divided by 25.
    Set drive to layer four to the sum of drive to layer four and the product of handover and thalamic input.
    If subplate voltage is greater than 1.2, then perform the following actions.
        Set subplate voltage to 0.
        Set burst count to the sum of burst count and 1.
        Set drive to layer four to the sum of drive to layer four and the difference of 1 and handover.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display burst count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the essential story is a state machine that changes its own role over developmental time, and a small class makes that handover explicit.

class Subplate:
    """A transient amplifier that trains layer 4 and then stands down."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.v = 0.0
        self.tau = 25.0     # long time constant: an immature, leaky cell
        self.bursts = 0

    def step(self, thalamic, week, dt=1.0):
        # the waiting period: afferents pile up in the subplate first
        handover = 0.0 if week < 22 else min(1.0, (week - 22) / 10.0)
        gain = 3.0 * (1.0 - handover)
        self.v += dt * ((gain * thalamic - self.v) / self.tau)
        fired = 0
        if self.v > 1.2:
            self.v = 0.0
            self.bursts += 1
            fired = 1
        relayed = (1.0 - handover) * fired      # subplate to layer 4
        direct = handover * thalamic            # thalamus straight to layer 4
        return relayed + direct

for week in (20, 24, 28, 32):
    cell, drive_to_l4 = Subplate(), 0.0
    for t in range(500):
        wave = 1.0 if (t % 100) < 20 else 0.0   # spontaneous retinal wave
        drive_to_l4 += cell.step(wave, week)
    print("week %d: subplate bursts %2d, total drive reaching layer 4 %5.1f"
          % (week, cell.bursts, drive_to_l4))

Run, this prints 5 bursts and 5.0 units of drive at week 20, then 5 and 24.0, then 0 and 60.0, then 0 and 100.0: the subplate carries the whole load, then quietly stands down.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Before birth, a mature looking neuron with a full dendritic tree sitting in a thick, loose, matrix rich band directly beneath a cortical plate whose own cells still look primitive.

After birth, a lone neuron stranded in the white matter, positive for connective tissue growth factor or neuropeptide Y, surrounded by nothing but myelin and oligodendrocytes, with no cortical layer to call home.

CHAPTER 19. CORTICAL BASKET CELLS (COG)

A basket cell weaves its axon around the cell bodies of hundreds of neighbours like wickerwork, and with one spike it mutes the lot of them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A basket cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron: it releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main braking chemical, making its targets less likely to fire.

What makes it special is where it brakes, on the cell body and thick nearby dendrites, a zone called perisomatic, which is the last place a decision to fire can be overruled.

Sitting at that choke point, it does not merely reduce activity, it dictates the millisecond at which its neighbours may speak.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is round with several thick dendrites, the receiving branches, radiating in all directions, a pattern called multipolar.

Those dendrites are smooth, lacking the tiny mushroom-shaped spines that excitatory cells use.

The axon, the output cable, breaks immediately into a dense horizontal thicket that stays largely within the home layer and ends in clusters of swellings cupping other cells' somata.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 25 micrometres across, roughly a quarter of a hair's width.

Small basket cells spread their axon about 300 micrometres sideways, while large ones reach a millimetre or more, and axons within a single layer have been traced several millimetres; total axonal length runs to several centimetres packed into a volume smaller than a grain of salt.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Small basket cells, axon confined to the home layer and column, giving tight private inhibition.

• Nest basket cells, intermediate in reach, whose terminals form loose nests rather than tight baskets.

• Large basket cells, crossing both columns and layers to inhibit a slab of cortex hundreds of micrometres wide.

• Cholecystokinin basket cells, a chemically distinct family named for the peptide cholecystokinin they carry, which fire more slowly, release GABA less reliably, and are switched off by cannabis-like molecules the brain makes itself.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Basket cells occupy layers 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 of the whole neocortex, the six-layered sheet covering the hemispheres, and are essentially absent from layer 1.

They are present in every cortical area: primary visual, auditory, somatosensory, motor, prefrontal, insular and cingulate cortex and all association territories.

They are dense at two interfaces in particular, the layer 4 to layer 3 boundary where thalamic sensory input is handed upward, and the layer 5 to layer 6 boundary where cortical output is assembled.

NUMBER:

Parvalbumin-containing basket cells are reported to be fewer than 8 percent of all neocortical neurons.

Against this section's budget of 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and 3.2 billion inhibitory ones, a defensible estimate is about 600 million, range 480 to 800 million: roughly 3.7 percent of neocortical neurons, about 19 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons, and about 0.7 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence is moderate, because the fraction is well constrained in rodent, monkey and sampled human tissue but nobody has counted human basket cells brain-wide.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This is the classic fast-spiking neuron, firing 100 to 200 spikes per second for long stretches with almost no slowing.

Its action potential is unusually narrow, 0.2 to 0.4 milliseconds at half height, because potassium channels of the Kv3 family repolarise the membrane extremely fast.

A short membrane time constant of 5 to 10 milliseconds and a low input resistance of 60 to 150 megaohms make it a coincidence detector rather than a patient accumulator, and its fast upstroke depends on Nav1.1 sodium channels encoded by the gene SCN1A.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives on the smooth dendrites from nearby pyramidal cells, from thalamic fibres carrying sensory data, and from long-range cortical axons.

Output reaches the somata and proximal dendrites of hundreds, by some estimates more than a thousand, pyramidal cells plus other basket cells, through GABA type A receptors rich in the alpha-1 subunit that open and close within a few milliseconds.

Human basket cells also synapse on themselves: robust perisomatic self-innervation, called an autapse, is common in human and mouse supragranular basket cells and prolongs their own silence after each spike.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres, and branches many hundreds of times in a small volume, with terminal swellings clustered in strings of three to eight around each target soma.

Conduction along these local axons is fast, and measured velocity rises with the amount of myelin present.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is GABA, built by the enzyme glutamic acid decarboxylase 67 and loaded into vesicles by the vesicular GABA transporter.

Parvalbumin basket cells carry parvalbumin, not a transmitter but a fast calcium sponge that shortens the calcium signal and keeps release crisp; cholecystokinin basket cells co-release that peptide and are silenced from the target's side by endocannabinoids acting on cannabinoid receptor type 1.

MYELINATION:

Unusually for an inhibitory cell, the proximal axon is myelinated in patches, myelin being the fatty insulating sheath that speeds conduction.

The sheath is discontinuous, with long bare stretches between short internodes, and mitochondria, the cell's power plants, gather beneath those internodes, an arrangement that looks tuned for timing precision rather than raw speed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

These are among the most energy-hungry neurons in the cortex, holding more and larger mitochondria than neighbouring interneurons, enriched in cytochrome c oxidase, the final enzyme of the oxygen-burning chain.

The bill comes from pumping sodium and potassium back after high-frequency firing and from sustaining a high probability of release, and gamma-band synchrony collapses quickly when that energy supply is disturbed.

PLASTICITY:

The maturation of these cells opens and closes critical periods, the windows in early life when cortex is most reshapeable by experience.

Their axonal arbors remodel extensively during development, and in adulthood the perineuronal net encasing them limits further structural change, so dissolving that net experimentally restores juvenile plasticity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The distinctive support here is the perineuronal net, a stiff lattice of sugar-coated proteins including aggrecan and brevican, secreted jointly by astrocytes and the neuron, wrapping the soma like a string bag.

No other cortical cell is so consistently enwrapped, and the net does two jobs: it pins perisomatic synapses in place and it absorbs the oxidative damage this cell's furious metabolism generates.

Oligodendrocytes also treat these axons differently, laying short discontinuous internodes instead of the long uniform ones they build elsewhere.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete basket cells and the region loses its clock.

Gamma rhythms, the 30 to 80 cycles per second oscillation that binds the features of a percept together, are paced by these cells, and without them cortical activity becomes an unsynchronised murmur in which everything fires a little and nothing fires together.

The region also loses gain control, so a weak stimulus and a strong one would produce nearly the same saturated response.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Basket cells are wired to each other by gap junctions, protein tunnels built largely of connexin 36 that pass current directly, so a network of them synchronises within a millisecond.

The autapse gives each cell a private negative feedback loop limiting its own burst length, and cholecystokinin basket cells are shut off retrogradely when a depolarised target manufactures endocannabinoids on the spot and sends them backwards across the synapse for seconds at a time.

Parvalbumin buffers calcium inside the terminal and so shapes short-term plasticity from within, and these axons help instruct oligodendrocytes where to place myelin.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic conductance from local pyramidal cells (0 to 20 nanosiemens); thalamocortical drive (0 to 8 nanosiemens, latency 1 to 3 milliseconds); acetylcholine and noradrenaline tone (0 to 1 normalised); gap junction current from coupled basket cells (coupling coefficient 0.02 to 0.15).

• OUTPUTS: GABA type A conductance onto pyramidal somata and proximal dendrites, 1 to 5 nanosiemens per connection, reversal minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts, onto 300 to 1500 targets; autaptic conductance onto self of similar size.

• STATE: membrane potential, sodium and potassium gating variables, autaptic conductance, short-term depression factor, intracellular calcium.

• PARAMETERS: membrane time constant 5 to 10 milliseconds; input resistance 60 to 150 megaohms; threshold minus 45 to minus 40 millivolts; spike half-width 0.2 to 0.4 milliseconds; maximum sustained rate 100 to 200 hertz; release probability 0.4 to 0.9.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibitory decay 4 to 10 milliseconds; autaptic decay about 4 milliseconds; refractory period about 1 millisecond; gamma cycle 12 to 33 milliseconds; perineuronal net turnover, weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: in schizophrenia these cells show reduced glutamic acid decarboxylase 67 messenger ribonucleic acid and thinned perineuronal nets, and gamma synchrony and working memory degrade; in Dravet syndrome, loss of one SCN1A copy stops them sustaining firing and the cortex tips into seizure.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run one fast-spiking basket cell that brakes itself through its own autapse, and to report how many inhibitory pulses it delivers.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called self inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called refractory countdown with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 400 times.
    Calculate the difference of 40 and self inhibition.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.06.
    Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
    Set voltage to the result.
    Calculate the product of self inhibition and 0.88.
    Set self inhibition to the result.
    If refractory countdown is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of refractory countdown and 1.
        Set refractory countdown to the result.
        Set voltage to -70.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    If voltage is greater than or equal to -42, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
        Calculate the sum of self inhibition and 22.
        Set self inhibition to the result.
        Set refractory countdown to 2.
        Set voltage to -70.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a whole-cortex simulation holds hundreds of millions of these cells stepping every 0.05 milliseconds, so this inner loop must compile to a handful of machine instructions.

#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    /* Fast-spiking basket cell with a GABAergic autapse on its own soma. */
    const double dt = 0.05;         /* ms per step */
    const double tau_m = 6.0;       /* membrane time constant, ms */
    const double v_rest = -70.0;    /* mV */
    const double v_thresh = -42.0;  /* mV */
    const double e_gaba = -75.0;    /* chloride reversal, mV */
    const double tau_aut = 4.0;     /* autaptic decay, ms */
    const double g_aut_step = 6.0;  /* conductance added per own spike */
    const double drive = 40.0;      /* steady excitatory drive, mV */
    double v = v_rest, g_aut = 0.0;
    int refrac = 0, spikes = 0;

    for (int step = 0; step < 8000; step++) {  /* 8000 steps = 400 ms */
        g_aut -= g_aut * dt / tau_aut;         /* self-inhibition fades */
        if (refrac > 0) { refrac--; v = v_rest; continue; }
        double inhib = g_aut * (e_gaba - v);   /* the cell braking itself */
        v += dt * ((v_rest - v) + drive + inhib) / tau_m;
        if (v >= v_thresh) {
            spikes++;
            v = v_rest;
            g_aut += g_aut_step;
            refrac = (int)(1.0 / dt);          /* 1 ms absolute refractory */
        }
    }
    printf("spikes in 400 ms: %d (%.1f Hz)\n", spikes, spikes / 0.4);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a smooth-dendrite multipolar cell whose axon forms tight cups of boutons around its neighbours' cell bodies, which fires at 150 spikes per second without tiring, whose spikes are half as wide as anyone else's, and which sits inside a visible mesh cage of perineuronal net.

Nothing else in the cortex combines a basket-shaped terminal field, parvalbumin, and non-adapting fast spiking.

CHAPTER 20. CHANDELIER CELLS, ALSO CALLED AXO-AXONIC CELLS (COG)

The chandelier cell hangs vertical strings of transmitter-releasing beads through the cortex like candlesticks on a light fitting, and every string clamps the one spot on another neuron where its spikes are born.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A chandelier cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's principal braking chemical.

It is defined by one fact: it contacts only the axon initial segment of its targets, the short starting stretch of the output cable where an action potential is actually triggered, which is why it is also called an axo-axonic cell.

Holding that single spot, it can license or forbid a pyramidal cell's spike without touching anything else about that cell's computation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is small to medium and often sits at the top of layer 2, with dendrites climbing into layer 1 and descending into layer 3.

Its axon divides into vertically oriented strings of boutons called cartridges, each running alongside one target's axon initial segment.

The parallel array of cartridges looks exactly like the candle holders of a chandelier, which is how the cell was named in the 1970s.

SIZE:

The cell body measures 10 to 20 micrometres.

A cartridge is 10 to 40 micrometres long, a single labelled chandelier cell contributes about two to three boutons per target initial segment, one cell forms several hundred cartridges, and in monkey prefrontal cortex bouton density peaks near 750 per square millimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Superficial chandelier cells with somata at the layer 1 to layer 2 border, the commonest form in human cortex.

• Deep chandelier cells in layers 5 and 6 with shorter, denser cartridges.

• A population enriched at the layer 3 to layer 4 border in human temporal cortex, matching the transcriptomic cluster labelled inhibitory layer 2 to 5 PVALB SCUBE3, which expresses the chandelier marker genes UNC5B, NOG and COL15A1.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Chandelier cells occupy layers 2 through 6 of the whole neocortex, densest in layer 2 and upper layer 3, and are sparse or absent from layer 4 in rodents.

They are found in prefrontal, temporal, parietal, occipital, motor and cingulate cortex, with equivalent cells in hippocampus and amygdala that fall outside this section's budget.

The interface they occupy is not between two brain regions but between two compartments of one neuron: the boundary where a pyramidal cell's body ends and its axon begins.

NUMBER:

Chandelier cells are consistently described as fewer than 5 percent of cortical interneurons.

Against 3.2 billion inhibitory neocortical neurons that gives about 100 million, range 60 to 160 million: roughly 0.6 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, about 3 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons, and about 0.12 percent of all neurons in the nervous system.

Confidence is low to moderate, because chandelier cells are hard to sample and were explicitly under-sampled even in the largest human patch-sequencing study.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are fast-spiking but measurably unlike basket cells, with higher input resistance of 150 to 300 megaohms, a more depolarised resting potential near minus 65 millivolts, spike half-widths of 0.3 to 0.5 milliseconds, more adaptation, a tendency to stutter, and lower maximum rates of 50 to 150 hertz.

They often fire spontaneously between stimuli, which matters because the clamp on the initial segment is therefore applied by default rather than on request.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive on dendrites in layers 1 to 3 from local pyramidal cells, long-range cortical axons and neuromodulatory fibres.

Output goes exclusively to axon initial segments: each chandelier cell contacts 35 to 50 percent of the pyramidal neurons in its axonal field, and each pyramidal neuron is contacted by roughly four different chandelier cells.

The receiving GABA type A receptors there are enriched in the alpha-2 subunit, a signature distinguishing these synapses from perisomatic ones.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is fine, largely unmyelinated in its terminal arbor, and organised vertically into cartridges rather than spread horizontally.

Its total length is less than a large basket cell's but its placement precision is far greater, since each bouton must land within a few micrometres, and cartridge development is guided by neuregulin acting on the ErbB4 receptor with the intracellular partner DOCK7 over a narrow window running in mice from about postnatal day 12 to day 18.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is GABA and the cells carry parvalbumin.

The twist is that GABA at the axon initial segment is not reliably inhibitory: that compartment lacks much of the potassium-chloride cotransporter KCC2 that pumps chloride out, so local chloride is high and the GABA reversal potential sits near minus 55 millivolts instead of the usual minus 75.

The synapse therefore depolarises a resting cell toward minus 55 millivolts while shunting a cell that has climbed above it, making the chandelier cell a voltage clamp rather than a simple brake.

MYELINATION:

The terminal cartridges are unmyelinated, necessarily, since myelin would physically block boutons from reaching the target membrane.

The proximal axon may carry short myelin patches as in other parvalbumin cells, though this is less documented; the relevant myelin is on the other side of the synapse, because the target's initial segment ends where its own myelin begins and the cartridge occupies the bare stretch before it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High but below basket cells, because these cells fire fast yet adapt and stutter instead of sustaining maximal rates.

They depend on oxidative phosphorylation, the oxygen-burning route to cellular energy, and their burden is concentrated in a terminal arbor whose thousands of release sites must be kept loaded.

PLASTICITY:

Axo-axonic synapses are strikingly plastic in structure.

Raising the activity of either the pyramidal cell or the chandelier cell during the developmental window causes a reversible loss of axo-axonic connections, a homeostatic rule matching inhibition to the target's excitability, and in adult visual cortex experience continues to reshape chandelier function and cartridge structure.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes near the axon initial segment face an unusual task: they must keep extracellular potassium and chloride stable around a compartment that deliberately runs a non-standard chloride gradient.

Perineuronal nets around chandelier somata are present but generally lighter than around basket cells, and it is honest to say the specialisation here is structural rather than glial, since it is the ankyrin-G scaffold inside the target's initial segment, not any glial cell, that defines where a cartridge may attach.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove chandelier cells and the cortex loses its veto.

Pyramidal cells would still be inhibited on their bodies and dendrites, so activity would not explode, but the power to cancel an already-decided output at the last possible moment would vanish, along with fine control of spike timing at the start of a cortical volley.

Layers 2 and 3, where they are densest, would send leakier, less crisp output to the rest of the brain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The headline non-classical feature is compartment-specific chloride physiology, in which the same transmitter depolarises or hyperpolarises depending on which micron of membrane it lands on.

Their effect is state dependent, flipping with arousal and with the target's membrane potential, and they are gap-junction coupled to other parvalbumin cells.

They perform structural homeostasis by adding and removing whole cartridges, and their targets can fight back by relocating the initial segment along the axon, sliding the trigger zone out from under the cartridge.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: local glutamatergic excitation onto layer 1 to 3 dendrites (0 to 12 nanosiemens); long-range cortico-cortical input (0 to 5 nanosiemens); cholinergic and serotonergic modulation (0 to 1 normalised); arousal state (0 to 1).

• OUTPUTS: GABA type A conductance onto the axon initial segment of 200 to 600 pyramidal neurons, 0.5 to 3 nanosiemens per target, local reversal minus 60 to minus 50 millivolts.

• STATE: membrane potential, adaptation current, stutter phase, per-target cartridge count, target initial segment chloride concentration.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 150 to 300 megaohms; resting potential about minus 65 millivolts; sustained firing 50 to 150 hertz; boutons per target 2 to 10; fraction of local pyramidal cells contacted 0.35 to 0.50.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibitory decay 5 to 12 milliseconds; chloride accumulation and recovery at the initial segment 0.1 to 1 second; cartridge structural turnover, days.

• FAILURE MODE: in schizophrenia the density of cartridges detectable by the GABA transporter GAT-1 falls about 40 percent in prefrontal cortex while the alpha-2 receptor subunit on initial segments rises, read as failed compensation; chandelier numbers are reduced and boutons smaller in prefrontal cortex in autism; in epilepsy, sustained firing with chloride loading can turn the veto into a trigger.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to work out whether a chandelier cell input helps or hinders a pyramidal cell, given the high chloride level at the axon initial segment.
Create a number variable called target voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called driving force with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called net current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called chandelier active with initial value true.
Repeat the following steps exactly 500 times.
    Calculate the difference of -55 and target voltage.
    Set driving force to the result.
    If chandelier active is not true, then perform the following actions.
        Set driving force to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of 0.8 and driving force.
    Set net current to the result.
    Calculate the sum of net current and 22.
    Set net current to the result.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and target voltage.
    Calculate the sum of net current and the result.
    Set net current to the result.
    Calculate the product of net current and 0.08.
    Calculate the sum of target voltage and the result.
    Set target voltage to the result.
    If target voltage is greater than or equal to -50, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
        Set target voltage to -70.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is one line of arithmetic about chloride, and Python makes that line impossible to misread.

DT = 0.05          # milliseconds per step
E_LEAK = -70.0     # resting potential, mV
TAU = 12.0         # pyramidal membrane time constant, ms
V_THRESH = -50.0   # spike threshold, mV
E_CL_AIS = -55.0   # chloride reversal AT the axon initial segment, mV
G_CHC = 0.8        # chandelier conductance, normalised

def run(drive, chandelier_on, ms=500.0):
    """Return spike count and mean voltage of one pyramidal target."""
    v, spikes, v_sum = E_LEAK, 0, 0.0
    steps = int(ms / DT)
    for _ in range(steps):
        g = G_CHC if chandelier_on else 0.0
        # The sign is set by (E_CL_AIS - v), not by the word "inhibition".
        dv = (E_LEAK - v) + drive + g * (E_CL_AIS - v)
        v += DT * dv / TAU
        if v >= V_THRESH:
            spikes += 1
            v = E_LEAK
        v_sum += v
    return spikes, v_sum / steps

for drive in (8.0, 22.0):
    for on in (False, True):
        s, mv = run(drive, on)
        print(f"drive={drive:4.1f} chandelier={'on ' if on else 'off'} "
              f"spikes={s:4d} mean V={mv:7.2f} mV")

# Quiet cell: the chandelier DEPOLARISES it, from about -62 to about -59 mV.
# Driven cell: the same synapse CLAMPS it below threshold and abolishes firing.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Find a parvalbumin-positive cell whose axon forms vertical rows of beads, each row hugging the start of another cell's axon and nothing else.

The cartridge is the fingerprint: no other neuron in the human brain builds parallel candlestick arrays aimed at a single micron-scale target on hundreds of partners.

CHAPTER 21. TRANSLAMINAR CELLS (All-Layers Blanket-Damping Neuron) (COG)

Most cortical inhibitory cells work sideways within their own floor of the building; the translaminar cell drops its axon down the stairwell and switches off three floors at once.

Translaminar is an address in disguise, stating only that the axon crosses horizontal cortical layers.

All-Layers names the anatomy that matters functionally: these inhibitory cells sit mostly in the deep layers and send axons through the entire cortical thickness, from layer 6 up to layer 1, contacting pyramidal cells in every layer they pass.

Blanket-Damping names the work - using gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory chemical, these cells provide general gain control over the whole cortical column rather than a targeted veto of one layer.

Neuron marks it as a principal inhibitory cortical cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A translaminar cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), whose axon deliberately crosses the boundaries between cortical layers.

Laminar means layered, so translaminar simply means across the layers, and the name describes wiring geometry rather than chemistry; reviews of cortical parvalbumin neurons now treat these cells as the third named morphological class of fast-spiking interneuron alongside basket and chandelier cells.

Its job is vertical binding, making the six layers of one small patch of cortex reach a single decision instead of arguing floor by floor.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is multipolar with smooth, spine-free dendrites, like other fast-spiking cells.

The distinguishing feature is an axon that turns vertically after leaving the soma, running perpendicular to the cortical surface and shedding branches in each layer it passes.

In human tissue this pattern was recovered in a parvalbumin type labelled layer 4 to 5 MEPE, whose axons spanned layer 3 into layer 4 and were described as resembling mouse interlaminar or translaminar basket cells; the trunk may ascend, descend, or both.

SIZE:

Somata are 12 to 22 micrometres across.

Vertical axonal reach is the point, 400 to more than 1200 micrometres, enough to cross from layer 2 to layer 5 in the roughly 2.5 to 3 millimetre thickness of human neocortex, while horizontal spread stays narrow at 200 to 500 micrometres, giving a tall column-shaped envelope.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Descending parvalbumin translaminar cells, somata in layers 2 and 3, axons reaching layers 4 and 5.

• Ascending parvalbumin translaminar cells, somata in layers 5 and 6, axons climbing into layers 4 and 3.

• Somatostatin translaminar cells, a separate chemical family whose long vertical axons also cross layers but target dendrites rather than cell bodies.

• Translaminar basket variants, whose vertical axon still ends in perisomatic baskets in the destination layer.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata occur in layers 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 of the neocortex, with the axon by definition present in at least two layers.

The interfaces they cross are the ones that matter most: the layer 4 to layer 3 boundary where thalamic input is handed upward, the layer 3 to layer 5 boundary where the cortex commits to an output, and the layer 5 to layer 6 boundary where feedback to the thalamus is assembled.

They are reported in sensory, motor and association cortex, including human middle temporal gyrus, mouse visual cortex and mouse barrel cortex.

NUMBER:

No direct count exists.

Treating translaminar cells as a minority of parvalbumin and somatostatin interneurons gives an estimate of about 80 million in the human neocortex, with a wide range of 30 to 160 million, roughly 0.5 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 2.5 percent of the 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons.

Confidence is low and the reason must be stated plainly: the category is defined by axon geometry visible only in fully reconstructed cells, and full human reconstructions still number in the low hundreds.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The parvalbumin members are fast-spiking, 80 to 180 hertz, with spike half-widths of 0.25 to 0.45 milliseconds and membrane time constants of 6 to 12 milliseconds.

The somatostatin members are low-threshold spiking, with higher input resistance of 200 to 400 megaohms, a rebound burst on release from hyperpolarisation, and clear adaptation.

Both share one consequence of their geometry: the same spike arrives in different layers at different times, spread over one to five milliseconds of conduction delay.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input is dominated by the home layer, so the cell samples what its own floor is doing.

Output is distributed across layers, and the strongest documented case runs downward from layer 5, where activating layer 5 recruits fast-spiking neurons in the superficial layers about three times more strongly than excitatory ones, so translaminar recurrence from layer 5 suppresses the layers above.

A caution matters for any simulation: anatomy overstates function here, because layer 2 and 3 parvalbumin cells have abundant descending collaterals into layer 5 yet make very few working synapses there, so a model must never assume that a branch passing through a layer inhibits it.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, 0.2 to 0.6 micrometres, and unusually straight over its vertical run before branching, depositing boutons in layer-specific bands rather than uniformly along the trunk.

Conduction delays of one to five milliseconds across the depth of the cortex are the functionally important number, because they set the phase relationship between layers during an oscillation.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA in every case.

Parvalbumin translaminar cells carry parvalbumin; somatostatin translaminar cells carry the peptide somatostatin, which is itself released and acts slowly on receptors away from the synapse, and neither group is known to release a fast excitatory transmitter.

MYELINATION:

A long vertical trunk is the part of any interneuron axon most likely to be myelinated, and patchy myelination of parvalbumin interneuron axons is documented, with conduction velocity rising in step with the amount of myelin present.

Terminal branches within each target layer are bare, and direct measurements on identified human translaminar cells have not been published, so this must be treated as an inference from the wider parvalbumin literature.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for the fast-spiking members, comparable to basket cells, with abundant mitochondria and heavy dependence on oxygen-based energy production.

There is one added cost basket cells do not carry, maintaining a long axon trunk that requires continuous transport of proteins and mitochondria over a millimetre or more, while the slower somatostatin members are cheaper to run.

PLASTICITY:

Vertical inhibition is shaped by sensory experience during development, and the columnar organisation of cortex depends on it forming correctly.

Because the axon must find and stop in the correct layer, guidance and pruning errors appear as mislaminated inhibition rather than simple weakness; in adults, individual synapses change strength normally but layer targeting appears fixed.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Cortical astrocytes are themselves organised into layer-specific varieties, so a translaminar axon passes through several distinct glial environments in one run, and its branches in layer 1 meet astrocytes chemically unlike those meeting its branches in layer 5.

That is genuinely unusual, since most cortical neurons keep their entire axonal arbor inside one glial neighbourhood; otherwise oligodendrocyte and microglial support is not known to be special, and it is honest to say so.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take translaminar cells away and the cortical column stops being a column.

Layer 4 would still receive sensory input, layer 2 and 3 would still process it and layer 5 would still issue commands, but the three would drift out of register in time and the vertical alignment that makes a column respond to one stimulus as one unit would blur.

Concretely, the suppression layer 5 imposes on the superficial layers after it fires would vanish, and cortical activity would ring on instead of resetting.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The defining non-classical property is that a single spike produces a different result in each layer because of conduction delay, so the cell is a spatial delay line rather than a point inhibitor.

Parvalbumin members are gap-junction coupled to partners within their home layer, so a vertical message can be launched by a horizontally synchronised group.

Somatostatin members release a peptide by volume transmission, meaning it diffuses to receptors on cells they never touch, so their influence on a distant layer is part wiring and part chemistry, and their axons help instruct where oligodendrocytes place myelin along the trunk.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic excitation from the home layer (0 to 15 nanosiemens); weaker excitation from other layers (0 to 4 nanosiemens); neuromodulatory tone (0 to 1 normalised); gap junction current from same-layer partners.

• OUTPUTS: GABA type A conductance delivered to two or more named layers, 0.5 to 3 nanosiemens per connection, each with its own conduction delay; somatostatin peptide for the somatostatin members.

• STATE: membrane potential, adaptation current, one delay queue per target layer, per-layer short-term depression factors.

• PARAMETERS: vertical axon length 400 to 1200 micrometres; conduction velocity 0.2 to 0.8 metres per second; targets per layer 50 to 400; connection probability into a passed-through layer, which may be near zero and must therefore be a free parameter, never assumed.

• TIME CONSTANTS: conduction delay 1 to 5 milliseconds; inhibitory decay 5 to 12 milliseconds for parvalbumin members and 15 to 40 milliseconds for somatostatin members; adaptation 50 to 200 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of vertical inhibition lets activity in one layer recruit the others unchecked, the classic starting condition for a focal cortical seizure, and in schizophrenia weakened parvalbumin function is thought to desynchronise layers as well as cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to let one translaminar interneuron spread inhibition from its home layer to the layers above and below, each after its own conduction delay.
Create a number variable called cell voltage with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called layer delays with initial value empty list.
Add 3 to the end of layer delays.
Add 1 to the end of layer delays.
Add 5 to the end of layer delays.
Create a list variable called pending arrivals with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step number with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 200 times.
    Calculate the sum of step number and 1.
    Set step number to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of cell voltage divided by 6.
    Calculate the difference of 0.25 and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.5.
    Calculate the sum of cell voltage and the result.
    Set cell voltage to the result.
    If cell voltage is greater than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set cell voltage to 0.
        For each delay in layer delays, perform the following operations.
            Calculate the sum of step number and delay.
            Add the result to the end of pending arrivals.
        End the loop.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in pending arrivals.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because the essential idea is a set of per-layer delay queues, and JavaScript arrays express a queue clearly without any library.

const DT = 0.5;                        // milliseconds per step
const STEPS = 200;                     // 100 ms of simulated time
const LAYERS = ["L2/3", "L4", "L5"];
const DELAY_MS = [1.5, 0.5, 2.5];      // conduction delay from the L4 soma
const TAU_I = 8.0;                     // decay of delivered inhibition, ms
const DRIVE = 0.25;                    // excitation in the home layer

const queues = LAYERS.map(() => []);   // pending arrivals, one queue per layer
const inhib = LAYERS.map(() => 0);     // inhibition present in each layer
let v = 0, spikes = 0;                 // normalised potential, threshold 1

for (let step = 0; step < STEPS; step++) {
  const t = step * DT;
  v += DT * (-v / 6.0 + DRIVE);        // fast-spiking membrane, tau = 6 ms
  if (v >= 1.0) {
    v = 0;
    spikes++;
    LAYERS.forEach((_, i) => queues[i].push(t + DELAY_MS[i]));  // one spike, three futures
  }
  LAYERS.forEach((_, i) => {
    inhib[i] *= Math.exp(-DT / TAU_I);
    while (queues[i].length > 0 && queues[i][0] <= t) {
      queues[i].shift();
      inhib[i] += 1.0;                 // the spike finally lands here
    }
  });
}
LAYERS.forEach((name, i) =>
  console.log(name.padEnd(5), "delay", DELAY_MS[i], "ms  residual inhibition",
              inhib[i].toFixed(3)));
console.log("translaminar spikes fired:", spikes);

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Reconstruct the whole cell and look at the shape of the axon cloud: if it is taller than it is wide, and deposits boutons in two or more named layers while the dendrites stay in one, it is translaminar.

A basket cell's arbor is a horizontal disc, a chandelier cell's is a forest of short candlesticks, and a translaminar cell's is a narrow vertical column punched through the cortical sheet.

CHAPTER 22. MARTINOTTI CELLS (COG)

Carlo Martinotti drew this cell in 1889 as a neuron that sends its axon the wrong way - upward toward the brain's surface. A century later, we learned it climbs up there to switch off the antennae of every excited cell nearby.

The cell that silences a pyramidal neuron's own dendrites when it fires fast.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Martinotti cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) together with the peptide somatostatin.

Its signature is an ascending axon that climbs to layer 1, the thin outermost layer of the cortex, and spreads horizontally there, which is where pyramidal cells park their apical tufts, the fine upper branches that collect feedback and context from the rest of the brain.

Its best-known behaviour is lateral inhibition on demand: when one pyramidal cell fires a fast burst, the Martinotti cell it excites turns around and dampens the neighbouring pyramidal cells, an effect called frequency-dependent disynaptic inhibition.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is small to medium and often bitufted, meaning two dendritic bundles emerge from opposite poles like the tufts on an ear of wheat.

The axon ascends as a trunk through the layers above, then branches profusely into a broad horizontal plexus in layer 1 that spreads into neighbouring cortical columns.

Along the way it also leaves collaterals in the layers it passes, so it inhibits nearer dendrites as well as distant tufts.

SIZE:

Somata measure 10 to 20 micrometres across.

The ascending axon runs 500 to 1500 micrometres vertically in human cortex and the layer 1 plexus can extend 500 to over 1000 micrometres horizontally, reaching beyond the parent column, for a total axonal length of several centimetres, much of it spent simply climbing.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• T-shaped Martinotti cells, typically in layer 5, whose axon rises as one bare trunk and only branches on arrival in layer 1, giving the letter T.

• Fanning-out Martinotti cells, marked by the calcium-binding protein calretinin and corresponding to the somatostatin CALB2 transcriptomic type, branching on the way up and arborising in layers 2 and 3 as well as layer 1.

• Deep-layer Martinotti cells in layers 5b and 6, associated with the somatostatin MYH8 type.

• In human cortex, classic Martinotti shapes were found at the layer 2 to layer 3 border and within layer 3, alongside a sparse variant with unusually few axon branches and a strong descending component.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata sit in layers 2, 3, 5 and 6 of the neocortex, most abundantly in layers 2 and 3 and in layer 5, with the axon terminating in layer 1.

They are present throughout the neocortex, including primary visual, auditory and somatosensory cortex, motor cortex, and prefrontal and temporal association areas.

The interface they own is the layer 1 boundary, the meeting point between cortex and the incoming feedback fibres from other cortical areas and from higher-order thalamus, and they are the only inhibitory cell whose job is defined by delivering local inhibition into that foreign territory.

NUMBER:

Somatostatin cells are consistently reported as about 30 percent of cortical GABAergic neurons, and Martinotti cells are the most abundant somatostatin subtype.

Against 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons that gives about 450 million Martinotti cells, range 320 to 640 million, roughly 2.8 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 14 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons.

Confidence is moderate for the somatostatin total and lower for the Martinotti share of it, because the Martinotti label is morphological and human sampling has been biased toward the upper layers.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Martinotti cells are low-threshold spiking, beginning to fire at unusually negative voltages because of low-threshold calcium channels of the T type.

Input resistance is high at 200 to 500 megaohms, so small currents move them a long way, and the membrane time constant is long at 20 to 40 milliseconds, making them patient integrators rather than coincidence detectors.

They adapt strongly, show a voltage sag during hyperpolarisation caused by the current known as Ih, rebound with a burst when released from inhibition, and rest near minus 60 millivolts, close enough to threshold to be recruited easily.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The input synapse from pyramidal cells is the most distinctive in the cortex: it starts extremely weak and strengthens several-fold within a burst, a property called strong short-term facilitation.

The mechanism is a protein handshake across the synapse, in which Elfn1 on the Martinotti cell's surface grabs and constitutively switches on the presynaptic receptor mGluR7, holding initial release probability very low.

Output reaches the apical tufts and apical dendrites of pyramidal cells across several columns and also other interneurons, while the Martinotti cell is itself inhibited by vasoactive intestinal peptide cells and by parvalbumin cells to differing degrees.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, 0.1 to 0.4 micrometres, with a long unbranched ascending stretch and a dense terminal plexus of small boutons contacting fine dendritic shafts rather than cell bodies.

Conduction is slow, roughly 0.1 to 0.5 metres per second, adding several milliseconds between the Martinotti spike and the arrival of inhibition in layer 1.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the fast transmitter and somatostatin the co-released peptide, acting on slow receptors that can sit far from the release site.

Group I metabotropic glutamate receptors, especially mGluR1a, are almost completely co-localised with somatostatin in human temporal cortex, and switching them on makes Martinotti cells fire and inhibit the local circuit.

Acetylcholine, the transmitter of attention and arousal, also depolarises these cells and facilitates their lateral inhibition in both human and mouse neocortex.

MYELINATION:

Martinotti cell axons are essentially unmyelinated.

This is a design consequence rather than an oversight, because the cell's usefulness depends on being slow and integrative, and the absence of myelin explains the multi-millisecond delay between a pyramidal burst and the arrival of tuft inhibition.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, and much lower than a parvalbumin basket cell's, because firing rates are lower, action potentials are broader and cheaper per unit of signalling, and the huge mitochondrial load of fast-spiking cells is absent.

The one specialised cost is peptide manufacture, since somatostatin must be made in the cell body, packed into large dense-core vesicles and shipped up the axon, and cannot be replenished locally the way GABA can.

PLASTICITY:

The facilitating input synapse is itself very fast plasticity, changing strength within tens of milliseconds and resetting within a second.

Over longer times, Martinotti inhibition of apical dendrites gates whether a pyramidal cell can generate the dendritic calcium spikes that let its own synapses strengthen, so this cell is a gatekeeper for plasticity elsewhere, and its layer 1 arbor remodels during learning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia that matter here are layer 1 astrocytes, morphologically and molecularly distinct from deeper ones, which surround the Martinotti terminal plexus.

Because somatostatin acts by volume transmission, diffusing through the extracellular space, the peptide-degrading enzymes these astrocytes carry set how far the signal spreads, a role glia do not play for fast GABA synapses.

Martinotti somata are not enwrapped in perineuronal nets and the axon is not myelinated, so oligodendrocytes play essentially no part in this cell's life.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove Martinotti cells and the cortex loses its brake on runaway excitement and its sense of context.

A pyramidal cell that fired a burst would no longer quieten its neighbours, so a small local excitation would recruit an ever-widening group instead of standing out against a suppressed background, and sharp receptive field tuning would soften.

Layer 1 would become a place where feedback arrives with no local editing, so top-down expectation would be applied to every cell equally rather than selectively.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Martinotti cells control dendritic computation in others, vetoing the calcium and NMDA-receptor spikes that pyramidal tufts use to fuse top-down with bottom-up information.

They are recruited by a trans-synaptic protein interaction rather than by voltage alone, they release a peptide by volume transmission, and they answer to metabotropic glutamate receptors that report accumulated rather than instantaneous glutamate.

They are gap-junction coupled to other somatostatin cells, receive cholinergic gating that makes their lateral inhibition state-dependent, and by suppressing dendritic spikes they decide which synapses in the neighbourhood may undergo long-term change.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: facilitating glutamatergic synapses from local pyramidal cells (initial release probability 0.03 to 0.10, facilitating five to tenfold in a burst); cholinergic input (0 to 1 normalised); inhibition from vasoactive intestinal peptide and parvalbumin cells (0 to 6 nanosiemens); group I metabotropic glutamate receptor activation (0 to 1).

• OUTPUTS: GABA type A conductance onto pyramidal apical dendrites and tufts in layer 1 and layers 2 to 3, 0.2 to 1.5 nanosiemens per contact, 200 to 800 targets across several columns; somatostatin peptide into the extracellular space.

• STATE: membrane potential, T-type calcium inactivation, Ih activation, adaptation current, per-synapse facilitation variable, peptide vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 200 to 500 megaohms; membrane time constant 20 to 40 milliseconds; resting potential about minus 60 millivolts; threshold about minus 45 millivolts; maximum firing 20 to 60 hertz; conduction delay 3 to 10 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: facilitation recovery 150 to 300 milliseconds; inhibitory decay 15 to 40 milliseconds; peptide action, seconds; adaptation 100 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of somatostatin and of somatostatin neurons is one of the best-replicated findings in Alzheimer's disease and tracks cognitive decline, with somatostatin subtypes prominently affected in single-cell atlases of Alzheimer's middle temporal gyrus; reduced somatostatin expression in prefrontal cortex is also robust in major depression, and losing this dendritic brake is a route into epilepsy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a Martinotti cell is recruited by a neighbouring pyramidal cell, using a synapse whose strength grows with every spike in a burst.
Create a number variable called release probability with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cell voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called step number with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 4000 times.
    Calculate the sum of step number and 1.
    Set step number to the result.
    Calculate the product of release probability and 0.9995.
    Set release probability to the result.
    Calculate the product of conductance and 0.9933.
    Set conductance to the result.
    Calculate the remainder of step number divided by 200.
    If both the result is equal to 0 and step number is less than or equal to 1600 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of 1 and release probability.
        Calculate the product of 0.05 and the result.
        Calculate the sum of release probability and the result.
        Set release probability to the result.
        Calculate the product of 120 and release probability.
        Calculate the sum of conductance and the result.
        Set conductance to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of -60 and cell voltage.
    Calculate the sum of the result and conductance.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.00333.
    Calculate the sum of cell voltage and the result.
    Set cell voltage to the result.
End the loop.
Display cell voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the facilitating synapse needs a few lines of clear arithmetic run at three different input rates, and readability matters more than speed for a single cell.

DT = 0.1          # ms per step
U0 = 0.05         # very low initial release probability, set by Elfn1 and mGluR7
TAU_F = 200.0     # facilitation relaxes to baseline over ~200 ms
TAU_SYN = 15.0    # excitatory conductance decay on the Martinotti cell, ms
TAU_M = 30.0      # Martinotti membrane time constant, ms
V_REST, V_TH = -60.0, -45.0
W = 120.0         # synaptic weight scale, mV per unit release

def run(rate_hz, n_spikes=8, total_ms=400.0):
    """Drive one Martinotti cell with a burst of pyramidal spikes."""
    u, g, v, out, peak = U0, 0.0, V_REST, 0, V_REST
    arrivals = [i * (1000.0 / rate_hz) for i in range(n_spikes)]
    nxt = 0
    for step in range(int(total_ms / DT)):
        t = step * DT
        u += DT * (U0 - u) / TAU_F
        g -= DT * g / TAU_SYN
        while nxt < len(arrivals) and arrivals[nxt] <= t:
            u += U0 * (1.0 - u)       # each spike makes the next one stronger
            g += W * u
            nxt += 1
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + g) / TAU_M
        peak = max(peak, v)
        if v >= V_TH:
            out += 1
            v = V_REST
    return out, peak

for rate in (5.0, 20.0, 50.0):
    spikes, peak = run(rate)
    print(f"burst at {rate:5.1f} Hz -> Martinotti spikes {spikes}, peak {peak:6.2f} mV")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a bitufted cell whose axon ignores its neighbours' cell bodies entirely, climbs to the very top of the cortex and fans out under the surface.

Then test it: a single spike in the pyramidal cell next door does nothing, a burst makes the Martinotti cell fire and silence the neighbourhood, and no other cortical neuron combines an axon that ends in layer 1 with a synapse that must be earned by a burst.

CHAPTER 23. NON-MARTINOTTI CELLS (Sentry-Freeing Entry-Layer Neuron) (COG)

This cell carries the same peptide as the Martinotti cell and does the opposite job: instead of climbing to the roof to silence the cortex, it stays downstairs and switches off the guards, letting incoming sensation through.

Non-Martinotti defines a cell only by what it is not, which teaches a student nothing.

Sentry-Freeing names the target and the effect: these cells preferentially inhibit the fast-spiking parvalbumin basket cells that stand guard over layer 4 cell bodies, releasing the entry layer from inhibition rather than muting it.

Entry-Layer names where the axon goes: unlike Martinotti cells, whose axons climb to layer 1, these cells send their axons into layer 4 - the layer where signals from the thalamus first enter the cortical sheet.

Neuron marks it as a functional cortical cell, warning of the classic error where its own quasi-fast spikes lead to it being mistaken for the very sentries it inhibits.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A non-Martinotti cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and carrying the peptide somatostatin, exactly like a Martinotti cell, but whose axon never reaches layer 1.

The name is honestly negative, defining the cell by what it does not do; its axon stays in the middle and lower layers and, in sensory cortex, aims especially at layer 4, the layer that receives raw input from the thalamus, the brain's sensory relay.

Its most surprising job is disinhibition: it preferentially inhibits the fast-spiking parvalbumin interneurons of layer 4, and because those were holding layer 4 down, silencing them lets layer 4 speak louder.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small to medium, multipolar or bitufted, with sparsely spiny dendrites.

The axon branches locally into a compact, roughly spherical or basket-like arbor with a strong horizontal component, and in the layer 5 population a prominent ascending branch that stops in layer 4 instead of continuing to the surface.

Beside a Martinotti cell the difference is immediate: one arbor is a tall lollipop reaching the surface, the other a dense ball sitting on the thalamic input layer.

SIZE:

Somata are 10 to 18 micrometres across.

The arbor spans 200 to 500 micrometres horizontally and rarely exceeds 400 micrometres vertically, keeping the cell inside two or three layers, for a total axonal length of one to three centimetres, shorter than a Martinotti cell's because none is spent climbing.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Layer 4 targeting non-Martinotti cells, captured by the X94 mouse line and matching the somatostatin HPSE transcriptomic type, with somata in layer 4 and upper layer 5.

• Deep-layer non-Martinotti cells associated with the somatostatin MYH8 type, in layer 5b and layer 6 with local arbors.

• Basket-like somatostatin cells whose terminals cluster near target cell bodies rather than on dendrites.

• A human counterpart is likely but not yet firmly named, the closest published match being a somatostatin variant with a sparse, sparsely branching axon and a strong descending component.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Somata sit in layers 4, 5a, 5b and 6 of the neocortex, with the layer 4 targeting form concentrated at the boundary between layer 4 and layer 5a.

The population is strikingly area-dependent: in mouse barrel cortex the somatostatin cells of layer 4 are non-Martinotti, whereas in mouse visual cortex the layer 4 somatostatin cells are Martinotti cells, so the same layer is wired differently in two sensory areas.

The interface it occupies is therefore the thalamic input interface, where signals arriving from outside the cortex are first inspected.

NUMBER:

There is no direct human count.

Taking non-Martinotti cells as roughly a quarter to a third of cortical somatostatin cells, and somatostatin cells as about 30 percent of the 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, gives about 190 million, range 100 to 320 million, which is about 1.2 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and roughly 6 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons.

Confidence is low for a specific reason: the split between Martinotti and non-Martinotti somatostatin cells varies by cortical area, so any single global fraction is a rough average rather than a constant.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells are described as quasi-fast-spiking, firing narrow action potentials at high rates but with a distinctive stuttering pattern in which bursts are separated by irregular silent gaps.

Input resistance is lower than a Martinotti cell's at 100 to 250 megaohms, the membrane time constant is shorter at 8 to 20 milliseconds, and the low-threshold calcium rebound that defines Martinotti cells is weak or absent.

Maximum sustained rates of 50 to 120 hertz place them between true fast-spiking parvalbumin cells and slow, adapting Martinotti cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input arrives from local excitatory cells in layers 4 and 5 and, importantly, directly from thalamic fibres, so the cell hears sensory input at first hand.

Like other somatostatin cells its excitatory inputs facilitate during a train, though less dramatically than at Martinotti synapses.

Output is aimed disproportionately at parvalbumin fast-spiking interneurons within layer 4, and also at layer 4 excitatory cells and layer 5 pyramidal dendrites, so the net effect on layer 4 is a mixture of direct inhibition and indirect release.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, 0.1 to 0.4 micrometres, densely branched over a short distance, with numerous small boutons, a substantial fraction landing on the somata and proximal dendrites of interneurons rather than on distal pyramidal dendrites.

Conduction delays are well under a millisecond within the local arbor, so unlike the Martinotti cell this type delivers its inhibition promptly.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA plus somatostatin, the same pairing as the Martinotti cell.

Some members co-express neuropeptide Y, and the layer 4 targeting type is marked by the gene HPSE, encoding the enzyme heparanase, which acts on the sugary scaffolding outside the cell; metabotropic glutamate signalling is present but the extreme Elfn1-driven facilitation of Martinotti synapses is weaker here.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated, with no specialised myelin arrangement described.

That absence is itself informative rather than a gap in the record, because the arbor is local and delays are already short, so insulation would buy nothing, and a modeller should therefore use pure cable delays well under one millisecond.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Intermediate: firing rates approach those of fast-spiking cells, but stuttering keeps the average rate over a second far lower, so the sodium and potassium pumping bill falls between a Martinotti and a basket cell.

Peptide synthesis and axonal transport of dense-core vesicles add a modest fixed cost, as for all somatostatin cells.

PLASTICITY:

Because these cells sit on the thalamic input layer, their strength is adjusted whenever sensory input changes, and depriving an animal of a sensory input alters somatostatin inhibition in the corresponding layer 4 within days.

They also gate the plasticity of others, since by controlling the parvalbumin cells that enforce critical period timing they influence when a cortical area can be rewired, while their own synapses strengthen and weaken in the ordinary way.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Layer 4 astrocytes are unusually dense and unusually good at clearing glutamate, because layer 4 receives the fastest and strongest excitatory barrages in the cortex, and these are the astrocytes surrounding non-Martinotti cells.

The heparanase carried by the layer 4 targeting type acts directly on the extracellular sugar matrix that astrocytes help build, suggesting this neuron actively renovates its own glial surroundings, an unusual arrangement; oligodendrocyte and microglial support otherwise appears ordinary.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove non-Martinotti cells and layer 4 loses its volume control.
