The fast-spiking cells of layer 4 would run unopposed, so incoming sensory signals would be clamped at a fixed low gain and the cortex could no longer open the gate wider when a stimulus deserved attention or narrow it when the input was noise.

The flavour lost is adaptive sensitivity at the front door: sensation would still arrive, but the cortex would stop deciding how much to let in.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The characteristic non-classical feature is that this cell's main output is a double negative, a sign inversion no single synapse can express.

Stuttering firing makes it an intermittent gate rather than a rate coder, so the gaps carry as much information as the bursts.

It releases somatostatin and sometimes neuropeptide Y by volume transmission, reaching receptors on cells it never contacts, it is gap-junction coupled to other somatostatin cells, and its heparanase lets it remodel the extracellular matrix around it, which is structural engineering rather than signalling.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: thalamocortical excitation (0 to 10 nanosiemens, latency 1 to 3 milliseconds); local layer 4 and layer 5 excitation with moderate facilitation (0 to 12 nanosiemens); inhibition from vasoactive intestinal peptide cells (0 to 5 nanosiemens); acetylcholine (0 to 1 normalised).

• OUTPUTS: GABA type A conductance onto layer 4 parvalbumin interneurons, weighted 2 to 4 times higher than onto excitatory cells, 0.5 to 3 nanosiemens per contact across 50 to 300 targets; weaker conductance onto layer 4 excitatory cells and layer 5 pyramidal dendrites; somatostatin peptide release.

• STATE: membrane potential, adaptation current, stutter phase variable, per-synapse facilitation variable, peptide pool.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 100 to 250 megaohms; membrane time constant 8 to 20 milliseconds; maximum rate 50 to 120 hertz; fraction of output onto parvalbumin cells 0.5 to 0.8; area-dependent abundance, from near zero in some visual cortex layer 4 to dominant in barrel cortex layer 4.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibitory decay 10 to 25 milliseconds; stutter interval 20 to 200 milliseconds; facilitation recovery 100 to 300 milliseconds; deprivation-driven change, days.

• FAILURE MODE: if this cell fails, layer 4 fast-spiking inhibition becomes unregulated and sensory gain locks at a fixed value, a plausible substrate for the sensory over-responsivity and under-responsivity reported in autism and fragile X syndrome, and somatostatin subtypes are among the populations prominently affected in single-cell atlases of Alzheimer's disease cortex.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show how a non-Martinotti cell raises the output of layer four by inhibiting the fast-spiking cells that were holding layer four down.
Create a number variable called non martinotti rate with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called fast spiking rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called layer four rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called scratch with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Calculate the product of non martinotti rate and 1.6.
    Calculate the difference of 12 and the result.
    Set scratch to the result.
    If scratch is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set scratch to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of scratch and fast spiking rate.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0625.
    Calculate the sum of fast spiking rate and the result.
    Set fast spiking rate to the result.
    Calculate the product of fast spiking rate and 1.2.
    Calculate the difference of 12 and the result.
    Set scratch to the result.
    If scratch is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set scratch to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of scratch and layer four rate.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.0333.
    Calculate the sum of layer four rate and the result.
    Set layer four rate to the result.
End the loop.
Display fast spiking rate to the console.
Display layer four rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the disinhibition motif is three coupled rate equations, and Python lets the three lines sit next to each other where a reader can compare them.

DT = 0.5                       # milliseconds per step
TAU_NMC, TAU_PV, TAU_L4 = 10.0, 8.0, 15.0    # rate time constants, ms
W_NMC_PV = 1.6                 # non-Martinotti cell inhibits the PV interneuron
W_PV_L4 = 1.2                  # PV interneuron inhibits the layer 4 principal cell
THALAMIC = 12.0                # sensory drive reaching PV and principal cells

def relu(x):
    """Firing rates cannot go below zero."""
    return x if x > 0.0 else 0.0

def run(drive_nmc, ms=300.0):
    nmc = pv = l4 = 0.0
    for _ in range(int(ms / DT)):
        nmc += DT * (-nmc + relu(drive_nmc)) / TAU_NMC
        pv  += DT * (-pv + relu(THALAMIC - W_NMC_PV * nmc)) / TAU_PV
        l4  += DT * (-l4 + relu(THALAMIC - W_PV_L4 * pv)) / TAU_L4
    return nmc, pv, l4

for drive in (0.0, 4.0, 8.0):
    nmc, pv, l4 = run(drive)
    print(f"nMC drive {drive:4.1f} -> nMC {nmc:5.2f}  PV {pv:5.2f}  layer 4 {l4:5.2f}")

# Driving an INHIBITORY cell harder makes layer 4 louder, not quieter.
# That sign inversion is the whole point of the non-Martinotti cell.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Stain for somatostatin, then follow the axon: if it carries the peptide but never reaches layer 1, building a dense ball of terminals on the thalamic input layer instead, it is a non-Martinotti cell.

Confirm it electrically by the stuttering, quasi-fast-spiking discharge, and functionally by the paradox that exciting it makes its region more active.

CHAPTER 24. SST-CHODL CELLS (Far-Hushing Slow-Wave Neuron) (COG) (PUC)

This neuron does not whisper to one partner - it exhales a cloud of silence across a patch of cortex the width of a grain of rice, and it does so about once a second all night long.

Far-Hushing records its extraordinary long-range reach: its axon spans millimetres across neocortex, allowing a single cell to coordinate quietude across distant columns.

Slow-Wave describes its timing: it fires rhythmically during deep, non-REM sleep, driving cortical networks into the slow-wave oscillations that define deep restful sleep.

Neuron marks it as the specialized cortical interneuron that enforces global sleep-state synchronization.


This chapter merges the two entries that previously described this same cell, and the alternative name proposed for it, Wide-Travelling Sleep-Onset Neuron, is recorded here as a synonym rather than as a second type.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a Previously Unnamed Construct: a wide-field, volume-transmitting inhibitory neuron of layer 1 whose firing locks to the slow oscillation of deep sleep.

It is assembled from three documented facts that have never been given one name.

First, layer 1 is the richest home of neurogliaform cells, small inhibitory neurons that release gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical, usually written GABA, into the extracellular space rather than a tidy cleft.

Second, layer 1 cells expressing neuron-derived neurotrophic factor, abbreviated NDNF, produce both fast inhibition through GABA type A receptors and unusually slow inhibition through GABA type B receptors, and they control pyramidal cells in every layer.

Third, these layer 1 cells fall into subpopulations whose activity tracks particular sleep stages and rides with the hippocampal sharp-wave ripples that replay memories.

Together that is a cell whose job is to hush the top of the cortex, broadly and slowly, in time with the sleeping brain's slowest rhythm.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is small and round, with short dendrites radiating in all directions like a sea urchin's spines.

From that small centre grows an enormous, extremely fine axon plexus that looks under a microscope like a ball of spider silk.

The axon carries a bouton, a swelling holding transmitter, roughly every 4 micrometres, and unusually many of those boutons face no postsynaptic partner at all.

That is the anatomical signature of volume transmission: the cell aims at a volume, not a target.

SIZE:

Soma diameter is about 10 to 15 micrometres and the dendritic tree is compact, generally under 200 micrometres across.

The defining feature is the axon plexus, estimated here at 400 to 900 micrometres across, wider than one cortical column.

That figure is an inference, because the best-measured human layer 1 relative, the compact rosehip cell, reaches only about 288 micrometres horizontally, so confidence in the wide-field number is moderate at best.

Total axon length likely exceeds 15 millimetres packed into that small ball.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Human layer 1 has a far richer interneuron repertoire than the mouse, with at least four physiological types: canopy cells, neurogliaform cells, alpha-7 cells and cells expressing vasoactive intestinal peptide.

The far-hushing type sits at the neurogliaform and canopy end of that spectrum, in the transcriptomic groups marked by the gene LAMP5.

A compact relative, the rosehip cell, makes up 10 to 15 percent of layer 1 inhibitory neurons and has boutons of 0.37 cubic micrometres, about four times a neurogliaform cell's.

Human layer 1 also holds neurogliaform cells expressing the transcription factor LHX6, rare in rodents and plentiful in primates.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 1 of the neocortex, concentrated in its deeper part where apical tufts are thickest, in every cortical area.

It straddles two interfaces a simulation must represent: the layer 1 to layer 2 boundary, where its axon cloud meets pyramidal tufts, and the boundary between neighbouring columns, which its cloud simply ignores.

Relatives occur in all cortical layers, in the hippocampus as ivy cells, and in the amygdala, but the far-hushing form is a layer 1 resident.

NUMBER:

My best estimate is 25 million across both hemispheres, plausible range 15 to 35 million, and confidence is moderate to low.

That is about 0.15 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 0.8 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion inhibitory neurons of the neocortex.

The estimate is anchored on layer 1 holding some 150 to 250 million neurons, essentially all inhibitory, with the wide-field volume-transmitting group taking a modest slice.

Layer 1 cells have been reported as about 8.3 percent of all cortical GABA-releasing cells, which is consistent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The classic signature is late spiking: inject a steady current and the cell rides a slow ramp for tens or hundreds of milliseconds before its first action potential.

Input resistance is high at roughly 150 to 350 megaohms, and the membrane time constant is 10 to 20 milliseconds.

Resting potential is near minus 65 millivolts, spikes are broad, and firing accelerates through a long current step instead of adapting away.

In deep sleep the cell fires in bursts locked to the up state, the depolarised, active half of the slow oscillation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from thalamic matrix axons, long-range cortico-cortical feedback fibres in layer 1, cholinergic fibres of the nucleus basalis and local pyramidal collaterals.

Outputs go onto apical tuft dendrites of pyramidal neurons in layers 2, 3 and 5, onto other layer 1 interneurons, and onto the presynaptic terminals of incoming axons, because GABA type B receptors sit there too and switch off transmitter release before it happens.

One action potential is enough to give a partner a two-part response: a slow GABA type A component rising in about 4 milliseconds and decaying with a time constant near 36 milliseconds, then a much slower GABA type B component.

These cells also form gap junctions with each other, so a local group behaves partly as one unit.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Very fine, well under 0.5 micrometres, unmyelinated, branching every 60 to 70 micrometres.

Conduction is slow at roughly 0.1 to 0.3 metres per second, but distances are short so the delay is a fraction of a millisecond.

The axon never leaves layer 1, which is why this cell's influence is broad but shallow.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the only fast transmitter, released by volume transmission so that micromolar concentrations linger for tens of milliseconds.

That slow, dilute GABA transient is the mechanistic reason the responses are slow, and it is what recruits GABA type B receptors, which most inhibitory cells cannot do alone.

The cells also secrete NDNF, and many express neuropeptide Y and nitric oxide synthase.

MYELINATION:

None, and layer 1 is nearly free of myelin.

A bare fine axon suits a design where speed is irrelevant and spread is everything.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and unevenly spread.

Spiking is infrequent, but maintaining tens of thousands of boutons and refilling a large releasable GABA pool across a wide plexus is costly.

Because the released GABA must be cleared from a whole volume, this cell externalises part of its energy bill to astrocytes.

PLASTICITY:

The strength of the GABA type B component shifts with sleep pressure and with cholinergic tone, so the inhibition itself is plastic.

Slow inhibition of the apical tuft decides whether inputs arriving at the top of a pyramidal cell can trigger the dendritic calcium spikes that permit long-term potentiation, so this cell is a switch controlling when other synapses may learn.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here do a job they do nowhere else in the cortex: they set the range of a chemical broadcast.

The GABA transporter GAT-3 on astrocyte membranes decides how far the cloud spreads and how long it lasts, making astrocytes the volume control on the signal rather than passive housekeepers.

Layer 1 also holds the primate-specific interlaminar astrocytes whose processes reach a millimetre down into layers 2 to 4, laying a vertical glial scaffold over this cell's horizontal chemical one.

Microglia prune tuft synapses during sleep, so the same patch sees chemical hushing and physical remodelling in the same window.

Oligodendrocyte involvement is negligible.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and layer 1 loses its brake and its clock at once.

Pyramidal tufts would become permanently permissive, so any weak input arriving at the top of the cortex could fire a dendritic calcium spike and be treated as important.

The cortex would lose the deep silent troughs between up states, and the slow wave of deep sleep would flatten into a smear.

The concrete loss is discrimination during sleep, the ability to hold most of the cortex quiet while one memory trace is replayed.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything interesting here happens outside the classical synapse.

The cell writes to a shared chemical field, so its output is a concentration in a volume, and a faithful simulation needs a diffusion term, not just a connection list.

It acts on presynaptic terminals, changing other cells' release probability without touching their membrane potential.

Its GABA type B action runs through a G protein cascade, a chain of internal messengers, so the effect peaks 150 to 250 milliseconds after the spike that caused it, far later than any axon delay could explain.

Gap junctions synchronise the local group.

By shaping the dendritic calcium spike it edits the pyramidal cell's own nonlinear computation rather than merely subtracting from it.

It also secretes NDNF, a growth-factor-like protein, so it speaks the slow trophic language as well as the fast chemical one.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic drive from thalamic matrix and cortico-cortical feedback axons in layer 1, 0 to 300 picoamps; acetylcholine from nucleus basalis as a gain and burst-threshold modulator; a slow oscillation phase signal from 0 to 2 pi radians at 0.5 to 1.0 hertz; local pyramidal collateral input.

• OUTPUTS: an extracellular GABA field of 0 to 5 micromolar over a radius of 200 to 450 micrometres; GABA type A conductance of 0 to 2 nanosiemens per target; GABA type B potassium conductance of 0 to 60 nanosiemens per target; presynaptic release probability scaled from 1.0 down to 0.3.

• STATE: membrane voltage; slow ramp variable producing late spiking; extracellular GABA concentration; bound GABA type B receptor fraction; activated G protein concentration; coupled neighbour voltage.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 150 to 350 megaohms; GABA added per spike about 0.6 millimolar inside the plexus; Hill coefficient 4 for G protein to channel coupling; half activation at 100 G protein units; maximum potassium conductance about 60 nanosiemens.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; GABA cloud decay about 60 milliseconds; GABA type A slow decay about 36 milliseconds; GABA type B peak at 150 to 250 milliseconds; slow oscillation cycle 1000 to 2000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing this slow, wide inhibition removes the brake on dendritic tufts and is a plausible route into epileptic runaway excitation, since layer 1 is where synchronising feedback arrives; weakened slow-wave sleep and impaired memory consolidation follow, a pattern seen in Alzheimer's disease; excessive activity would instead flatten cortical responsiveness and blunt attention.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a short burst of spikes from a far hushing slow wave neuron into a slow, delayed potassium brake on distant pyramidal tufts.
Create a number variable called cloud with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called bound receptor with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called active g protein with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called peak brake with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called step counter with initial value zero.
While step counter is less than three thousand, repeat the following steps.
    Create a boolean variable called fired with initial value false.
    Calculate the remainder of step counter divided by ten.
    Check if both step counter is greater than or equal to one thousand and step counter is less than one thousand one hundred is true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Check if that remainder is equal to zero.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set fired to true.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    If fired is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set cloud to the sum of cloud and six tenths.
    End the conditional block.
    Set cloud to the product of cloud and nine hundred and eighty four thousandths.
    Calculate the difference of one and bound receptor.
    Set bound receptor to the sum of bound receptor and the product of nine hundredths and the product of cloud and that difference.
    Set active g protein to the sum of active g protein and the difference of the product of eighteen hundredths and bound receptor and the product of thirty four thousandths and active g protein.
    Create a number variable called g squared with initial value zero.
    Set g squared to the product of active g protein and active g protein.
    Create a number variable called g fourth with initial value zero.
    Set g fourth to the product of g squared and g squared.
    Create a number variable called brake with initial value zero.
    Set brake to the quotient of g fourth divided by the sum of g fourth and one hundred.
    Check if brake is greater than peak brake.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set peak brake to brake.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step counter to the sum of step counter and one.
End the loop.
Display peak brake to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the computation of interest is a slow chemical cascade measured in hundreds of milliseconds, not microsecond spike timing, so a readable millisecond loop is exactly right.

# Python model of volume-transmitted GABA and the slow GABA-B brake it creates.
DT = 1.0                     # time step, milliseconds
K1, K2 = 0.09, 0.0012        # GABA-B binding and unbinding, per ms
K3, K4 = 0.18, 0.034         # G protein made and destroyed, per ms
KD, N = 100.0, 4.0           # four G proteins must gather to open one channel
G_KMAX, E_K = 0.06, -95.0    # potassium channel: microsiemens, mV
TAU_CLOUD = 60.0             # the GABA cloud drains in ~60 ms
PER_SPIKE = 0.6              # millimolar added to the cloud by one spike

def run(spike_steps, n_steps):
    gaba = r = g = 0.0       # cloud, bound receptor fraction, active G protein
    v_post, peak_gk, peak_t = -70.0, 0.0, 0
    for k in range(n_steps):
        if k in spike_steps:
            gaba += PER_SPIKE
        gaba += DT * (-gaba / TAU_CLOUD)             # diffusion plus uptake
        r += DT * (K1 * gaba * (1.0 - r) - K2 * r)   # slow binding, slower release
        g += DT * (K3 * r - K4 * g)
        gn = pow(g, N)                               # four G proteins per channel
        gk = G_KMAX * gn / (gn + KD)                 # the hush: potassium opens
        if gk > peak_gk:
            peak_gk, peak_t = gk, k
    return peak_gk, peak_t, peak_gk * (v_post - E_K)  # conductance, time, nanoamps

burst = set(1000 + 10 * i for i in range(10))        # ten spikes at 100 Hz at t = 1 s
gk, when, i_k = run(burst, 3000)
print("peak potassium conductance %.4f uS at %d ms (%d ms after the burst), %.3f nA"
      % (gk, when, when - 1000, i_k))

Run, it prints a peak potassium conductance of 0.0529 microsiemens arriving 172 milliseconds after the burst ends, worth 1.322 nanoamps of outward current.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Fire one cell once and a hush spreads over half a millimetre of cortex, arriving a fifth of a second late and lasting a quarter of a second.

No other cortical neuron produces a GABA type B response from a single spike, and none combines a tiny body, a spider-silk axon ball confined to layer 1, and a firing pattern that ticks with the slow wave of sleep.

CHAPTER 25. BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

A bipolar cell is the brain's permission slip: a thin upright cell whose whole job is to switch off other switch-off cells, so that a patch of cortex can suddenly listen harder.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A bipolar cell is a small nerve cell in the cerebral cortex, the folded outer sheet of the brain that carries out perception, language and thought.

It is inhibitory, which means it releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the chemical the brain uses to make other cells less likely to fire.

Most cortical bipolar cells also carry vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP), a short chain of amino acids used as a slow chemical message.

In humans and other primates these same cells almost always carry calretinin, a protein that mops up calcium and is used by anatomists as a stain-visible label.

Here is the surprise: a bipolar cell mostly inhibits other inhibitory cells rather than the excitatory pyramidal cells that do the region's main work.

Its favourite target is the somatostatin-expressing interneuron, a cell that clamps down on the far branches of pyramidal cells.

So when a bipolar cell fires, it takes the brakes off those branches, an effect called disinhibition.

The practical consequence is attention: when you notice something, bipolar cells across the relevant cortical area are firing, and the pyramidal cells there are briefly allowed to listen to inputs they had been ignoring.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The name says it: two poles.

One thin bundle of dendrites, the branches that collect input, runs straight up towards the brain surface, and another runs straight down.

The result is a tall, narrow, spindle-shaped cell that occupies a column of tissue barely wider than a human hair is thick.

The upward dendrite usually reaches layer 1, the almost cell-free surface layer where long-range feedback axons from other brain areas arrive.

The axon, the output cable, leaves the cell body and descends, branching sparsely across many layers rather than densely in one.

A subpopulation carries dendritic spines, small mushroom-shaped protrusions that receive excitatory contacts, and these spines are unusually mobile, appearing and vanishing over hours.

SIZE:

The cell body is small, roughly 8 to 12 micrometres across, which is about one tenth the width of a human hair.

The dendritic field is narrow, typically 50 to 100 micrometres wide, but tall, often 400 to 800 micrometres from top to bottom.

The axon runs vertically through most of the cortical depth, with a total branch length in the low millimetres.

These figures come mainly from rodent and monkey reconstructions; matched human measurements exist but are sparse, so treat them as approximate.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Classical bipolar cells with two tight dendritic tufts and a descending, translaminar axon.

• Bitufted or modified bipolar cells, which are the same plan with a slightly wider spread at each pole.

• Spiny bipolar cells, a minority whose dendrites carry highly dynamic spines.

• Interneuron-selective bipolar cells, which contact almost nothing but other interneurons and are the purest disinhibitory form.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies concentrate in layer 2 and upper layer 3 of the neocortex, with a smaller population in layers 4, 5 and 6.

They are found in every neocortical area, including primary visual cortex, primary auditory cortex, primary somatosensory cortex, motor cortex and the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex.

The dendritic tuft sits at an interface that matters: the boundary between layer 1 and layer 2, where feedback axons from higher cortical areas and from the thalamus, the brain's central relay station, terminate.

The descending axon crosses the layer 3 to layer 5 interface, so a single cell samples the surface and speaks to the depths.

NUMBER:

This batch of six types is allocated about 1.0 billion of the 3.2 billion inhibitory neurons in the 16.34 billion neuron neocortex, which is about 31 percent of cortical inhibitory cells and about 6.2 percent of all neocortical neurons.

For bipolar cells specifically I allocate about 300 million, with a defensible range of 250 to 450 million.

That is roughly 9 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons, about 1.8 percent of all neocortical neurons, and about 0.35 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole human nervous system.

Confidence is moderate, not high.

The reason is a genuine tension in the literature: calretinin-positive cells have been reported at about 8 percent of all neurons in human cortical areas, and up to half of them are bipolar, which would push the figure above 600 million, but that clashes with the widely used estimate that calretinin cells are 10 to 30 percent of cortical GABA cells.

I have taken the conservative figure and flag the higher one as an upper bound rather than invent false precision.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting membrane voltage is about minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts.

Input resistance is high, commonly 200 to 500 megohms, meaning a small current makes a large voltage change, so these cells are easy to excite.

The membrane time constant is slow for an interneuron, around 15 to 25 milliseconds.

Firing is irregular spiking or bursting rather than fast spiking, with action potentials that broaden and slow during a train, a pattern called accommodation.

Typical rates in an awake animal are a few spikes per second at rest, rising to 20 to 60 spikes per second during arousal, reward or punishment.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from three main places: long-range excitatory axons from other cortical areas, thalamic axons, and neuromodulatory axons from the basal forebrain and brainstem.

Serotonin and acetylcholine both excite these cells directly, through the ionotropic serotonin receptor 5HT3a and through nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, which is why they respond within tens of milliseconds to a change in behavioural state.

Outputs go preferentially onto somatostatin-expressing interneurons, especially Martinotti cells, and secondarily onto parvalbumin-expressing basket cells and onto pyramidal dendritic shafts and spines.

The synapse onto a somatostatin cell is small but reliable and strongly facilitating, meaning it grows stronger during a burst.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, typically 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres in diameter, and conducts slowly, on the order of 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second.

It descends as a narrow vertical stalk with widely spaced boutons, the swellings where transmitter is released, rather than forming a dense local basket.

Total bouton count per cell is modest for an interneuron, in the high hundreds to low thousands.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fast transmitter is GABA, acting on GABA-A receptors, which are chloride channels that open in about a millisecond.

The co-transmitter is vasoactive intestinal peptide, released from dense-core vesicles only when the cell fires in bursts, and acting on VPAC1 and VPAC2 receptors over seconds.

Many of these cells also express calretinin, which is not released but buffers internal calcium and shapes how transmitter release adapts.

A minority co-express cholecystokinin, a second peptide, and a smaller minority express choline acetyltransferase, the enzyme that makes acetylcholine.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated.

Myelin is the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, and these thin local axons do not receive it, which is one reason they are slow.

The absence is functional, not accidental: a slow, spatially spread signal suits a modulatory gate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest compared with a fast-spiking cell, because the firing rate is low and the axon is short.

Estimated energy use is on the order of 10 to 20 percent of that of a parvalbumin basket cell of similar volume.

Peptide production is the expensive part: making, packaging and transporting VIP-filled dense-core vesicles requires sustained protein synthesis and axonal transport, so the cell keeps a well-developed Golgi apparatus, the organelle that packages proteins for export.

PLASTICITY:

Excitatory synapses onto these cells undergo long-term potentiation, a lasting strengthening, and in layer 2 and 3 the induction is postsynaptic while the expression is presynaptic, meaning the change is triggered in the bipolar cell but ends up as more transmitter released onto it.

Their dendritic spines turn over unusually fast, on a timescale of hours, so the map of who talks to them is rewritten continuously.

Their output synapses onto somatostatin cells are subject to short-term facilitation, giving burst-dependent gain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes, the star-shaped support cells, do something specific here: they express VPAC receptors, so the peptide this neuron releases acts on the glia as well as on neurons, raising astrocytic cyclic adenosine monophosphate and promoting glycogen breakdown to fuel local activity.

The same astrocytes relay the vasodilator signal onto nearby capillaries, so this cell type has a privileged line to the blood supply.

Microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, prune the fast-turning-over spines of these cells during development, and the perineuronal net, the lattice of sugar-protein that armours parvalbumin cells, is absent here, leaving these cells structurally plastic into adulthood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove bipolar cells and the cortex keeps working but loses its volume knob.

Sensory responses become flat and stimulus-locked: the region would respond the same way whether the animal was attending, rewarded, punished, running or still.

Feedback from higher areas would arrive in layer 1 and fail to change anything, because the somatostatin brake on distal dendrites would never lift.

The flavour that disappears is context: the ability of the same input to mean something different depending on what the animal is doing.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Vasoactive intestinal peptide released from this cell is a potent vasodilator, and stimulating a single VIP interneuron in a cortical slice widens nearby microvessels, so this neuron literally opens its own blood supply.

Peptide release is volume transmission: the molecule diffuses tens of micrometres and acts on any cell carrying the receptor, with no synapse required.

Back-propagating action potentials, spikes that travel backwards from the cell body into the dendrites, license calcium entry at the spines and gate spine turnover.

Calretinin buffering means intracellular calcium transients are brief, which shortens the window for calcium-dependent plasticity.

Local protein synthesis in the dendrites supports the rapid spine remodelling, and nicotinic receptor activation triggers gene transcription through calcium-responsive pathways, coupling arousal to lasting change.

These cells are electrically coupled to each other by gap junctions, protein channels that let current pass directly between cells, in early life more than in adulthood.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Long-range cortico-cortical excitation onto the dendritic tuft, 0 to 40 spikes per second per afferent, glutamatergic.

◦ Thalamic excitation onto proximal dendrites, 0 to 60 spikes per second, glutamatergic.

◦ Acetylcholine from the nucleus basalis of Meynert acting on nicotinic receptors, treated as a 0 to 1 normalised drive.

◦ Serotonin from the dorsal raphe nucleus acting on 5HT3a receptors, 0 to 1 normalised drive.

◦ Inhibition from other interneurons, 0 to 5 nanosiemens peak conductance.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto somatostatin interneurons, peak conductance 0.3 to 1.5 nanosiemens per connection, facilitating.

◦ GABA onto parvalbumin basket cells and onto pyramidal dendrites, 0.1 to 0.6 nanosiemens.

◦ Vasoactive intestinal peptide into the extracellular space, effective radius 20 to 50 micrometres, released only above about 15 spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, refractory timer, spike-frequency adaptation variable, internal calcium concentration, dense-core vesicle pool level, and per-synapse facilitation variables.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage minus 62 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, input resistance 200 to 500 megohms, membrane time constant 15 to 25 milliseconds, refractory period 2 to 4 milliseconds, peptide release threshold about 15 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ GABA-A synaptic decay 6 to 12 milliseconds, membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds, facilitation 100 to 300 milliseconds, peptide action 1 to 30 seconds, spine turnover hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of the gene MECP2 from these cells alone reproduces much of Rett syndrome in mice, including social and behavioural deficits, so failure here weakens inhibition overall, blocks experience-dependent plasticity and derails cortical maturation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide at each time step whether a bipolar cell fires, and how strongly it lifts somatostatin inhibition off a pyramidal dendrite.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value negative sixty two.
Create a number variable called firing_threshold with initial value negative forty.
Create a number variable called peptide_release_threshold with initial value fifteen.
Create a number variable called recent_spike_rate with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called somatostatin_brake with initial value one.
Create a list variable called spike_times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called current_time with initial value zero.
While current_time is less than three hundred, repeat the following steps.
    Set long_range_drive to the excitation arriving from other cortical areas.
    Set acetylcholine_drive to the arousal signal arriving from the basal forebrain.
    Calculate the sum of long_range_drive and acetylcholine_drive.
    Set total_drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of total_drive and membrane_voltage.
    Set voltage_change to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of voltage_change divided by twenty.
    Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and the result.
    If membrane_voltage is greater than or equal to firing_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Add current_time to the end of spike_times.
        Set membrane_voltage to negative fifty five.
        Set somatostatin_brake to the product of somatostatin_brake and zero point six five.
    End the conditional block.
    Get the total number of items in spike_times.
    Set recent_spike_rate to the result.
    If recent_spike_rate is greater than peptide_release_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Display "release vasoactive intestinal peptide and dilate the local vessel" to the console.
    End the conditional block.
    Set somatostatin_brake to the sum of somatostatin_brake and zero point zero one.
    Set current_time to the sum of current_time and one.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the model is small, the arithmetic is plain, and readability matters most here.
DT = 0.5  # milliseconds per step

class Unit:
    """A leaky integrator that turns input current into a firing rate."""
    def __init__(self, tau, gain, thresh):
        self.tau, self.gain, self.thresh, self.v = tau, gain, thresh, 0.0
    def step(self, drive):
        self.v += DT * (-self.v + drive) / self.tau
        return max(0.0, self.gain * (self.v - self.thresh))  # rate, arbitrary units

vip = Unit(tau=18.0, gain=2.0, thresh=0.20)   # high input resistance, slow membrane
sst = Unit(tau=25.0, gain=1.5, thresh=0.10)   # dendrite-targeting partner cell
pyr = Unit(tau=12.0, gain=1.0, thresh=0.00)   # one pyramidal apical dendrite

r_vip = r_sst = 0.0
for step in range(600):                        # 300 ms of simulated time
    ms = step * DT
    attending = 100.0 <= ms < 200.0
    acetylcholine = 0.8 if attending else 0.0  # burst from the basal forebrain
    long_range = 0.6 if attending else 0.2     # axon arriving from another area
    r_vip = vip.step(long_range + acetylcholine)
    r_sst = sst.step(0.50 - 0.35 * r_vip)      # the bipolar cell silences SST
    r_pyr = pyr.step(0.70 - 0.30 * r_sst)      # the dendrite is released from inhibition
    if step % 100 == 0:
        print("t=%6.1f ms  VIP=%5.2f  SST=%5.2f  PYR=%5.2f" % (ms, r_vip, r_sst, r_pyr))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A tall narrow two-poled cell in layer 2 or 3, stained by calretinin and by vasoactive intestinal peptide, firing irregularly, and wiring almost exclusively onto other inhibitory cells.

If you find an inhibitory cell whose main effect on a pyramidal neuron is to excite it, you have found a bipolar cell.

CHAPTER 26. DOUBLE BOUQUET CELLS (COG)

Stain a slice of human cortex for one protein and a forest appears: thousands of thin vertical tassels, hanging down in near-perfect ranks about 30 micrometres apart, like the warp threads of a loom.

Double Bouquet is a shape name that describes the axon's tight vertical horsetail bundle, which hangs through the cortical layers to inhibit the dendrites of pyramidal cells within an extremely narrow column.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A double bouquet cell is an inhibitory cortical neuron whose axon collapses into a single narrow vertical rope called a horsetail.

Inhibitory again means it releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main calming transmitter.

The horsetail descends through several cortical layers and releases GABA almost exclusively onto the dendrites and dendritic spines of pyramidal cells inside one very narrow vertical cylinder of tissue.

It does not touch cell bodies, which is the opposite of a basket cell.

Functionally it is a vertical privacy screen: it isolates one microcolumn, a strip of cortex only a few tens of micrometres wide, from the excitatory drive spilling in from its neighbours.

It is also close to a primate speciality.

Classic horsetail bundles are prominent in monkey and human cortex, and have been very hard to find in rats and mice, which is one reason rodent-based models of cortex may be missing a layer of structure that human cortex has.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Two tufts of dendrites, one going up and one going down, give the cell its name.

From the lower pole the axon emerges and immediately turns into a tight vertical bundle of thin, wavy, densely beaded branches.

The bundle looks like a horse's tail, hence the anatomical nickname.

In human temporal neocortex these bundles were measured at a mean diameter of 12.10 plus or minus 0.63 micrometres, with a mean centre-to-centre spacing of 29.8 plus or minus 0.91 micrometres, and a density of 11.65 plus or minus 0.44 bundles per 10,000 square micrometres.

Those bundles line up one-to-one with the bundles of myelinated axons that also run vertically, so the inhibitory scaffold and the output scaffold of the microcolumn share an address.

SIZE:

The cell body is small to medium, roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across.

The dendritic tufts each extend 150 to 300 micrometres vertically and stay within about 100 micrometres horizontally.

The horsetail descends 800 to 1500 micrometres, from layer 2 down through layers 3, 4 and into layer 5, while staying inside a cylinder about 12 micrometres wide.

Total axonal length is estimated in the several millimetres, carrying on the order of 2000 to 5000 boutons, with the bouton density highest in layer 3.

The morphometric numbers above are directly measured in human tissue and are among the more solid figures in this chapter; the bouton counts are estimates.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Calbindin-dominant double bouquet cells, the classic form, with a long horsetail through layers 2 to 5.

• Calretinin-co-expressing double bouquet cells, which in human temporal cortex are the vast majority, since most calbindin-positive horsetails are also calretinin-positive.

• Short-horsetail variants confined to layers 2 and 3.

• Somatostatin-expressing forms, reflecting the finding that more than 40 percent of calbindin cells in human cortex carry somatostatin and more than 80 percent of somatostatin cells carry calbindin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in layer 2 and upper layer 3 of the neocortex.

The horsetails are found across the cortical mantle, and have been documented in primary visual cortex (area 17), secondary visual cortex (area 18), temporal neocortex, and prefrontal areas, with regional differences in bundle density.

The critical interface is vertical rather than horizontal: the cell straddles the layer 2 to layer 3 boundary with its soma and crosses the layer 4 to layer 5 boundary with its axon.

Because the axon bundle sits inside a bundle of myelinated pyramidal axons, the cell also lives at the interface between the inhibitory network and the cortical output cable tract.

NUMBER:

I allocate about 170 million double bouquet cells, with a defensible range of 120 to 250 million.

That is roughly 5.3 percent of cortical inhibitory neurons, about 1.0 percent of all neocortical neurons, and about 0.2 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is moderate.

The anchor is good for once: at about 11.65 bundles per 10,000 square micrometres of cortical surface, and a human neocortical surface area of roughly 2000 square centimetres for both hemispheres, the horsetail count comes out in the hundreds of millions, which is consistent with the allocation above.

The uncertainty is whether every bundle corresponds to exactly one cell, which is not established.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting membrane voltage around minus 65 millivolts.

Input resistance moderate to high, roughly 150 to 350 megohms.

Firing is adapting and non-fast-spiking, with a clear delay to the first spike in some cells, and typical steady rates of 5 to 30 spikes per second.

Human recordings show that spine-innervating double bouquet cells receive spontaneous inhibitory currents that are modulated differently from those of parvalbumin dendrite-targeting cells by group three metabotropic glutamate receptors, a class of slow glutamate sensor, so their input tuning is pharmacologically distinct.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input comes from local pyramidal cells within the same or adjacent microcolumn and from thalamic afferents.

Output is startlingly selective: in monkey cortex about 48 percent of calbindin-positive double bouquet cell synapses are onto dendritic spines, most of the rest onto dendritic shafts, and essentially none onto somata.

The postsynaptic partners include both pyramidal cells and non-pyramidal cells.

Because spines are where excitatory synapses live, this cell places its inhibition exactly where excitation arrives, one spine at a time.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, in the range 0.1 to 0.3 micrometres, unmyelinated, slow, with conduction velocities well under 0.5 metres per second.

Boutons are packed closely along wavy collaterals, giving the beaded appearance under the microscope.

The defining feature is geometric: the arbor is one to two orders of magnitude narrower horizontally than that of any basket cell.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the transmitter, acting mainly on GABA-A receptors on spine heads and dendritic shafts.

Calbindin D-28k and calretinin are the calcium-binding markers; they are not released.

A substantial minority co-release somatostatin, a peptide that acts on somatostatin receptors over seconds and suppresses local excitability, and some carry neuropeptide Y.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated, but with a twist that matters for simulation: the horsetail runs inside a bundle of heavily myelinated pyramidal axons.

That means this unmyelinated inhibitory axon is packed among insulated cables, in a geometry that favours ephaptic effects, where the electric field of one axon nudges its neighbours without any synapse.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate.

Thousands of boutons must be supplied with vesicles and mitochondria over a long thin axon, so axonal transport is a continuous cost, but the low firing rate keeps sodium pumping modest.

PLASTICITY:

Spine-targeted inhibition can be gated on and off spine by spine, and the number of inhibitory synapses on any given spine changes with experience.

The cell's own excitatory inputs show long-term plasticity, and group three metabotropic glutamate receptors provide a slow presynaptic control that shifts its inhibitory input over minutes.

Because the cell defines microcolumn boundaries, its structural plasticity is one plausible substrate for the sharpening of cortical maps during learning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here have an unusual job: they must clear GABA from a very confined vertical cylinder without letting it spill sideways, and astrocytic processes are correspondingly aligned along the bundle.

Oligodendrocytes, the cells that make myelin, are abundant in the same bundle because of the myelinated pyramidal axons, and their processes physically bound the horsetail, which may be what keeps it so narrow.

Microglia are unremarkable here.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete double bouquet cells and the cortex loses its vertical grain.

Activity that should stay in one microcolumn would bleed sideways into its neighbours, so fine discriminations that depend on adjacent columns representing slightly different things - two nearby orientations, two neighbouring fingertips, two similar speech sounds - would blur together.

The region would still respond, but with lower spatial resolution and less crisp tuning.

In a human brain this would also erase one of the few architectural features that visibly distinguishes primate cortex from rodent cortex.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Inhibition placed on a spine head does not merely subtract; it divides, because the open chloride channel short-circuits the excitatory current arriving at that spine, a mechanism called shunting inhibition.

That makes this cell a multiplier, not an adder.

Somatostatin released from a subset of these cells acts by volume transmission down the same narrow cylinder, giving a slow, spatially confined suppression.

The tight packing of the horsetail inside a myelinated bundle permits ephaptic coupling and constrains the extracellular potassium and GABA concentration inside the column.

Local dendritic protein synthesis supports the maintenance of the long, thin, bouton-dense axon, and the cell instructs the placement of inhibitory synapses onto spines through cell-adhesion signalling.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Local pyramidal excitation, 0 to 40 spikes per second per afferent, glutamatergic, mainly onto proximal dendrites.

◦ Thalamic excitation, 0 to 50 spikes per second.

◦ Inhibition from other interneurons, modulated by group three metabotropic glutamate receptors.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto pyramidal dendritic spines, about 48 percent of contacts, peak conductance 0.2 to 0.8 nanosiemens per contact.

◦ GABA onto dendritic shafts, most of the remainder.

◦ Somatostatin by volume transmission in a subset, effective radius under 20 micrometres.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, adaptation current, per-bouton vesicle pool, per-spine chloride conductance, and the column identity index that determines which targets are reachable.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Bundle radius about 6 micrometres, bundle spacing 29.8 micrometres, bouton count 2000 to 5000, spine-targeting fraction 0.48, resting voltage minus 65 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ GABA-A decay 6 to 10 milliseconds, membrane 12 to 20 milliseconds, adaptation 100 to 400 milliseconds, somatostatin action 1 to 10 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss or thinning of calbindin-positive interneurons is reported in schizophrenia and in some epilepsies, and in simulation the predicted consequence is loss of microcolumnar separation, with excitation spreading laterally and cortical tuning becoming coarse.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to apply the shunting inhibition of one double bouquet cell to the dendritic spines inside its own microcolumn and to no others.
Create a number variable called bundle_radius with initial value six.
Create a number variable called spine_index with initial value zero.
Create a list variable called spine_conductances with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called spikes_delivered with initial value zero.
For each spine in the local tissue volume, perform the following operations.
    Set horizontal_distance to the distance from the spine to the centre of the horsetail.
    If horizontal_distance is less than or equal to bundle_radius, then perform the following actions.
        Set bouton_density to the bouton density of the horsetail at the depth of this spine.
        Calculate the product of bouton_density and zero point zero zero one.
        Set added_conductance to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set added_conductance to zero.
    End the conditional block.
    Add added_conductance to the end of spine_conductances.
    Set spine_index to the sum of spine_index and one.
End the loop.
Repeat the following steps exactly twenty times.
    For each conductance in spine_conductances, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the product of conductance and zero point eight eight.
        Set conductance to the result.
        Calculate the product of conductance and sixty.
        Set shunt_factor to the sum of one and the result.
        Calculate the quotient of one divided by shunt_factor.
        Set spine_gain to the result.
        Display spine_gain to the console.
    End the loop.
    Set spikes_delivered to the sum of spikes_delivered and one.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the computation is geometric bookkeeping over a column, not timing critical.
import math

BUNDLE_RADIUS = 15.0        # micrometres; bundles sit about 30 um apart in human cortex
TAU_GABA_A = 8.0            # milliseconds, decay of the inhibitory conductance

def bouton_density(depth_um):
    """Boutons per 100 um of horsetail axon, densest in layer 3."""
    return 12.0 * math.exp(-((depth_um - 700.0) ** 2) / (2.0 * 400.0 ** 2))

class Spine:
    def __init__(self, x_um, depth_um):
        self.x, self.depth, self.g, self.gain = x_um, depth_um, 0.0, 1.0

spines = [Spine(x, d) for x in (2.0, 9.0, 22.0, 40.0)
                      for d in (300.0, 700.0, 1100.0, 1600.0)]

def horsetail_spike(spines, dt=1.0):
    """One action potential in the double bouquet cell, then dt milliseconds of decay."""
    for s in spines:
        if abs(s.x) <= BUNDLE_RADIUS:                     # only inside its own bundle
            s.g += 0.0010 * bouton_density(s.depth)       # nanosiemens added per spike
        s.g *= math.exp(-dt / TAU_GABA_A)
        s.gain = 1.0 / (1.0 + 60.0 * s.g)                 # shunting divides the spine gain

for _ in range(20):                                       # a 20 Hz-equivalent train
    horsetail_spike(spines)

for s in spines:
    if s.x in (2.0, 40.0):
        print("x=%5.1f um  depth=%6.1f um  g=%7.5f nS  spine gain=%5.3f"
              % (s.x, s.depth, s.g, s.gain))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the cortex makes a pencil-thin vertical rope of axon a dozen micrometres wide and more than a millimetre long, spaced at regular 30 micrometre intervals across the whole sheet, targeting spines and never somata.

If you see the horsetail, you have seen a double bouquet cell.

CHAPTER 27. SMALL BASKET CELLS OF THE VIP TYPE (Slim-Column Soma-Targeting Neuron) (COG)

This is the cortex's dimmer switch: a small cell that grips the body of its neighbour, then lets that neighbour tell it to loosen its grip.

The existing label welds a shape word onto a peptide barcode (VIP, or vasoactive intestinal polypeptide), which tells a reader nothing about the cell's function.

Slim-Column names the reach: the axons run vertically in a narrow columnar plume rather than spreading sideways like large basket cells, confining restraint to a thin vertical slice of cortex.

Soma-Targeting describes the precise target: these cells throw terminals around the cell bodies (soma) and proximal dendrites of their targets, right where a neuron's output decision is made.

Neuron marks it as a functional cortical interneuron, whose specific behavioral role within the vasoactive intestinal polypeptide population remains an active area of study.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This small inhibitory neuron of the cerebral cortex quietens other cells rather than exciting them.

It is a basket cell because its output fibre weaves a loose basket around its neighbours' cell bodies, and the VIP type because it makes vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP), a small messenger first found in the gut.

It sets how hard inhibition presses at the exact point where a target neuron decides whether to fire.

The target can push back with fatty messengers that switch this cell off for seconds, so this is inhibition the recipient can negotiate with.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round with three to five branches leaving in different directions, a shape called multipolar.

Its receiving branches, the dendrites, are smooth rather than studded, and its output fibre, the axon, coils into a dense local tangle whose swellings cluster around neighbouring cell bodies.

SIZE:

• Cell body diameter is 10 to 15 micrometres, one seventh the width of a human hair.

• The dendritic field spans 150 to 300 micrometres and the axon tangle 200 to 400 micrometres.

• Total axon length is a few millimetres, carrying roughly 800 to 2000 release sites.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Compact small basket cells of layers two and three, with the tightest tangle.

• Looser nest basket variants forming open cradles rather than baskets.

• Sparser deep variants in layers five and six with wider axon fields.

• A human-enriched form with more elaborate dendrites than its rodent equivalent.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It occupies the whole neocortex, the six-layered sheet covering each hemisphere, commonest in layers two and three and sparser in layers five and six.

Many sit exactly at the layer one and layer two boundary, sending dendrites into layer one, so they read surface feedback and act one layer below it.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 120 million in both hemispheres, range 80 to 200 million, with low to moderate confidence because nobody has counted this type directly in a human brain.

That is about 4 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion inhibitory neurons of the neocortex, 0.7 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons counted by Azevedo and colleagues in 2009, and 0.14 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts and input resistance is high at 200 to 400 megaohms, so small inputs move it easily.

Its spike is broad at 0.6 to 1.0 milliseconds, firing is irregular and slows during steady input, and top sustained rates are only 20 to 60 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from nearby excitatory pyramidal cells and from long-range fibres carrying acetylcholine from the basal forebrain and serotonin from the brainstem raphe nuclei.

Outputs land on pyramidal cell bodies and first dendritic segments, reaching roughly 10 to 25 percent of pyramidal cells within 150 micrometres.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres thick and conducts at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second.

Release depends on N-type calcium channels built from the Cav2.2 protein, sitting far from the release machinery, which makes release unreliable, jittery and prone to continuing after the spike.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fast transmitter is gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory chemical, co-packaged with vasoactive intestinal peptide and usually cholecystokinin (CCK).

Its terminals carry the cannabinoid type one receptor (CB1), the receptor that responds to the active ingredient of cannabis.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none, since myelin is fatty insulation that speeds conduction and these thin local axons run bare.

Short patches are reported on the first stretch of some human interneuron axons, but they are the exception.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, because slow firing and a short axon keep the pumping bill far below a fast-spiking basket cell's.

Staining for cytochrome oxidase, an enzyme of the cell's power plants, is low to moderate.

PLASTICITY:

Its defining plasticity runs backwards: an active target makes endocannabinoid molecules, chiefly 2-arachidonoylglycerol, that drift back onto this cell's CB1 receptors.

The result, depolarisation-induced suppression of inhibition, silences output for seconds to tens of seconds, and repeated pairing locks it in for hours.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Unlike parvalbumin basket cells this one wears no perineuronal net, the lattice that stiffens its faster cousins, so it stays structurally flexible for life.

Its astrocytes carry monoacylglycerol lipase, the enzyme that destroys the endocannabinoid signal, so these support cells set how far and how long that retrograde message spreads.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cortex keeps its clock but loses its volume knob.

Metronomic inhibition would continue, but nothing could soften it according to what the target was doing, so activity would stop swinging with attention, arousal and reward.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Release keeps dribbling for hundreds of milliseconds after a burst, because leftover calcium keeps triggering vesicles, turning a spike train into a smear.

Peptides leave by volume transmission, diffusing through the fluid between cells to many targets at once, and receptors on the axon itself let the output change without the cell body knowing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: pyramidal glutamate synapses at 0 to 40 events per second; acetylcholine at 0 to 10; serotonin through the fast type three receptor; endocannabinoid level from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto pyramidal cell bodies and proximal dendrites as a synchronous quantum plus an asynchronous tail; peptides into the extracellular space during bursts.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation current; residual terminal calcium; CB1 occupancy; releasable vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 200 to 400 megaohms; threshold minus 42 millivolts; release probability 0.2 to 0.4; maximum cannabinoid suppression 60 to 90 percent.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; asynchronous tail 50 to 300 milliseconds; cannabinoid suppression 5 to 20 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: adolescent cannabis exposure clamps CB1 receptors and, in animal models, permanently weakens this inhibition, and loss of the type disrupts theta rhythms and the cortical rhythm abnormalities reported in schizophrenia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to produce the two-wave output of a vasoactive intestinal peptide small basket cell whose target has recently answered back with a cannabinoid signal.
Create a number variable called depolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called endocannabinoid level with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called release strength with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called release times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 400, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the sum of depolarisation and 3.
    Set depolarisation to the result.
    Check if depolarisation is greater than 21.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of 1 and endocannabinoid level.
        Set release strength to the result.
        Add clock to the end of release times.
        Calculate the sum of clock and 15.
        Add the result to the end of release times.
        Set depolarisation to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 1.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
Display release strength to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is bookkeeping of release events over time, where readability matters more than speed.

# VIP small basket cell: cannabinoid damping plus a delayed second wave.
DT, V_REST, V_THRESH, TAU_M = 1.0, -63.0, -42.0, 12.0
ASYNC_DELAY, EC_RISE, EC_DECAY = 15, 0.01, 0.98

def run(drive, target_active):
    v, ec, events = V_REST, 0.0, []     # ec is endocannabinoid level, 0 to 1
    for t, i_in in enumerate(drive):
        v += DT * (-(v - V_REST) + 40.0 * i_in) / TAU_M
        if v > V_THRESH:
            s = max(0.0, 1.0 - ec)
            events.append((t, 0.7 * s))                # synchronous quantum
            events.append((t + ASYNC_DELAY, 0.3 * s))  # asynchronous tail
            v = V_REST
        if target_active[t]:
            ec = min(1.0, ec + EC_RISE)
        ec *= EC_DECAY
    return events

if __name__ == "__main__":
    ev = run([1.0] * 300, [False] * 150 + [True] * 150)
    print("before %.2f, after %.2f" % (sum(s for t, s in ev if t < 150),
                                       sum(s for t, s in ev if t >= 150)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small cortical cell with a broad spike, a tight axon that baskets its neighbours' cell bodies, vasoactive intestinal peptide inside it, and a terminal that falls silent for ten seconds after the target gets busy.

CHAPTER 28. LARGE AND SMALL BASKET CELLS EXPRESSING CHOLECYSTOKININ (Feeling-Coloured Loose-Timing Neuron) (COG)

Cannabis works on your mind largely by talking to this one cell, which carries more receptors for the drug than almost any other neuron in the brain.

The existing title is a committee minute naming size and a peptide, cholecystokinin, in one unspeakable phrase.

Feeling-Coloured records that about eighty-six percent of these cells carry the type 1 cannabinoid receptor, making them the perisomatic channel through which the brain's own cannabis-like messengers and emotional states dial inhibition.

Loose-Timing states the defining physiological contrast with parvalbumin basket cells: these terminals use N-type calcium channels loosely coupled to the release sensor, making their gamma-aminobutyric acid release asynchronous and smeared in time rather than clock-precise.

Neuron marks it as a principal perisomatic interneuron whose modulation directly influences the power of cortical gamma rhythms.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This family of inhibitory cortical neurons wraps its output fibres around the cell bodies of excitatory neurons and makes cholecystokinin (CCK), a peptide messenger named for its first known job of contracting the gallbladder.

Like every basket cell it inhibits at the target's decision point, but where fast parvalbumin baskets act as a metronome, this family is a mood-sensitive brake whose release is slow, imprecise and easily fatigued.

The working summary is that parvalbumin cells set the clock and cholecystokinin cells set the emotional tone of inhibition.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is oval to pear shaped, with smooth dendrites that in the large form climb from the middle layers to layer one.

The axon often leaves from a dendrite rather than the cell body, then curls into branches bearing large, well-spaced swellings that cradle target cell bodies loosely.

SIZE:

• Small form: cell body 10 to 15 micrometres, axon field 200 to 400 micrometres wide.

• Large form: cell body 15 to 25 micrometres, axon field 500 to 900 micrometres wide across two or three layers.

• Total axon length is 3 to 15 millimetres, with 1000 release sites in the small form and over 5000 in the large.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Small cholecystokinin basket cells with a compact single-layer axon field.

• Large cholecystokinin basket cells crossing layers and column boundaries.

• A subgroup carrying vesicular glutamate transporter type three, releasing a little glutamate alongside GABA.

• A subgroup co-expressing vasoactive intestinal peptide, overlapping the previous entry.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy the whole neocortex, concentrated in layers two, three and five and present in layers four and six.

The large form sits in layer three or upper layer five with dendrites reaching the layer one and layer two interface, sampling feedback from other cortical areas and the thalamus, and the same family populates the hippocampus and amygdala.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 230 million in both hemispheres, range 150 to 350 million, counting only cells not already counted as the vasoactive intestinal peptide small basket type, with low to moderate confidence.

That is about 7 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, 1.4 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and 0.27 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is minus 60 to minus 68 millivolts and input resistance is 120 to 300 megaohms.

The spike is broad at 0.7 to 1.2 milliseconds because these cells lack the fast potassium channels of parvalbumin cells, firing accommodates from an opening burst down to 10 to 50 spikes per second, and many fire irregularly under constant input.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory inputs facilitate, meaning each spike in a train produces a larger response, so this cell is recruited by sustained activity rather than single events.

Outputs target pyramidal cell bodies and proximal dendrites, a third of boutons land on other inhibitory cells, and connection probability onto neighbours is 20 to 40 percent for the small form.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axon diameter is 0.2 to 0.6 micrometres and conduction runs at 0.2 to 0.6 metres per second.

Release depends on N-type Cav2.2 channels rather than the P/Q-type Cav2.1 channels of parvalbumin cells, and that difference, with loose coupling to the release sensor, explains the unreliability, jitter and long trailing release.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fast transmitter is gamma aminobutyric acid, co-packaged with cholecystokinin, which is released during bursts and acts on the cholecystokinin type two receptor.

The terminals carry the densest cannabinoid type one receptors in the cortex, plus mu-opioid and muscarinic acetylcholine receptors, all of which throttle release.

MYELINATION:

Almost entirely absent, with short patchy segments reported on the thicker proximal axons of some large basket cells.

The bouton-bearing branches are always bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and far below a parvalbumin basket cell, because firing rates are low.

Terminals still hold healthy mitochondria, since trailing release for hundreds of milliseconds demands continuous calcium pumping.

PLASTICITY:

Depolarisation-induced suppression of inhibition is stronger here than at any other cortical synapse, cutting release by 60 to 90 percent for five to twenty seconds.

A constant background cannabinoid tone also holds baseline release below maximum, and pairing target activity with firing gives long-term depression lasting hours.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

No perineuronal net surrounds these cells, in contrast with the parvalbumin baskets beside them, so they stay plastic into adulthood.

Their astrocytes are part of the signalling loop rather than bystanders, holding the enzyme that clears the endocannabinoid signal and carrying cannabinoid receptors themselves.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and cortical inhibition becomes emotionally deaf.

The region keeps precise gamma-band inhibition but loses the slow brake that reports arousal, stress, satiety and reward, and loses all perisomatic tuning by cannabinoids, opioids, serotonin and acetylcholine.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Asynchronous release is the headline feature: after a burst this terminal keeps dribbling GABA for two hundred to five hundred milliseconds, turning a spike train into a lasting blanket.

Cholecystokinin leaves by volume transmission and excites neighbouring parvalbumin basket cells, so this cell chemically hands control to its faster cousin.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: facilitating glutamate synapses at 0 to 50 events per second; acetylcholine and serotonin at 0 to 10; endocannabinoid and opioid levels from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: synchronous and asynchronous GABA onto pyramidal cell bodies and proximal dendrites; cholecystokinin into the extracellular space.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation current; residual presynaptic calcium; vesicle pool occupancy; cannabinoid receptor occupancy.

• PARAMETERS: spike half-width 0.7 to 1.2 milliseconds; input resistance 120 to 300 megaohms; synchronous release probability 0.15 to 0.35; maximum cannabinoid suppression 0.6 to 0.9.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 12 to 25 milliseconds; residual calcium decay 40 to 100 milliseconds; asynchronous tail 200 to 500 milliseconds; cannabinoid suppression 5 to 20 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: adolescent cannabinoid overstimulation permanently weakens this synapse and reduces prefrontal gamma power, a finding repeatedly linked to schizophrenia risk, and abnormal wiring of these cells disrupts spatial coding.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model the two waves of inhibition from a cholecystokinin basket terminal, one locked to the spike and one trailing behind it on leftover calcium.
Create a number variable called residual calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cannabinoid occupancy with initial value 0.5.
Create a number variable called channel gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called synchronous output with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called asynchronous output with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called late release with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Calculate the difference of 1 and cannabinoid occupancy.
Set channel gain to the result.
While clock is less than 400, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the remainder of clock divided by 25.
    Check if the result is equal to 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of residual calcium and channel gain.
        Set residual calcium to the result.
        Calculate the sum of synchronous output and channel gain.
        Set synchronous output to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of residual calcium and residual calcium.
    Set late release to the result.
    Calculate the sum of asynchronous output and late release.
    Set asynchronous output to the result.
    Calculate the product of residual calcium and 0.98.
    Set residual calcium to the result.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 1.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
Display asynchronous output to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this is a tight numerical loop over a fine time step, the case where a compiled kernel belongs inside a simulation engine.

/* CCK basket terminal: N-type calcium channels that CB1 throttles.
   Compile with: cc file.c -lm                                     */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.1, tau_ca = 60.0;   /* milliseconds */
    double ca = 0.0, cb1 = 0.0;             /* calcium, CB1 occupancy */
    double sync = 0.0, async_q = 0.0;
    int step;
    for (step = 0; step < 4000; step++) {   /* 400 milliseconds */
        double t = step * dt;
        if (step % 250 == 0) {              /* presynaptic spike at 40 Hz */
            double gain = 1.0 - 0.8 * cb1;  /* CB1 closes N-type channels */
            ca += gain;
            sync += 0.9 * gain;
        }
        if (t > 200.0) cb1 = 1.0;           /* target releases 2-AG */
        async_q += dt * 0.0001 * pow(ca, 4.0);
        ca -= dt * ca / tau_ca;
    }
    printf("synchronous %.2f, asynchronous %.2f\n", sync, async_q);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A basket cell with a broad accommodating spike, boutons that keep leaking inhibition for half a second after the burst ends, and a terminal so thick with cannabinoid receptors that a puff of cannabis silences it.

CHAPTER 29. CORTICAL NEUROGLIAFORM CELLS (COG)

Most neurons whisper into a single ear; this one opens a valve and floods the neighbourhood, inhibiting hundreds of cells it has never formally met.

Neurogliaform describes the cell's dense, spiderweb-like bush of fine processes. This unique geometry allows it to release a cloud of gamma-aminobutyric acid that floods the local tissue, inhibiting nearby neurons through volume transmission without requiring direct synapses.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This small inhibitory neuron grows the densest, finest, most nearly spherical ball of output fibre in the brain, and it was named because it looks more like a glial support cell than a neuron.

It does not aim its transmitter at a partner but releases gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory chemical, into the fluid between cells, where it diffuses and quietens everything inside the cloud.

That mode is called volume transmission, and one spike produces a slow, long inhibitory wave in most of its neighbours at once.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round with seven to twelve short dendrites radiating out like a dandelion clock, branching once or twice and stopping.

The axon splinters immediately into hundreds of hair-thin branches, and its release sites are tiny, extremely numerous, and mostly face no receiving structure, which is the fingerprint of volume transmission.

SIZE:

• Cell body diameter is 8 to 15 micrometres, among the smallest in the cortex.

• The dendritic ball spans 200 to 250 micrometres and the axonal plexus 300 to 400 micrometres.

• Total axon length can exceed 20 millimetres, packing 5000 to 15000 release sites, the highest density of any cortical neuron.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Round neurogliaform cells of layers two to six with the classic spherical plexus.

• Elongated neurogliaform cells of layer one, whose plexus is flattened into a horizontal sheet.

• Ivy cells, a looser-plexus relative found mainly in the hippocampus.

• A primate-enriched group carrying the LHX6 gene, rare in rodents but plentiful in humans.

• The human rosehip cell, a compact relative of human layer one with no mouse counterpart.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy every layer and area of the neocortex, densest in layer one, where the elongated form dominates, and in layer two and upper layer three, and they also populate the hippocampus, amygdala, striatum and parts of the thalamus.

Because so many sit at the layer one and layer two interface, they form an inhibitory curtain where long-range feedback fibres meet the tips of pyramidal dendrites.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 350 million across both hemispheres, range 250 to 450 million, with moderate confidence in the proportion and low confidence in the absolute figure, since gene surveys place the LAMP5 group that contains them at 10 to 20 percent of human cortical inhibitory neurons.

That is about 11 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, 2.1 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and 0.41 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system, of which only about 35 million sit in layer one.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The signature is late spiking: given a current just strong enough to fire it, the voltage creeps up a long slow ramp and the first spike arrives hundreds of milliseconds later.

Resting voltage is minus 62 to minus 70 millivolts, input resistance in mouse layer one is about 173 megaohms, spike width is about 0.7 milliseconds, and voltage sag is small at about 8 percent.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation arrives from local pyramidal cells, from long-range feedback fibres, and in layer one from the higher-order thalamus.

Output reaches everything nearby, including incoming presynaptic terminals, with a connection probability onto layer two pyramidal cells of about 72 percent in mouse layer one, and every target shows a slow GABA type A component followed by a GABA type B component from a single spike.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:
