Branches are the thinnest in the cortex at 0.1 to 0.3 micrometres and conduct at only 0.1 to 0.3 metres per second, though distances are so small that delays stay near a millisecond.

The axon is entirely unmyelinated, as it must be, since myelin would seal the very release sites the cell depends on.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is gamma aminobutyric acid, released into the extracellular space, co-packaged with neuropeptide Y, which suppresses excitability and protects against seizures.

Most also carry neuronal nitric oxide synthase, the enzyme that makes nitric oxide gas on demand, and identifying markers include reelin, alpha-actinin-2 and LAMP5.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable, for a real biological reason rather than a gap in knowledge.

The cell's whole strategy depends on transmitter escaping from bare membrane, so the insulation that would seal that membrane is absent by design.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low to moderate at rest, because the cell is reluctant to fire at all.

Twenty millimetres of axon and thousands of release sites still impose a large standing cost, and nitric oxide production consumes oxygen, so this type suffers early when oxygen runs short.

PLASTICITY:

The output synapse depresses strongly with use, because so many small release sites empty quickly, and recovery takes seconds.

Longer-term change is dominated by regulation of the GABA transporters that mop up the cloud, since cloud size, not any single synapse, is what matters here.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here do something they do nowhere else: they set the physical size of an inhibitory signal, because their GABA transporter type three decides how far the cloud spreads before clearance.

Block those transporters and one spike inhibits a far larger sphere of tissue, while astrocyte end-feet translate this cell's nitric oxide into local widening of blood vessels.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove neurogliaform cells and the cortex loses its only way to inhibit by place rather than by partnership.

Feedback landing in layer one would go unchecked, the slow braking that follows bursts would vanish, and the region would become locally excitable and prone to synchronised discharge.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It reliably recruits the slow GABA type B receptors from a single spike, which no other cortical cell does, and it inhibits incoming axon terminals as well as cells, a form of presynaptic gating.

It is electrically coupled by gap junctions not only to its own kind but unusually to other classes of inhibitory neuron, and it widens local blood vessels with nitric oxide while narrowing them with neuropeptide Y.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate synapses from pyramidal cells and long-range feedback at 0 to 30 events per second; higher-order thalamic input in layer one; gap junction current of 0 to 50 picoamperes.

• OUTPUTS: an extracellular GABA cloud peaking at 1 to 10 micromolar within 20 micrometres; slow GABA type A and type B currents in every neuron inside it; nitric oxide and neuropeptide Y onto local arterioles.

• STATE: membrane voltage; the slow ramp variable behind late spiking; extracellular GABA by radius; vesicle depletion; gap junction coupling of 0.03 to 0.15.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 120 to 250 megaohms; effective GABA diffusion 0.3 to 0.5 square micrometres per millisecond; cloud radius 40 to 100 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds; delay to first spike 100 to 800 milliseconds; slow GABA type A current 50 to 200 milliseconds; GABA type B current 150 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: silencing these cells removes the slow blanket brake and favours seizure spread, and their nitric oxide role makes them a probable weak link in small-vessel disease and the blood flow failures of early Alzheimer's disease.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to work out which neurons a single neurogliaform spike inhibits, given that its transmitter escapes into the space between cells rather than being aimed at a partner.
Create a number variable called cloud radius with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called maximum radius with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called neuron distances with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called inhibited neurons with initial value empty list.
Add 8 to the end of neuron distances.
Add 25 to the end of neuron distances.
Add 90 to the end of neuron distances.
While clock is less than 200, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the product of clock and 0.35.
    Set cloud radius to the result.
    Check if cloud radius is greater than maximum radius.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set cloud radius to maximum radius.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 1.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
For each distance in neuron distances, perform the following operations.
    Check if distance is less than cloud radius.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add distance to the end of inhibited neurons.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in inhibited neurons.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the physics is a short diffusion calculation that the standard mathematics library covers with no external package.

# One neurogliaform spike: point-source diffusion plus transporter clearance.
import math

D_EFF, UPTAKE_TAU = 0.4, 90.0   # um^2 per ms, clearance constant in ms
K_GABAB, N_HILL, SCALE = 4.0, 2.0, 2000.0   # uM, Hill exponent, source scale

def gaba_at(r_um, t_ms):
    spread = 4.0 * math.pi * D_EFF * t_ms
    c = SCALE / (spread ** 1.5) * math.exp(-(r_um * r_um) / spread)
    return c * math.exp(-t_ms / UPTAKE_TAU)

def run(radius_um):
    gabab, peak = 0.0, 0.0
    for t in range(1, 601):                   # 600 milliseconds
        c = gaba_at(radius_um, float(t))
        peak = max(peak, c)
        target = c ** N_HILL / (c ** N_HILL + K_GABAB ** N_HILL)
        gabab += (target - gabab) / 150.0     # slow G protein step
    return peak, gabab

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for r in (5.0, 20.0, 60.0):
        print("radius %4.0f um: peak %6.3f uM, GABA-B %.4f" % ((r,) + run(r)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A tiny cell that takes half a second to decide to fire, then inhibits everything within fifty micrometres whether or not it has synapses on them, while controlling the local blood supply on the side.

CHAPTER 30. SINGLE BOUQUET CELLS (COG)

This cell inhibits only the inhibitors, so when it fires, the cortex below it gets louder, not quieter.

Single Bouquet describes the cell's defining shape - a single descending tuft of axon that drops from layer 1 into layers 2 and 3. Naming this single tuft maintains clarity while keeping it cleanly paired with the double bouquet cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This inhibitory neuron lives in layer one, the thin, almost cell-free skin at the top of the cerebral cortex, and drops its output fibre straight down into layers two and three like the stem of a bouquet.

Its remarkable property is what it inhibits: not excitatory pyramidal cells but the inhibitory cells that hold pyramidal cells down, and silencing a silencer is called disinhibition.

When feedback from a higher brain area says that something matters, this cell lifts the brakes off a small group of deep output neurons.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The small cell body sits in the lower half of layer one, with short dendrites spreading horizontally to sample the feedback fibres running there.

The axon descends as a horsetail, a slim vertical bundle of parallel branches passing through layer two into layer three, and the name records its resemblance to the descending half of the deeper double bouquet cell.

SIZE:

• Cell body diameter is 8 to 14 micrometres.

• The dendritic field is horizontal, 100 to 250 micrometres wide, confined to layer one.

• The descending bundle is 50 to 100 micrometres across and reaches 300 to 500 micrometres down.

• Total axon length is 2 to 6 millimetres with an estimated 1000 to 3000 release sites.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Classic single bouquet cells with a tight horsetail inside one cortical column.

• Broader variants whose bundle splays across two neighbouring columns.

• Cells overlapping the layer one vasoactive intestinal peptide group, whose axons reach layers five and six.

• Cells overlapping the layer one alpha7 group, whose collaterals stop in layer five A.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in neocortical layer one, chiefly its lower half, immediately beneath the pia mater, the delicate membrane covering the brain, and the axon crosses into layers two and three, so the cell straddles the most important boundary for top-down control in the cortex.

They are described in rodent somatosensory, visual and frontal cortex, with comparable cells reported in monkey and human, and the pyramidal cells that ultimately benefit sit in layer five.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 25 million across both hemispheres, range 15 to 45 million, and confidence is low: layer one holds on the order of 0.5 to 1 percent of cortical neurons and no human count of this type exists.

That is about 0.8 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, 0.15 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and 0.03 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system, and because this population partly overlaps the layer one vasoactive intestinal peptide and alpha7 groups it must not simply be added to their totals.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is minus 58 to minus 65 millivolts and input resistance is high at 200 to 400 megaohms, so a small feedback input fires the cell.

It is not late spiking: it answers a current step with a prompt burst of two to five spikes, then adapts strongly and irregularly, with a spike width of 0.5 to 0.8 milliseconds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from long-range feedback axons from higher cortical areas, from higher-order thalamic nuclei such as the posterior medial nucleus, and from cholinergic fibres of the basal forebrain.

Outputs land almost exclusively on inhibitory cells, contacting seven distinguishable subtypes of layer two and three interneuron that between them inhibit the whole length of a layer five pyramidal cell, and the connection is strictly one-way with no return inhibition and no electrical coupling.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The descending axon is 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, so the trip from layer one to layer three takes one to two milliseconds.

Its small boutons sit on the cell bodies and proximal dendrites of the target interneurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is gamma aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical.

Subsets carry vasoactive intestinal peptide or the alpha7 nicotinic receptor, and all are strongly driven by acetylcholine, the attention transmitter, which is how a change of behavioural state throws this switch.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable in practice.

The descending axon is short, thin and locally branching, and is unmyelinated throughout, because insulating three hundred micrometres is not worth the metabolic cost.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, because the cell is small, fires in brief bursts and maintains only a few millimetres of axon.

Its energy use rises sharply during high acetylcholine states, that is during attention and active exploration, which is exactly when it is working.

PLASTICITY:

Its output synapses onto interneurons depress with use, so a long burst yields diminishing disinhibition and the switch resets itself.

The feedback input onto its dendrites is experience-dependent, and in developing cortex bottom-up sensory input must arrive first for top-down connections onto layer one cells to form correctly.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

This cell sits inside the glia limitans, the astrocyte boundary separating brain tissue from the fluid space beneath the pia mater, so its astrocytes are doing barrier duty at the same time.

In humans it also sits among interlaminar astrocytes, a primate specialisation whose single long fibres dive through the layers beside its own descending axon.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cortex loses its fast, targeted way of saying yes.

Feedback could still suppress activity through neurogliaform cells but could no longer release a specific column of deep output neurons, so attention-driven boosting would weaken and the cortex would become a system that can only turn things down.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The essential non-classical feature is sign inversion: this cell is inhibitory yet its net effect is excitatory, so any simulation treating inhibition as uniformly suppressive gets the wrong answer.

Its targets are chosen by cell class rather than proximity, so wiring cannot be modelled as a distance rule, and the deliberate absence of reciprocal and electrical coupling keeps the switch one-way.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: long-range cortical feedback glutamate synapses at 0 to 30 events per second; higher-order thalamic synapses at 0 to 20; acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors at 0 to 15.

• OUTPUTS: one-way GABA release onto seven classes of layer two and three interneuron, unitary currents of 20 to 80 picoamperes.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation current; vesicle depletion at each target class; nicotinic receptor desensitisation.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 200 to 400 megaohms; threshold about minus 40 millivolts; 0.4 to 0.8 hertz of target firing lost per hertz of this cell's firing; conduction delay 1 to 2 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds; adaptation 100 to 300 milliseconds; depression recovery 0.5 to 2 seconds; disinhibitory window 50 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: stuck on, deep output neurons lose inhibitory control and the cortex becomes hyperexcitable; stuck off, top-down attention cannot select a column, consistent with the attentional and sensory gating deficits reported in schizophrenia and autism spectrum conditions.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show how one single bouquet cell in layer one raises the firing rate of a layer five pyramidal cell by silencing the layer two and three interneurons that were holding it down.
Create a list variable called interneuron rates with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called single bouquet rate with initial value 10.
Create a number variable called suppression per hertz with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called weight to pyramid with initial value 0.8.
Create a number variable called suppression with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called silenced rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called contribution with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pyramid rate with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 7 times.
    Add 8 to the end of interneuron rates.
End the loop.
Calculate the product of single bouquet rate and suppression per hertz.
Set suppression to the result.
For each baseline rate in interneuron rates, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of baseline rate and suppression.
    Set silenced rate to the result.
    Calculate the product of silenced rate and weight to pyramid.
    Set contribution to the result.
    Calculate the sum of total inhibition and contribution.
    Set total inhibition to the result.
End the loop.
Calculate the difference of 20 and total inhibition.
Set pyramid rate to the result.
Display pyramid rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is named-population bookkeeping across seven interneuron classes, and a dictionary makes the class-specific wiring self-documenting.

# Single bouquet cell inhibits layer 2/3 interneurons, so firing it RAISES
# layer 5 pyramidal output. Inhibition with a plus sign.
IN_TO_PYR = {"basket": 1.2, "chandelier": 1.5, "martinotti": 0.8,
             "bipolar": 0.4, "double_bouquet": 0.5,
             "neurogliaform": 0.7, "bitufted": 0.6}
SBC_WEIGHT, BASELINE_HZ = 0.6, 8.0   # target hertz lost per hertz; resting rate
PYR_DRIVE_HZ, INHIB_GAIN = 20.0, 0.35

def pyramid_rate(sbc_hz):
    total = 0.0
    for name, w in IN_TO_PYR.items():
        rate = BASELINE_HZ - SBC_WEIGHT * sbc_hz
        if rate < 0.0:
            rate = 0.0                   # cannot fire below zero
        total += w * rate
    out = PYR_DRIVE_HZ - INHIB_GAIN * total
    return out if out > 0.0 else 0.0

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for sbc in (0.0, 5.0, 10.0, 15.0):
        print("bouquet %4.1f Hz -> pyramid %5.2f Hz" % (sbc, pyramid_rate(sbc)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A layer one cell with a slim horsetail axon dropping into layers two and three, contacting interneurons and almost nothing else, one way only, with no return connection and no electrical coupling.

CHAPTER 31. CANOPY CELLS (COG)

Spreading a thin horizontal net just under the surface of the brain, this cell touches almost nothing directly beneath it.

Canopy is a plain-English shape name: the axon spreads as a flat horizontal sheet across the upper half of layer 1, lying over the cortical tissue like a protective roof.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This inhibitory neuron lives almost exclusively in layer one, the thin outermost sheet of the cerebral cortex, and stretches its output fibre sideways across the upper half of that layer.

It was named in 2019, when recording and gene profiling showed that the cells previously lumped together in layer one were four separate populations.

Its role appears to be light, wide, purely fast inhibition of incoming feedback fibres and of the tips of pyramidal dendrites, delivered as a brief burst that reports change rather than level.

Honesty matters here: the type is newly recognised and its precise computational role is still being worked out.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits in layer one, with dendrites that spread wider than a neurogliaform cell's but branch far less, giving a broad sparse receiving field.

The axon runs horizontally, parallel to the brain surface within the upper half of layer one, and its measured density is roughly 65 percent lower than a neurogliaform cell's, so it is a thin net rather than a dense ball.

SIZE:

• Cell body diameter is 10 to 16 micrometres.

• The dendritic field is flat, 200 to 400 micrometres wide but only 50 to 100 micrometres tall.

• The axon net spans 300 to 600 micrometres horizontally within the top 50 micrometres of layer one.

• Total axon length is a few millimetres with perhaps 1000 to 3000 sparse release sites.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The canonical form of the upper half of layer one, matching the LAMP5, FAM19A1 and TMEM182 gene signature.

• Variants in prefrontal cortex whose axon dips slightly into layer two.

• Human layer one contains cells of comparable sparse horizontal geometry, though the correspondence between human and mouse layer one types remains an open question.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in neocortical layer one and essentially nowhere else, which is unusual since most cortical types occupy several layers, and it concentrates immediately beneath the pia mater, the thin membrane covering the brain.

Because its cell body sits above the neurogliaform cells, the two form stacked sheets, and the boundary between them is a real interface inside a layer only a tenth of a millimetre thick in mouse and a few tenths in humans.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 30 million across both hemispheres, range 15 to 50 million, and confidence is low: canopy cells are about 40 percent of layer one interneurons in mouse barrel cortex, layer one holds on the order of 0.5 to 1 percent of neocortical neurons, and no human count exists.

That is about 0.9 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, 0.18 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and 0.035 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is about minus 64 millivolts, input resistance about 142 megaohms, and the spike is narrow at about 0.45 milliseconds.

It is not late spiking but onset spiking, firing promptly at the start of a current step and then adapting fast, and its voltage sag of about 15 percent sits between the neurogliaform cell's 8 percent and the alpha7 cell's 21 percent.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are the classic layer one set: long-range feedback axons from other cortical areas, higher-order thalamic fibres, and fibres carrying acetylcholine and noradrenaline.

Its connection probability onto layer two pyramidal cells is only about 18 percent against roughly 72 percent for neurogliaform cells, its currents are weak and lack any slow GABA type B component, and it forms reciprocal connections with neurogliaform cells so the two sheets inhibit each other.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, conducting at 0.2 to 0.4 metres per second, so an event at one end of the net reaches the other within about two milliseconds.

Running horizontally for long distances by layer one standards, a single canopy cell influences several adjacent cortical columns.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is gamma aminobutyric acid, and the cell carries the marker protein NDNF, short for neuron-derived neurotrophic factor, which it shares with neurogliaform cells.

Unlike neurogliaform cells it lacks neuropeptide Y, and that single negative marker is one of the cleanest ways to tell the two apart.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable.

These axons are unmyelinated over their whole length, because distances are short and layer one contains very few oligodendrocytes, the cells that manufacture myelin.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, since onset spiking with strong adaptation means the cell fires only briefly.

Layer one is thinly supplied with blood vessels compared with deeper layers, and this cell's low duty cycle suits that lean environment.

PLASTICITY:

Layer one is the main landing site for top-down feedback, and the strength of feedback synapses onto layer one interneurons is shaped by experience during development.

Its own output synapses depress moderately with use, and long-term plasticity rules specific to canopy cells have not yet been reported, which it would be wrong to invent.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

This cell sits inside the glia limitans, the astrocyte boundary separating brain tissue from the fluid space beneath the pia mater, so its astrocytes are simultaneously doing barrier duty.

In human cortex it also sits among interlaminar astrocytes, a primate specialisation whose long fibres descend through layers two to four, while microglia here are the first to sense inflammation crossing from the meninges.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the canopy cell and the top of layer one loses its only fast, horizontally spreading brake.

Neurogliaform cells would still deliver slow blanketing inhibition from below, but the crisp wide inhibition that greets the first arrival of a feedback signal would be gone, so feedback would spread with no leading edge to mark where and when it began.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The canopy cell is a spatial integrator, summing feedback across several columns with wide flat dendrites and returning inhibition to the same territory, so it computes an average over horizontal space rather than a local decision.

Onset spiking with strong adaptation makes it a differentiator in time, responding to the rate of change of its input, and the deliberate absence of a GABA type B component divides labour with the neurogliaform sheet below it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: long-range feedback glutamate synapses spread over 300 to 600 micrometres at 0 to 30 events per second; higher-order thalamic synapses; acetylcholine and noradrenaline; reciprocal GABA from neurogliaform cells.

• OUTPUTS: fast GABA type A currents onto distal pyramidal dendritic tufts and other layer one interneurons, 10 to 40 picoamperes, with no GABA type B component.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation current; pooled horizontal input; vesicle depletion.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance about 142 megaohms; resting potential about minus 64 millivolts; spike half-width about 0.45 milliseconds; sag about 15 percent; connection probability onto layer two pyramidal cells about 0.18.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 12 to 20 milliseconds; adaptation 300 to 700 milliseconds; inhibitory current decay 5 to 15 milliseconds; depression recovery 0.3 to 1 second.

• FAILURE MODE: weakening this population is expected to blur the boundary between externally driven and internally generated activity, a pattern discussed in relation to hallucinations and sensory overload, though direct disease evidence is still thin and provisional.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to pool the feedback signals sweeping horizontally across the top of layer one and turn them into the brief onset burst that identifies a canopy cell.
Create a number variable called pooled drive with initial value 36.
Create a number variable called depolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called net drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 500, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the difference of pooled drive and adaptation.
    Set net drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of net drive and 0.07.
    Set net drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of depolarisation and 0.93.
    Set depolarisation to the result.
    Calculate the sum of depolarisation and net drive.
    Set depolarisation to the result.
    Check if depolarisation is greater than 24.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set depolarisation to 6.
        Calculate the sum of adaptation and 2.
        Set adaptation to the result.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 1.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this is a fine-grained integrate-and-fire loop with an adaptation current, and megaohms multiplied by nanoamperes gives millivolts directly, keeping the units honest.

/* Canopy cell: pools horizontal feedback, answers with an onset burst. */
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.1, R = 142.0;           /* ms per step, megaohms */
    const double tau_m = 14.0, tau_w = 500.0;   /* milliseconds          */
    const double v_rest = -64.0, v_th = -40.0, v_reset = -58.0;
    const double dw = 0.03, i_in = 0.40;        /* nanoamperes           */
    double v = v_rest, w = 0.0;
    int step, first = 0, last = 0;
    for (step = 0; step < 5000; step++) {       /* 500 milliseconds */
        double drive = R * (i_in - w);          /* millivolts       */
        v += dt * (-(v - v_rest) + drive) / tau_m;
        w -= dt * w / tau_w;
        if (v >= v_th) {
            v = v_reset;
            w += dw;
            if (step < 1000) first++;
            if (step >= 4000) last++;
        }
    }
    printf("spikes first 100 ms: %d, last 100 ms: %d\n", first, last);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A cell found only in the top half of layer one, with a sparse horizontal axon net, a narrow spike, prompt onset firing, the NDNF marker but no neuropeptide Y, and an inhibitory output with no slow tail.

CHAPTER 32. ALPHA7 CELLS, NAMED FOR A NICOTINIC RECEPTOR SUBUNIT (Fast-Alerting Inhibition Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell nicotine reaches first, and the receptor it is named for is missing a copy in some people with schizophrenia and epilepsy.

The old label names a protein subunit (alpha-7 nicotinic acetylcholine receptor), which tells a student nothing about the cell's actual function.

Fast-Alerting names the speed and signal: the alpha-7 receptor opens and closes in milliseconds - far quicker than other nicotinic receptors - answering a pulse of acetylcholine the instant attention sharpens.

Inhibition names the output: sitting predominantly on layer 1 inhibitory cells, it converts that incoming alerting chemical into a rapid brake applied to the upper dendrites of pyramidal cells.

Neuron marks it as a key cholinergic-responsive interneuron in the upper cortical sheet.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This inhibitory neuron of cortical layer one is named for the alpha7 subunit, a building block of one kind of nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, a channel that opens when acetylcholine, the brain's attention transmitter, binds to it, and which nicotine opens too.

The alpha7 version is unusual in three ways: it opens extremely fast, it shuts itself within milliseconds even while acetylcholine is still present, and it admits calcium far more freely than other nicotinic receptors.

Calcium inside a neuron is not merely charge but a chemical command that switches on enzymes and genes, so this cell turns a puff of acetylcholine into a brief electrical event plus a longer chemical instruction, then inhibits inside layer one and down in the deep output layers.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is the largest of the four layer one interneuron populations, with dendrites leaving in several directions, a shape called multipolar.

The axon branches locally and, distinctively, sends collaterals down to layer five A, the upper part of the fifth layer where a major class of cortex-to-cortex output neurons lives.

SIZE:

• Cell body diameter is 12 to 20 micrometres, the largest in layer one.

• The dendritic field spans 150 to 350 micrometres within layer one.

• The local axon field spans 200 to 400 micrometres and the descending collateral reaches 400 to 700 micrometres down.

• Total axon length is 3 to 8 millimetres with perhaps 1500 to 4000 release sites.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The canonical form of the lower half of layer one, matching the LAMP5 and CHRNA7 gene signature, CHRNA7 being the gene encoding the alpha7 subunit.

• Variants whose descending collateral stops in layer two or three rather than reaching layer five A.

• In humans only, a partially duplicated gene called CHRFAM7A makes a modified subunit that brakes the normal receptor, so the human response differs from the mouse one and varies between individuals.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy the lower half of neocortical layer one and almost nowhere else, making this, with the canopy cell, one of two populations essentially unique to that layer.

Its descending axon crosses layers two, three and four to reach layer five A, so the cell spans the whole sheet and links top-down feedback to cortical output by the shortest anatomical path.

NUMBER:

The best estimate is roughly 16 million across both hemispheres, range 8 to 30 million, with low confidence: alpha7 cells are about 20 percent of layer one interneurons in mouse barrel cortex, layer one holds on the order of 0.5 to 1 percent of neocortical neurons, and there is no human count.

That is about 0.5 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion cortical inhibitory neurons, 0.1 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons, and 0.019 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is about minus 60 millivolts, the most depolarised of the layer one populations, with input resistance about 142 megaohms and a narrow spike of about 0.42 milliseconds.

It shows the largest voltage sag of any layer one cell at about 21.5 percent, produced by hyperpolarisation-activated channels carrying a current called Ih, plus an active depolarising hump just below threshold carried by a calcium conductance.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are dominated by acetylcholine fibres from the basal forebrain acting on its alpha7 receptors, together with long-range cortical feedback and higher-order thalamic synapses arriving in layer one.

Outputs reach other layer one interneurons, the distal dendritic tufts of pyramidal cells, and layer five A neurons through the descending collateral, so its net effect can be inhibitory or disinhibitory depending on which pathway dominates.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, conducting at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, so the trip from layer one to layer five A takes one to three milliseconds.

Boutons on the descending collateral are sparse and spread along its length rather than clustered.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter released is gamma aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical.

The defining receptor received is the alpha7 nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, built from five copies of the alpha7 subunit, notable for a fractional calcium current of roughly 10 percent, and unlike its layer one neighbours this cell lacks the NDNF marker.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable.

Even the long descending collateral is unmyelinated, as is typical of cortical inhibitory axons, and the cell compensates for slow conduction by resting at an unusually depolarised voltage.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, because the high resting potential makes the sodium and potassium pumps work harder at rest than in a cell sitting at minus 70 millivolts.

Calcium arriving through alpha7 receptors must be pumped back out, one of the most expensive housekeeping tasks a neuron performs, and the standing Ih current leaks continuously.

PLASTICITY:

Calcium entering through alpha7 receptors is itself a plasticity signal, switching on calcium-dependent enzymes and gene transcription, so acetylcholine here produces changes outlasting the electrical event by minutes to hours.

The receptors desensitise within milliseconds and recover over hundreds of milliseconds, a fast use-dependent plasticity built into the protein, and chronic nicotine changes their number and sensitivity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here carry alpha7 receptors too, so acetylcholine arriving in layer one triggers calcium waves in the surrounding glia at the same moment it excites this neuron.

Microglia carry alpha7 receptors as part of the cholinergic anti-inflammatory pathway, so acetylcholine here is simultaneously an attention signal and an immune-calming one, and that triple sensitivity is genuinely unusual.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the alpha7 cell and layer one loses its fastest link between acetylcholine and cortical output.

Other layer one cells answer acetylcholine, but only this one answers in milliseconds and delivers the consequence straight down to layer five A, so the region would keep its attention system but lose its reflexes.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The alpha7 receptor is a genuine second messenger source, admitting enough calcium to trigger release from internal stores and drive gene transcription, and its desensitisation means the cell reports the onset of acetylcholine and ignores a steady background.

The Ih current gives rebound firing after inhibition, so the cell can be recruited by being switched off, and through glial alpha7 receptors it joins metabolic and immune loops with no electrical readout at all.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: acetylcholine transients peaking in the tens of micromolar for a few milliseconds; long-range feedback glutamate synapses at 0 to 30 events per second; higher-order thalamic synapses; GABA from other layer one interneurons.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto other layer one interneurons, onto pyramidal distal dendrites, and onto layer five A neurons through the descending collateral.

• STATE: membrane voltage; alpha7 open fraction; alpha7 available fraction, meaning the part not desensitised; internal calcium; Ih activation.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance about 142 megaohms; resting potential about minus 60 millivolts; spike half-width about 0.42 milliseconds; sag about 21.5 percent; fractional calcium current 0.10 to 0.12; peak conductance 1 to 3 nanosiemens.

• TIME CONSTANTS: alpha7 activation under 1 millisecond; desensitisation 5 to 20 milliseconds; recovery 200 to 800 milliseconds; Ih activation 100 to 500 milliseconds; membrane 12 to 20 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: deleting one copy of CHRNA7, as in the 15q13.3 deletion syndrome, is associated with generalised epilepsy, intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorders and schizophrenia, and reduced receptor levels accompany the failure of P50 sensory gating, the inability to suppress the response to a repeated click.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model how a brief puff of acetylcholine opens the alpha7 receptors on this cell, admits calcium, then shuts them within milliseconds even though the acetylcholine is still present.
Create a number variable called open fraction with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called available fraction with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called acetylcholine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium entry with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium step with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 200, repeat the following steps.
    Check if clock is less than 10.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set acetylcholine to 1.
        Calculate the product of available fraction and 0.75.
        Set available fraction to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set acetylcholine to 0.
        Calculate the sum of available fraction and 0.002.
        Set available fraction to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of acetylcholine and available fraction.
    Set open fraction to the result.
    Calculate the product of open fraction and 0.11.
    Set calcium step to the result.
    Calculate the sum of calcium entry and calcium step.
    Set calcium entry to the result.
    Calculate the sum of clock and 1.
    Set clock to the result.
End the loop.
Display calcium entry to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is a two-state kinetic scheme where readability of the state variables matters more than raw speed.

# Alpha7 receptor: opens in under a millisecond, desensitises in a few,
# and carries an unusually large calcium fraction.
DT, TAU_ON, TAU_DESENS, TAU_RECOVER = 0.1, 0.4, 8.0, 400.0   # milliseconds
G_MAX, E_REV, PCA = 2.0, 0.0, 0.11   # nanosiemens, millivolts, calcium share

def puff(acetylcholine, v_hold=-60.0):
    """acetylcholine is a list of 0 or 1 flags, one per 0.1 ms step."""
    open_p, avail, peak, charge = 0.0, 1.0, 0.0, 0.0
    for ach in acetylcholine:
        drive = 1.0 if ach else 0.0
        open_p += DT * (drive * avail - open_p) / TAU_ON
        avail += DT * ((1.0 - avail) / TAU_RECOVER - drive * avail / TAU_DESENS)
        current = G_MAX * open_p * (v_hold - E_REV)   # picoamperes, inward
        peak = min(peak, current)                     # inward is negative
        charge += current * DT                        # femtocoulombs
    return peak, charge, PCA * charge

if __name__ == "__main__":
    peak, charge, ca = puff([1] * 100 + [0] * 900)    # 10 ms puff, 90 ms after
    print("peak %.1f pA, charge %.0f fC, calcium %.0f fC" % (peak, charge, ca))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The biggest cell body in layer one, resting high at minus 60 millivolts, with the largest sag of any layer one neuron, a calcium hump under threshold, no NDNF marker, an axon dropping to layer five A, and a nicotinic receptor that slams shut milliseconds after it opens.

CHAPTER 33. LAMP5-LHX6 CELLS (Primate-Enriched Diffuse-Inhibition Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that proves your cortex is not simply a large mouse cortex, because it carries a molecular passport stamped in the wrong birthplace.

The old label is a pair of gene symbols (LAMP5 and LHX6), which carries no functional meaning for a reader.

Primate-Enriched records its remarkable evolutionary expansion: while confined to deeper layers in rodents, in primates - including humans, marmosets, and macaques - it represents nearly 4% of all cortical inhibitory neurons and spans across both upper and lower layers.

Diffuse-Inhibition names its physiological mode: its gene profile matches the neurogliaform family, whose members release a slow cloud of gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) into the surrounding tissue rather than targeting a single synaptic partner.

Neuron marks it as a key evolutionary interneuron class, recording its verified abundance and inhibitory style while functional recordings continue to map its precise circuit role.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a small inhibitory nerve cell of the cerebral cortex that quietens its neighbours rather than exciting them.

Its name comes from two genes it switches on together: LAMP5, which stands for lysosomal associated membrane protein family member 5, and LHX6, which stands for LIM homeobox 6 and is a master control gene.

That pairing is the puzzle, because LAMP5 usually marks cells born in a nursery at the base of the embryonic brain called the caudal ganglionic eminence, while LHX6 marks cells born in a different nursery next door called the medial ganglionic eminence.

So this is a neurogliaform cell, meaning a cell with a tiny dense ball of axon, built out of parts from the wrong factory.

Its job is slow, wide, blanket inhibition: it does not aim at one partner but bathes a small ball of tissue in the calming chemical gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round, with five to ten short dendrites, the branches that collect incoming signals, radiating outward in every direction like a sea urchin.

The dendrites carry few or no spines, the tiny mushroom-shaped knobs that receive excitatory contacts on other cells.

The axon, the output cable, is the signature: it is astonishingly fine and branches so many times that it forms a dense grey haze around the cell body, which is why the family is called neurogliaform, meaning glia-shaped, since early anatomists mistook the haze for a support cell.

Some deep-layer members of this transcriptomic group have been reported with chandelier-like axon cartridges instead, and that disagreement is not yet settled.

SIZE:

The cell body measures roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across, about one fifth the width of a human hair.

The dendritic field spans about 200 to 300 micrometres.

The axon cloud fills a sphere of roughly 200 to 400 micrometres, yet the total length of axon crammed into that small sphere is measured in millimetres, typically 5 to 20 millimetres in human tissue.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Single-cell gene readouts split the group into several closely related clusters, commonly labelled Lamp5 Lhx6 and its numbered variants.

Anatomically there are at least two flavours: a deep form sitting in layers 5 and 6 and near the white matter border, and an upper-layer form that primates have expanded and mice largely lack.

The unresolved chandelier-like variant, provisionally called CHC2, is a third possible subtype.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It is found across the neocortex, the six-layered outer sheet of the brain, in the frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital, motor, somatosensory and visual areas.

It is enriched in the deep layers, layer 5 and layer 6, and it clusters at the interface where layer 6 meets the white matter, the cable bundle beneath the cortex.

In macaque, human and marmoset it also spreads upward into layers 2 and 3, unlike in mouse.

Related cells occur in the hippocampus and the amygdala, but those lie outside this chapter.

NUMBER:

In macaque cortex this type made up 3.9 percent of interneurons, that is 467 nuclei out of 12,097, and human and marmoset counts were similar.

Applying that fraction to the roughly 3.2 billion inhibitory neurons of the human neocortex gives about 125 million cells, with a plausible range of 90 to 160 million.

That is roughly 0.8 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons counted by Azevedo and colleagues in 2009, and roughly 0.15 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence in the fraction is moderate because it rests on several independent single-cell surveys; confidence in the absolute count is lower because it inherits every uncertainty in the total.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Inject a steady current and the cell does not fire at once; instead the voltage crawls upward for tens to hundreds of milliseconds before the first spike, a behaviour called late spiking.

The delay comes from a potassium current that opens quickly and fades slowly, acting like a handbrake that must wear off.

Input resistance is high, commonly 150 to 400 megaohms, so small currents move the voltage a long way.

Primate neurogliaform cells, unlike rodent ones, often show a sag, a slow rebound when the voltage is pushed downward.

More than 80 percent of neurogliaform-family cells can be driven into persistent firing, continuing to spike for tens of seconds after the stimulus stops.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives excitatory contacts from nearby pyramidal cells, from the thalamus, the brain's central relay station, and from long-range cortical fibres.

It sends its output onto the dendritic shafts and spines of pyramidal cells and onto many other interneurons, including cells of its own kind.

Crucially it also forms gap junctions, direct electrical tunnels between cells, with an unusually wide range of interneuron partners rather than only its own type.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, generally under 0.2 micrometres in diameter, and conducts slowly.

Boutons, the swellings where transmitter is released, occur roughly every 3 to 4 micrometres and are small.

Many boutons face no classical partner membrane at all, which is the anatomical basis of releasing into the space between cells.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is GABA, the brain's main calming chemical.

The cell also carries neuropeptide Y, a slow-acting signalling peptide, and members of the wider family carry neuronal nitric oxide synthase, the enzyme that makes the gas nitric oxide.

It is strongly silenced by mu-opioid receptors, the same receptors that morphine acts on, and it is excited through alpha-7 nicotinic receptors, which respond to the brain's own acetylcholine.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none.

The axon is unmyelinated, meaning it has no fatty insulating sleeve, because insulation would defeat the purpose of releasing transmitter continuously along the whole cable.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Far cheaper to run than a fast-spiking cell, because it fires slowly and lacks the expensive high-speed sodium and potassium machinery.

Against that, it must maintain millimetres of ultrafine membrane, so the housekeeping cost of transporting proteins and mitochondria along the axon is high relative to the cell's small volume.

PLASTICITY:

The slow inhibitory response it produces can be weakened for minutes by prior activity, largely through GABA-B autoreceptors on its own terminals sensing the transmitter it just released.

Opioids, whether the body's own or a drug, can switch its output off almost completely, which is a fast and reversible form of plasticity.

Persistent firing shows the axon itself can hold a memory of recent activity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are not bystanders but part of the computation.

Their GABA transporters, especially GAT-3, set how far the released GABA cloud spreads and how long it lasts, so astrocyte density around this cell effectively tunes the radius of its influence.

Nitric oxide made by neighbouring family members diffuses freely through glia and can widen local blood vessels, coupling this cell type to blood flow in a way most interneurons are not.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the deep cortex loses its slow, wide brake.

Layer 5 and layer 6 pyramidal cells would keep their fast point-to-point inhibition but lose the diffuse hush that follows a burst of activity, so runaway excitation would build more easily and the deep layers would drift toward the rodent pattern.

The distinctive primate expansion of this cell is one of the few concrete cellular differences that separates a human deep cortex from a mouse one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its central trick is volume transmission: GABA is released into the extracellular space and reaches receptors that are not at any synapse.

A single action potential can activate GABA-B receptors, the slow metabotropic kind that normally need a burst, because the transmitter lingers.

The cell is electrically coupled to other interneurons through gap junctions, so a group of them behaves partly as one sheet.

It can fire persistently for tens of seconds, meaning the axon stores state.

Nitric oxide from the family acts as a retrograde messenger and a vasodilator, letting the cell speak to blood vessels and to the terminals that contact it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: excitatory glutamate currents from local layer 5 and layer 6 pyramidal cells, thalamic afferents and long-range cortical fibres, in the range 0 to 300 picoamperes.

• INPUTS: acetylcholine acting on alpha-7 nicotinic receptors, modelled as a 0 to 1 gain on excitability.

• INPUTS: mu-opioid receptor occupancy, 0 to 1, which multiplies output strength by a factor from 1 down to about 0.1.

• OUTPUTS: an extracellular GABA concentration field centred on the cell, peaking near 100 micromolar inside the plexus and falling to under 1 micromolar by 60 micrometres.

• OUTPUTS: slow GABA-A currents with a decay time constant near 50 to 70 milliseconds and GABA-B currents onto pyramidal dendrites and neighbouring interneurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage, availability of the slow potassium brake, GABA-B autoreceptor occupancy, persistent-firing flag, local GABA concentration.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 150 to 400 megaohms, membrane time constant 10 to 25 milliseconds, resting potential near minus 65 to minus 70 millivolts, threshold near minus 42 millivolts, bouton spacing 3 to 4 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: brake decay 50 to 150 milliseconds, GABA-A slow decay 50 to 70 milliseconds, GABA-B response 150 to 500 milliseconds, persistent firing seconds to tens of seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of LAMP5-positive interneurons is seen in Alzheimer's disease, and reducing LAMP5 in mice worsens network hypersynchrony in both amyloid-driven and tau-driven models, so the simulated failure is removal of the slow blanket brake followed by runaway synchronous discharge.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to drive a Lamp5-Lhx6 cell to its delayed first spike by letting a slow potassium brake wear off.
Create a number variable called depolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called brake with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called change with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called elapsed with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
While elapsed is less than 600 remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the product of brake and 26.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the sum of change and depolarisation.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the difference of 30 and change.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of change divided by 150.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the sum of depolarisation and change.
    Set depolarisation to the result.
    Calculate the product of brake and 0.99875.
    Set brake to the result.
    If depolarisation is greater than 26, then perform the following actions.
        Add elapsed to the end of spike times.
        Set depolarisation to 0.
        Set brake to 0.5.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of elapsed and 0.1.
    Set elapsed to the result.
End the loop.
Display spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python was chosen because the delayed-spike logic is short and every number stays readable.
DT = 0.1           # milliseconds per step
TAU_M = 15.0       # membrane time constant, milliseconds
TAU_BRAKE = 60.0   # milliseconds for the slow potassium brake to fade
DRIVE = 30.0       # steady drive, millivolts above rest
THRESH = 26.0      # spike threshold, millivolts above rest
G_BRAKE = 26.0     # brake strength in millivolts when fully available
def run(duration=600.0):
    v = 0.0        # depolarisation above resting potential, millivolts
    brake = 1.0    # availability of the delaying potassium current, 0 to 1
    spikes = []
    for i in range(int(duration / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        # the brake subtracts from the drive, so threshold is out of reach at first
        dv = (DRIVE - v - G_BRAKE * brake) / TAU_M
        v += dv * DT
        brake -= (brake / TAU_BRAKE) * DT
        if v >= THRESH:
            spikes.append(round(t, 1))
            v = 0.0
            brake = min(1.0, brake + 0.25)   # each spike partly re-arms the brake
    return spikes
train = run()
print("first spike at", train[0], "ms")
print("spikes in 600 ms:", len(train))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the cell holding two contradictory birth certificates at once, LAMP5 from one embryonic nursery and LHX6 from the other, and it is about eight times more common in primate cortex than in mouse cortex.

CHAPTER 34. ROSEHIP CELLS (COG)

Under the microscope, its axon terminals bulge like the fat red berries of a wild rose - and nobody has ever found this cell in a mouse.

Rosehip is a shape name that teaches: the compact, bushy axon studded with large, round boutons genuinely looks like a rosehip, providing the exact visual fingerprint used to identify the cell under a microscope.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is an inhibitory nerve cell of the top layer of the human cerebral cortex, described for the first time in 2018 by Boldog and colleagues.

It releases gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the brain's main calming chemical.

Its speciality is precision at a distance: it places its brakes on the thin dendritic shafts of pyramidal cells far above their cell bodies, and by doing so it can veto a spike that is travelling backwards up into the dendritic tree.

That backward-travelling spike is the signal a pyramidal cell uses to tell its own dendrites that it has just fired, which is central to how the cortex decides what to learn.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and sits in layer 1, the thin, nearly cell-free skin at the very surface of the cortex.

It has about five or six main dendrites, on average 5.5, that stay within layer 1 and only occasionally dip into layer 2.

The axon forms a compact, bushy, almost shrub-like tangle directly around the cell, studded with unusually large boutons, the swellings that release transmitter, which give the cell its name.

SIZE:

Total dendritic length averages 1.96 millimetres, plus or minus 0.90.

Total axonal length averages 11.13 millimetres, plus or minus 1.99, packed into a bush measuring about 288 micrometres across and 263 micrometres tall.

Boutons average 0.37 cubic micrometres in volume, roughly four times larger than those of an ordinary neurogliaform cell, and sit about 3.97 micrometres apart along the axon.

The active zone, the small patch where transmitter is actually released, averages 0.11 square micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subtypes have been established.

The cell maps onto a single gene-expression cluster, originally labelled i5, so present evidence suggests one coherent type rather than a family, although human layer 1 as a whole holds at least four interneuron groups and the boundaries between rosehip cells and their neighbours are still being redrawn.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Layer 1 of the human neocortex, documented in temporal and frontal cortex from surgical and postmortem tissue.

It sits at two interfaces at once: the pial surface above, where the brain meets its membranes and cerebrospinal fluid, and the layer 1 to layer 2 border below.

Functionally it also straddles an interface, because its cell body is in layer 1 while its targets are the dendrites of pyramidal cells whose bodies lie down in layer 3.

NUMBER:

Rosehip cells make up 10 to 15 percent of the GABA-releasing neurons of layer 1.

Layer 1 is very sparsely populated, holding on the order of 1 percent of neocortical neurons, which would be about 160 million cells, nearly all of them inhibitory.

That yields roughly 16 to 25 million rosehip cells, and honesty demands a wider stated range of 10 to 35 million.

That is about 0.1 to 0.2 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 0.02 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Confidence is low, because the layer 1 fraction is an estimate rather than a direct count; the 10 to 15 percent figure itself is well measured.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting membrane potential averages minus 61.34 millivolts, plus or minus 5.8.

Input resistance averages 139.6 megaohms, plus or minus 54.1, and the membrane time constant averages 7.3 milliseconds, plus or minus 3.7.

The cell shows a sag of 1.73 and a resonance peak at 4.17 hertz, meaning it responds best to inputs arriving at about four times a second, in the theta rhythm band.

Its firing is irregular and stuttering: the variation in the gap between spikes averages 87 milliseconds, plus or minus 64.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Other layer 1 interneurons connect onto rosehip cells in about 45 percent of tested pairs, producing inhibitory potentials of 0.9 to 1.5 millivolts that get weaker with repetition.

Rosehip cells connect onto layer 3 pyramidal cells in about 46 percent of tested pairs.

Every one of 31 sampled boutons contacted a dendritic shaft, not a spine and not a cell body, which is an unusually pure targeting rule.

Those contacts sit 290 micrometres from the pyramidal cell body on average, with a range of 94 to 455 micrometres.

Rosehip cells also form gap junctions, direct electrical tunnels, with other rosehip cells in about 57 percent of pairs, with a coupling coefficient near 0.05.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, unmyelinated, and heavily branched, with 1.52 branch points per 100 micrometres.

The distinguishing feature is the bouton, four times the usual volume, which allows a small number of contacts to deliver a reliable brake.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, made by the enzyme glutamate decarboxylase 1, abbreviated GAD1.

The cell also carries cholecystokinin, a signalling peptide, and it lacks the cannabinoid receptor CNR1, somatostatin, calretinin and parvalbumin, a negative fingerprint that distinguishes it from every other known cortical interneuron.

Its gene signature includes TRPC3, SV2C, CPLX3, LAMP5, EYA4, PRSS12, NTNG1 and SSTR2.

MYELINATION:

None.

The axon is bare, which suits a cell whose entire output is delivered within a few hundred micrometres of its own cell body.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest.

Firing is slow and irregular, so the sodium and potassium pumping bill is small, but the large boutons hold big pools of vesicles that must be refilled, and 11 millimetres of axon must be supplied with mitochondria.

PLASTICITY:

Inputs onto the cell depress with repeated use, with paired-pulse ratios of 0.27 to 0.71, meaning the second of two closely spaced inputs is much weaker.

Because it controls backpropagating spikes, it sits upstream of learning itself: by suppressing dendritic calcium signals, from 18.8 percent to 12.8 percent change in fluorescence in the original measurements, it gates whether nearby synapses strengthen.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Layer 1 is dense in astrocyte processes and in the endfeet that wrap the surface blood vessels, and it lies directly beneath the glia limitans, the astrocyte wall that seals the brain from its coverings.

Astrocytic GABA transporters here must clear transmitter from a compartment with unusually little neuronal membrane, so the same amount of released GABA travels further than it would in a crowded deep layer.

Nothing uniquely specialised has been reported about the microglia around this cell.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove rosehip cells and layer 1 keeps its incoming long-range fibres but loses its finest brake on the apical dendrites below.

Pyramidal cells in layer 3 would still be inhibited near their cell bodies, but the microdomain veto that stops a single stretch of dendrite from confirming a spike would vanish.

The cortex would become blunter: less able to say no to one branch while saying yes to another.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The rosehip cell is a dendritic computation device that happens to be a separate neuron.

Its inhibition is placed in microdomains, small patches of the dendritic tuft, so it edits the dendrite's local arithmetic rather than the cell's overall firing rate.

It is electrically coupled to its own kind by gap junctions, so a small network of rosehip cells can act together.

Its large boutons and slow rising inhibitory potential, 7.3 milliseconds to rise, plus or minus 2.4, mean it works by lingering rather than by striking fast.

It is a genuine case of human cortical specialisation, with only weak transcriptomic similarity, a correlation of 0.34, to the nearest mouse candidate.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: inhibitory potentials from other layer 1 interneurons, 0.9 to 1.5 millivolts, depressing with paired-pulse ratios of 0.27 to 0.71.

• INPUTS: excitatory drive from long-range and thalamic fibres arriving in layer 1, plus gap junction current from neighbouring rosehip cells with a coupling coefficient near 0.05.

• OUTPUTS: inhibitory potentials of about 0.087 millivolts, plus or minus 0.059, delivered exclusively onto dendritic shafts of layer 3 pyramidal cells at 94 to 455 micrometres from the cell body.

• STATE: membrane voltage, sag current activation, inhibitory conductance rise and decay variables, gap junction voltage of coupled partners.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 61.34 millivolts, input resistance 139.6 megaohms, membrane time constant 7.3 milliseconds, resonance peak 4.17 hertz, sag ratio 1.73, connection probability to layer 3 pyramidal cells 0.46.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibitory rise 7.3 milliseconds, slow GABA-A decay of order 50 milliseconds, interspike interval variability 87 milliseconds, resonance period about 240 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: because the cell vetoes backpropagating spikes in the dendritic tuft, its loss predicts unchecked dendritic calcium entry, indiscriminate strengthening of synapses, and increased seizure risk in the superficial cortex; direct human disease evidence is still lacking and the simulation should flag this as an inference, not a measurement.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to place a rosehip cell's slow shunt on one dendritic shaft and measure how much of a backpropagating spike survives.
Create a number variable called shunt with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called elapsed with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called surviving with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called record with initial value empty list.
While elapsed is less than 300 remains true, repeat the following steps.
    If elapsed is equal to 90, then perform the following actions.
        Set shunt to 0.7.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of shunt and 0.9802.
    Set shunt to the result.
    Calculate the sum of shunt and 1.
    Set surviving to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of 40 divided by surviving.
    Set surviving to the result.
    If elapsed is greater than or equal to 100, then perform the following actions.
        If elapsed is less than or equal to 105, then perform the following actions.
            Add surviving to the end of record.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of elapsed and 1.
    Set elapsed to the result.
End the loop.
Display record to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C was chosen because a whole-cortex simulator runs millions of these compartments and C keeps the inner loop tiny. */
/* Build with: cc rosehip.c -lm */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define DT      0.05   /* milliseconds per step */
#define EL    -70.0    /* leak reversal, millivolts */
#define EGABA -75.0    /* chloride reversal at the shaft, millivolts */
#define GL      0.05   /* leak conductance, microsiemens */
#define CM      0.10   /* compartment capacitance, nanofarads */
#define TAUR    7.3    /* rosehip inhibitory rise, milliseconds */
#define TAUD   50.0    /* slow GABA-A decay, milliseconds */
#define GMAX    0.06   /* peak rosehip conductance, microsiemens */
static double bap(double t) {
    double s = t - 100.0;          /* backpropagating spike arrives at 100 ms */
    if (s < 0.0) return 0.0;
    return 40.0 * exp(-s / 2.0);   /* millivolt drive, brief and large */
}
int main(void) {
    double v = EL, r = 0.0, d = 0.0, peak = EL;
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < 6000; i++) {
        double t = i * DT;
        if (fabs(t - 90.0) < DT / 2.0) { r = 1.0; d = 1.0; }  /* rosehip fires first */
        r -= (r / TAUR) * DT;
        d -= (d / TAUD) * DT;
        double g = GMAX * (d - r);   /* slow rise, much slower decay */
        v += DT * (bap(t) * GL - g * (v - EGABA) - GL * (v - EL)) / CM;
        if (v > peak) peak = v;
    }
    printf("peak tuft depolarisation %.2f mV (rest %.1f mV)\n", peak, EL);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Boutons four times the normal size, arranged in a compact bush in layer 1, contacting dendritic shafts and nothing else, on a cell that is GAD1 and cholecystokinin positive but negative for CNR1, somatostatin, calretinin and parvalbumin, with no known counterpart in any rodent.

CHAPTER 35. BITUFTED CELLS (Repeat-Firing Feedback Damping Neuron) (COG)

It looks like a tiny tree with two crowns - one reaching up toward the surface and one reaching down toward the white matter - and that shape lets one small cell listen to every layer of the cortex at once.

Bitufted uses a Latin prefix to describe two dendritic tufts, which is an anatomical footnote that hides an unusually well-understood job.

Repeat-Firing names the condition under which the cell wakes up: the excitatory synapses made onto it grow stronger with each successive spike, so a single isolated spike barely moves it while a run of spikes recruits it powerfully.

Feedback names the loop: the cell is driven by the very same pyramidal cells it projects back onto, often forming a reciprocal pair.

Damping names the consequence: a rising dendritic inhibition that acts as a brake when local excitation becomes repetitive, keeping sustained activity from running away.

Neuron marks it as a key feedback-inhibitory cortical interneuron.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is an inhibitory nerve cell named for its shape rather than its chemistry: two tufts of dendrites, the input branches, leaving opposite poles of the cell body and running vertically.

Most bitufted cells release gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the brain's main calming chemical, and most also carry somatostatin, a signalling peptide.

Their job is to act as a learned, frequency-sensitive brake on the dendrites of excitatory pyramidal cells: the harder the pyramidal cells work, the stronger the bitufted cell's input becomes, so it switches on late in a burst and clamps the dendrites down.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is oval and vertically elongated.

Two dendritic tufts leave the top and bottom poles and extend vertically, often spanning most of the cortical depth, so the cell samples input from layer 1 down to layer 6.

The axon usually descends and ascends within a narrow vertical column, contacting dendrites rather than cell bodies.

SIZE:

Cell body about 10 to 20 micrometres across.

The dendritic span is the striking part: several hundred micrometres to more than a millimetre vertically, in a horizontal footprint often narrower than 150 micrometres.

Total axon length is typically a few millimetres, distributed along a vertical column.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Because bitufted is a shape and not a gene, the class cuts across molecular families.

The largest group is somatostatin positive, overlapping with Martinotti cells, which send an axon up into layer 1.

A second group is calretinin or vasoactive intestinal peptide positive, generally smaller, and often specialised to inhibit other interneurons rather than pyramidal cells, which disinhibits the network.

A third group carries neuropeptide Y and sometimes cholecystokinin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Predominantly layers 2 and 3 and layer 4 of the neocortex, with a further population in layer 5 and layer 6, across frontal, motor, somatosensory, visual, parietal and temporal areas.

The dendritic tufts cross every laminar interface in the cortex, and the upward tuft characteristically reaches the layer 1 to layer 2 border where long-range and thalamic fibres arrive.

NUMBER:

Bitufted morphology accounts for a substantial slice of cortical interneurons, and taking a conservative 8 to 12 percent of the roughly 3.2 billion neocortical inhibitory neurons gives about 250 to 400 million cells, with a working value near 320 million.

That is roughly 1.5 to 2.5 percent of the 16.34 billion neocortical neurons and about 0.37 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

Confidence is moderate for the order of magnitude and low for the precise value, because the class is defined by shape and different laboratories draw its boundary in different places.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Typically regular spiking or low-threshold spiking, meaning the cell fires readily from a hyperpolarised state, with clear adaptation, so the spike train slows during a maintained input.

Spikes are broader than those of fast-spiking cells, input resistance is moderate to high, and the cell often shows a burst at the start of a depolarisation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The signature property, shown by Reyes and colleagues in 1998, is target-cell-specific facilitation: excitatory contacts from a layer 2 or 3 pyramidal cell onto a bitufted cell grow stronger with each successive presynaptic spike, while contacts from the same pyramidal cell onto a multipolar interneuron grow weaker.

The consequence is that bitufted cells are recruited by sustained, high-frequency pyramidal firing and stay quiet during sparse activity.

Their output goes onto pyramidal dendrites, and in the calretinin and vasoactive intestinal peptide subgroup onto other interneurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin and mostly unmyelinated, running vertically, with boutons distributed along dendritic targets rather than concentrated on cell bodies.

Conduction is slow, in the range of a fraction of a metre per second, which suits a cell that acts on the timescale of hundreds of milliseconds rather than microseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the fast transmitter.

Somatostatin is the commonest co-transmitter, and subsets carry neuropeptide Y, calbindin, calretinin, vasoactive intestinal peptide, cholecystokinin or nitric oxide synthase.

Uniquely among cortical interneurons, this cell can also release GABA from its dendrites, acting backwards onto the terminals that excite it.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none on the fine collaterals, though sparse myelin has been reported on proximal segments of some cortical interneuron axons.

The functional consequence is slow conduction, which is acceptable because the cell's role is integrative rather than time-critical.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate.

Firing rates are lower than in fast-spiking cells, but the enormous vertical dendritic tree is expensive to maintain, and peptide transmitters such as somatostatin must be manufactured in the cell body and shipped the full length of the axon, which no small-molecule transmitter requires.

PLASTICITY:

Its defining plasticity is short term and presynaptic: facilitation with a time constant of roughly 100 to 300 milliseconds makes the cell a detector of sustained activity.

Its second form of plasticity is retrograde: bursts of backpropagating spikes raise calcium in the bitufted cell's dendrite, which triggers GABA release from that dendrite, which activates GABA-B receptors on the incoming pyramidal terminal and depresses the very input that drove it.

Somatostatin cells are also central to long-term changes in dendritic excitability during learning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around the vertical dendrites must clear glutamate along an unusually long, thin structure, and their transporters help keep the dendritic calcium signal local.

Because dendritic GABA release happens outside classical synapses, astrocytic GABA transporters set how far that retrograde signal travels.

Otherwise the glial environment is not reported to be special.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove bitufted cells and the cortex loses its use-dependent dendritic brake.

Brief, sparse activity would look normal, but sustained bursts would no longer recruit inhibition onto the apical dendrites, so pyramidal cells would keep amplifying their own input without limit.

Concretely, the region would lose the ability to stop a strong signal from spreading into every neighbouring column, and the crisp boundary between an attended feature and its surround would blur.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is a textbook case of computation happening outside the cell body.

Backpropagating action potentials invade its dendrites and raise calcium there.

That calcium triggers exocytotic release of GABA from the dendrite itself, which acts as a retrograde messenger on GABA-B receptors on the presynaptic pyramidal terminal, depressing the excitatory input, as Zilberter and colleagues showed in 2000.

Somatostatin is released by volume transmission and acts slowly over a wide area.

The vertical dendritic tree performs its own integration across layers before the cell body ever sees a summed input.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: facilitating glutamate synapses from local pyramidal cells, initial release probability near 0.09, facilitation increment near 0.15 per spike.

• INPUTS: long-range and thalamic excitation onto the upper dendritic tuft in layer 1, and cholinergic and serotonergic modulation setting a gain of roughly 0.5 to 2.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto the apical and basal dendrites of pyramidal cells, and in the calretinin and vasoactive intestinal peptide subgroup onto other interneurons.

• OUTPUTS: somatostatin by volume transmission, and dendritic GABA acting retrogradely on presynaptic GABA-B receptors.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation current, synaptic facilitation variable, dendritic calcium concentration, retrograde depression level, somatostatin store.

• PARAMETERS: baseline release probability 0.09, facilitation increment 0.15, spike threshold about 15 millivolts above rest, adaptation strength moderate, vertical dendritic span 400 to 1200 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: facilitation decay 100 to 300 milliseconds, excitatory potential decay 5 to 15 milliseconds, dendritic calcium decay about 100 to 200 milliseconds, retrograde GABA-B depression 300 to 600 milliseconds, somatostatin action seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: somatostatin messenger ribonucleic acid falls by about 38 percent in women and about 27 percent in men with major depressive disorder, with protein down about 19 percent in both, and reduced somatostatin interneuron function is linked to depression, anxiety and impaired dendritic gating; in simulation this appears as loss of the late, use-dependent dendritic brake.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run a burst of pyramidal spikes into a bitufted cell so that each input grows stronger, until the cell's own dendrite releases gamma-aminobutyric acid backwards and shuts that input down.
Create a number variable called release probability with initial value 0.09.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called working with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called amplitudes with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 12 times.
    Calculate the product of release probability and gain.
    Set working to the result.
    Calculate the product of working and 6.
    Set working to the result.
    Add working to the end of amplitudes.
    If working is greater than 3, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of calcium and 1.
        Set calcium to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    If calcium is greater than 2, then perform the following actions.
        Set gain to 0.2.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and release probability.
    Set working to the result.
    Calculate the product of working and 0.15.
    Set working to the result.
    Calculate the sum of release probability and working.
    Set release probability to the result.
End the loop.
Display amplitudes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript was chosen because this is a short state machine that runs in any browser or in Node with no setup.
const DT = 0.1;           // milliseconds per step
const U0 = 0.09;          // baseline release probability, pyramidal to bitufted
const DF = 0.15;          // facilitation added by each presynaptic spike
const TAU_F = 200.0;      // milliseconds for facilitation to fade
const TAU_E = 8.0;        // milliseconds, decay of the excitatory potential
const TAU_CA = 120.0;     // milliseconds, decay of dendritic calcium
const TAU_RETRO = 400.0;  // milliseconds, decay of the retrograde GABA-B brake
const VTH = 3.0;          // millivolts, spike threshold of the bitufted cell
